17The stress tensor and Ward identities

17.1The trace calibration on the selected construction

The trace asymptotic uses a one-dimensional conserved grade-four line and a calibrated limiting sequence of such tensors. Here we prove the finite- dimensional rank argument conditional on the interacting-center and scale-dependent comparison receipts stated below. The free reference rank is proved in Subsection 16.1. Detailed boundary-measure and all-radii arguments remain in Sections 18.18 and 18.20.

17.1.1The conserved tensor space has dimension one

Let \(K_0\) be the real finite-dimensional space of reference grade-four stress tensors and \(V_0\) its divergence space. Let

\(\displaystyle D_0 : K_0 \longrightarrow V_0\)

be the reference divergence. Let \(K\) be the interacting spin-kernel space, \(D : K \to V\) its fixed divergence, and, at scale \(i\),

\(\displaystyle O_i : K \longrightarrow V_0\)

the comparison map into the reference two-component table. Only \(O_i\) depends on scale. The interacting-center dimension equivalence and the scale-dependent comparison are supplied by the inputs stated below; the elementary rank conclusion is proved conditional on them. The free reference statement is not assumed: it is proved in Subsection 16.1. Concretely, \(K_0\) is the subspace of reference symmetric grade-four tensors with zero scalar skim, and \(V_0\) is the reference grade-five vector table. The interacting \(K\) is the centered grade-four kernel space, and \(D\) is the divergence rank map restricted to it. The optical map takes values in the corresponding reference vector table.

  1. \(D_0\) is surjective and \(\dim \ker D_0 = 1\);

  2. \(\dim K = \dim K_0\);

  3. eventually \(\ker O_i = \ker D\);

  4. eventually there is a linear map \(S_i : \mathbb{R}^2 \to K\) such that \(O_i S_i : \mathbb{R}^2 \to V_0\) is bijective.

Item 2 is proved below from the coherent grade-four equivalence and centering identities. On the selected table, these are supplied by the eventual coherent, unit-preserving isomorphisms of Theorem 17.5 and their completion in the completion paragraph. Centering gives the explicit inverse below, without requiring the maps to preserve expectations. This coherent-center input is therefore available for that selected family; it is not inferred from the free reference rank.

Lemma 17.1. (Rank-one conservation law) Under these four properties, \(\dim \ker D = 1\).

Proof. Choose \(i\) large enough that properties 3–4 hold. Since \(O_i S_i\) is surjective, \(O_i\) is surjective. Rank–nullity and the kernel identity give

\(\displaystyle \dim \ker D = \dim \ker O_i = \dim K - \dim V_0 .\)

The reference surjection \(D_0\) gives

\(\displaystyle \dim K_0 = \dim V_0 + \dim \ker D_0 = \dim V_0 + 1.\)

Property 2 identifies \(\dim K = \dim K_0\), so subtraction yields \(\dim \ker D = 1\).\(\Box\)

The free reference computation is proved in Subsection 16.1. In the notation there, the scalar skim is the real one-point average of the \(00\) tensor entry, the free symmetric grade-four table has dimension four, and the Maxwell-reduced grade-five vector table has dimension two. The zero-skim divergence map is onto and its kernel is the Weyl line. In particular, this is a proved finite-dimensional input to item 1, not a free-rank hypothesis. The map \(T\) from the four coefficient parameters is identified with that chapter's free stress coefficient map. Its point-probe identities and the low-grade signed-covariance classification are proved there.

To prove item 2 under the coherent-center input, let \(S\) be the reference table and \(T\) the interacting table. Assume that a coherent field equivalence \(U : S.Field_4 \to T.Field_4\) preserves conjugation and signed action and maps the reference unit to the interacting unit. For a stress array \(B\), define entrywise

\(\displaystyle (\textsf{U} B)_{ab} = U (B_{ab}), \qquad (\textsf{C}_T B)_{ab} = B_{ab} -\mathbb{E}_T (B_{ab}) \hspace{0.17em} 1_T .\)

Coherence makes \(\textsf{U}\) preserve symmetry, reality, and signed covariance. Centering then maps these arrays into the interacting centered kernel space. Thus

\(\displaystyle \textsf{S} (B) = \textsf{C}_T (\textsf{U} B)\)

is a linear map from the reference centered kernel to the interacting one. Define the reverse map \(\textsf{R} (B) = \textsf{C}_S (\textsf{U}^{- 1} B)\). For a centered reference array \(B\), write \(\lambda_{ab} =\mathbb{E}_T (U (B_{ab}))\). Since \(U (1_S) = 1_T\),

\(\displaystyle \textsf{U}^{- 1} (\textsf{C}_T (\textsf{U} B))_{ab} = B_{ab} - \lambda_{ab} 1_S .\)

The reference entry is centered, so \(\mathbb{E}_S (B_{ab}) = 0\); also \(\mathbb{E}_S (1_S) = 1\). Applying \(\textsf{C}_S\) gives \(B_{ab} - \lambda_{ab} 1_S - (- \lambda_{ab}) 1_S = B_{ab}\). This proves \(\textsf{RS} = I\). The same calculation with \(S, T\) reversed proves \(\textsf{SR} = I\). Hence the maps are inverse linear equivalences, which establishes item 2 conditional on the stated coherence, unit, and centering identities.

For property 3, assume the eventual optical-comparison input: on one tail of scales, uniformly for all tensors \(T\), the grade-five formula is

\(\displaystyle (O_i T) (a) = b_i \hspace{0.17em} U_{i, 5}^{- 1} ((DT) (a)), \qquad b_i > 0,\)

where \(U_{i, 5}\) is an isomorphism and \(b_i\) is the positive scalar normalization. The optical map is this scalar times \(U_{i, 5}^{- 1}\) composed with the divergence rank map. Therefore \(O_i T = 0\) exactly when \(DT = 0\). Positivity of \(b_i\) and invertibility of \(U_{i, 5}\) are used on this common tail. No kernel identity is asserted at earlier scales. Property 4, by contrast, also requires the scale-dependent two-direction limits and stable inversion established below; the free reference calculation does not prove it.

In particular, for all sufficiently large \(i\) and every tensor \(T\),

\(\displaystyle O_i T = 0 \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad DT = 0. \) (17.1)

Property 4 follows once the correction maps \(S_i\) have the stated columns \(u_i^0, u_i^1\) and \(\mathcal{B}_i = O_i \circ S_i\) converges to an isomorphism. The stable-inversion argument is written next; the scale- dependent convergence estimates are supplied in Proposition 17.7.

17.1.2Solving the two-direction correction

At scale \(i\), let \(u_i^0, u_i^1 \in K\) be the two correction tensors and define \(S_i : \mathbb{R}^2 \to K\) by \(S_i (e_0) = u_i^0\) and \(S_i (e_1) = u_i^1\). Set \(\mathcal{B}_i = O_i \circ S_i : \mathbb{R}^2 \to V_0\). Let \(T_i^0 \in K\) be the uncorrected scaled tensor and \(X_i = O_i (T_i^0) \in V_0\). Thus exact cancellation \(\mathcal{B}_i c_i + X_i = 0\) is precisely the optical conservation condition \(O_i (T_i^0 + S_i c_i) = 0\). The two-direction comparison input asserts that \(\mathcal{B}_i\) converges in operator norm to an isomorphism \(\mathcal{B}_{\infty}\). The following elementary lemma isolates the only finite-dimensional inverse argument.

Lemma 17.2. (Stable inversion in two directions) Let \(E, F\) be real normed spaces of dimension two. Suppose linear maps \(\mathcal{B}_i : E \to F\) converge in operator norm to a bijection \(\mathcal{B}_{\infty}\). Then there are \(i_0\) and \(C_0 < \infty\) such that for \(i \geqslant i_0\), \(\mathcal{B}_i\) is bijective and

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{B}_i^{- 1} y\| \leqslant C_0 \|y\| \qquad (y \in F) .\)

If \(\|X_i \| \leqslant C_1 A_i\) with \(A_i \geqslant 0\), the unique solution \(c_i = -\mathcal{B}_i^{- 1} X_i\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B}_i c_i + X_i = 0, \qquad \|c_i \| \leqslant C_0 C_1 A_i .\)

Proof. The unit sphere of \(E\) is compact. Since \(\mathcal{B}_{\infty}\) is injective, the continuous function \(u \mapsto \|\mathcal{B}_{\infty} u\|\) has a positive minimum \(m\) on that sphere. For large \(i\), \(\|\mathcal{B}_i -\mathcal{B}_{\infty} \| < m / 2\), and hence \(\|\mathcal{B}_i u\| \geqslant (m / 2) \|u\|\) for every \(u \in E\). Thus \(\mathcal{B}_i\) is injective, and equality of the finite dimensions makes it surjective. The same lower bound gives \(\|\mathcal{B}_i^{- 1} y\| \leqslant (2 / m) \|y\|\). Taking \(C_0 = 2 / m\) proves the claims, including the equation and bound for \(c_i\).\(\Box\)

The receiver argument is the already integrated Lemma 18.55 in Subsection 18.23; it is not duplicated here.

We recall how the receiver lemma is applied; the symbols in this paragraph (\(H\), \(x\), \(\rho\), \(w_i\), \(u_i\), \(U\)) are those of Lemma 18.55, whose space \(E\) we write \(\mathcal{E}\) to avoid a clash with the two-dimensional space \(E\) of the stable-inversion lemma. These symbols are not used elsewhere in this section. In the construction, \(\mathcal{E}\) is the real vector space of grade-five coefficient arrays satisfying reality and signed covariance, not the full unconstrained label space. The space \(H\) is the reference vector table. Its canonical field map is the quotient projection applied to the grade-five coefficient array; its kernel is the line generated by the nonzero equation-of-motion array \(x\). The scalar functional \(\rho\) is the free divergence bound after that same quotient projection. The exceptional vector is the medium

\(\displaystyle w_i = \beta_i^{- 1} \sigma_i^X - \sigma_i^L,\)

where \(\sigma_i^X\) is the transported chosen response, \(\sigma_i^L\) is the transported reference response at the same scale, and \(\beta_i\) is the positive scale parameter. The receiver application requires the following estimates: the maps \(v_i, u_i\) and their limits \(f, U\), the medium \(w_i\), and the weights \(e_i\) satisfy all the hypotheses of Lemma 18.55; in particular \(w_i \to x\), \(e_i v_i (w_i) \to 0\), \(e_i \rho (w_i) \to 0\), and \(v_i u_i = \text{id}_H\) eventually. The finite chart supplies the right inverse and the limiting chart supplies the section only under these input assumptions. The lemma shows how these receipts produce a uniformly bounded receiver with small weighted error. Lemma 18.56 derives the three medium limits from its explicit response and decay hypotheses; their selected-data application is verified in Proposition 17.7 below.

In the trace construction, \(E =\mathbb{R}^2\) labels the two correction sectors, \(F = V_0\) is the reference table space, and \(A_i\) is the reciprocal scale bound. The limiting matrix is invertible because the two reference directions are linearly independent. Its convergence is pointwise on a finite basis, hence in operator norm. The uncorrected divergence has norm at most \(C_1 A_i\). Solving the system therefore produces coefficients with the same \(A_i\) bound and exact cancellation. Applying the flux functional to this identity gives the coefficient normalization. The precise weighted remainder estimates and the coefficient bound below show that the false-sector term vanishes, while the true-sector term is \(- 4 c_i^1 + o (1)\). Since the uncorrected term tends to \(- \kappa\), the true-sector coefficient tends to \(- \kappa / 4\). In the selected construction \(\kappa = s_G = 2 b_0 (G)\) is the slope constant of (12.86). Conditional on these receipts, the limiting physical tensor is fixed by its Ward flux, not by an arbitrary rescaling.

The coefficient argument uses the following correction and flux inputs on the selected construction. The uncorrected size bound, \(A_i \leqslant a_i\), and the three flux limits are supplied in Proposition 17.7; the bounds on the solved coefficients follow from stable inversion. For the two correction directions \(u_i^0, u_i^1\), a linear flux functional \(\ell_i\), correction size \(A_i\), and positive weight \(a_i\):

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} & \|X_i \| \leqslant C_1 A_i, \qquad \|c_i \| \leqslant C_0 C_1 A_i, \qquad A_i \leqslant a_i, \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.2)} \\ & \ell_i (X_i) \longrightarrow - \kappa, \qquad a_i \ell_i (O_i u_i^0) \longrightarrow 0, \qquad a_i (\ell_i (O_i u_i^1) + 4) \longrightarrow 0, \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.3)} \\ & \|c_i \| \leqslant C_3 a_i \quad \text{eventually} . \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.4)} \end{array}\)

Here \(\ell_i\) is a linear functional on \(V_0\), and \(\ell_i (u_i^a)\) is shorthand for \(\ell_i (O_i u_i^a)\). The exact cancellation identity \(\mathcal{B}_i c_i + X_i = 0\) reads, in \(V_0\),

\(\displaystyle X_i + c_i^0 \hspace{0.17em} O_i u_i^0 + c_i^1 \hspace{0.17em} O_i u_i^1 = 0.\)

Since \(A_i \leqslant a_i\), the second bound in (17.2) already gives (17.4) with \(C_3 = C_0 C_1\). By (17.4) and the weighted limits, \(c_i^0 \ell_i (u_i^0) \to 0\) and \(c_i^1 (\ell_i (u_i^1) + 4) \to 0\). Hence \(c_i^1 \ell_i (u_i^1) = - 4 c_i^1 + o (1)\). Apply \(\ell_i\) to the exact cancellation identity. Since \(\ell_i (X_i) \to - \kappa\), it gives \(- \kappa - 4 c_i^1 + o (1) = 0\), so \(c_i^1 \to - \kappa / 4\). This proves coefficient extraction from the correction and flux inputs; it does not construct those inputs. It is the finite-dimensional calibration of the corrected stress sequence.

For clarity, the two limits used after solving the correction system follow from the following explicit scale-dependent receipts. Let \(B_i > 0\), let \(L_i, \Omega_i, \Delta_i\) be tensors, and define \(\mathcal{Y}_i (T) = B_i^{- 1} \mathcal{P}_i (T)\) for a linear scaled tensor map \(\mathcal{P}_i\). Put \(U_{L, i} =\mathcal{P}_i (L_i)\), \(U_{\Omega, i} =\mathcal{P}_i (\Omega_i)\), and \(U_{\Delta, i} =\mathcal{P}_i (\Delta_i)\). Assume

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlllllll} U_{L, i} & \longrightarrow R_0, & U_{\Omega, i} & \longrightarrow R_{\Omega}, & U_{\Delta, i} & \longrightarrow R_{\Delta}, & \frac{RQ_i}{B_i} & \longrightarrow 0, \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.5)} \end{array}\)

and \(T_i = B_i L_i + c_i^0 \Omega_i + c_i^1 \Delta_i\) with \(|c_i^0 | + |c_i^1 | \leqslant CRQ_i\). For the trace limit, assume in addition

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlllllllll} B_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} U_{L, i} & \longrightarrow 0, & h_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} U_{\Omega, i} & \longrightarrow 0, & |c_i^0 | & \leqslant C' h_i, & \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} U_{\Delta, i} & \longrightarrow 4 O, & c_i^1 & \longrightarrow - \kappa / 4. \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.6)} \end{array}\)

Here \(h_i \geqslant 0\) is the weight in the false-sector trace estimate. These hypotheses keep the inverse-normalization scale \(B_i\) distinct from that weight; no equality between these quantities is assumed. Here \(O\) denotes the reference trace field, so the limit \(4 O\) is field-valued.

Lemma 17.3. (Normalized and trace limits from the correction receipts) Under (17.5) and (17.6),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{Y}_i (T_i) \longrightarrow R_0, \qquad B_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{Y}_i (T_i) \longrightarrow - \kappa O.\)

Proof. By linearity and the definition of \(\mathcal{Y}_i\),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{Y}_i (T_i) = U_{L, i} + \frac{c_i^0}{B_i} U_{\Omega, i} + \frac{c_i^1}{B_i} U_{\Delta, i} .\)

The coefficient bound and \(RQ_i / B_i \to 0\) imply \(c_i^a / B_i \to 0\) for \(a = 0, 1\). Each \(U\)-sequence converges and is therefore bounded. Both correction terms vanish, proving the first limit. Taking traces before normalization gives

\(\displaystyle B_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{Y}_i (T_i) = B_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} U_{L, i} + c_i^0 \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} U_{\Omega, i} + c_i^1 \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} U_{\Delta, i} .\)

The first term tends to zero. The second is bounded by \(C' h_i | \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} U_{\Omega, i} |\), which tends to zero. The last tends to \((- \kappa / 4) (4 O) = - \kappa O\).\(\Box\)

In the selected application, \(\mathcal{P}_i\) is the scaled comparison map, \(B_i\) is the trace amplitude, and \(\mathcal{Y}_i\) is the normalized year map. Applying the lemma requires the beam and washout input: the base and correction beam limits, washout ratio, base trace vanishing, false-sector weighted trace vanishing, and true-sector trace limit in (17.5) and (17.6), on these same tensors and maps. Their same-data construction is installed in Proposition 17.7. The coefficient limit in the latter display is the conditional consequence proved above. The lemma proves the normalized tensor and trace conclusions once these estimates are installed; it does not identify the two weights or infer one from the other.

17.1.3The calibrated stress sequence

For this application retain the single comparison curve whose existence is supplied by Proposition 17.7, with the requirements below. The index \(i\) runs along that curve, with positive radii \(r_i\) strictly decreasing to zero. For these supplied admissible choices, form the corrected maps \(C_{i, d}\) of Theorem 17.5 and their early-index completion \(U_{i, d}\) described below. The theorem applies to every admissible choice, so it applies to this same retained curve. For each fixed grade \(d\), \(U_{i, d} = C_{i, d}\) for all sufficiently large \(i\); equality at every index is not required. The projected maps before correction are denoted \(U^{\text{raw}}_{i, d}\), never \(U_{i, d}\). Write \(U_i = U_{i, 4}\) for the grade-four chart and, for a tensor \(T\), set

\(\displaystyle Y_i (T) = r_i^4 U_i^{- 1} T.\)
Joint-curve input for calibrated stress.
Fix the physical and reference tables, their derivative maps, the physical conserved centered tensor \(R\), reference stress \(R_0\), reference trace field \(O\) and Ward normalization \(\kappa\). Fix also the extraction and its ultrafilter. Assume that there exist positive radii \(r_i\) strictly decreasing to zero, admissible matching charts and finite-volume limits satisfying the validity hypotheses of Theorem 17.5, such that the following additional receipts hold after forming and completing their corrected maps as above. On this same curve, require the eventual optical-comparison input and the coherent-center input used in the rank argument, the operator-norm convergence of \(\mathcal{B}_i\) to an isomorphism, and the bound \(\|X_i \| \leqslant C_1 A_i\). Require the correction cancellation and estimates (17.2)– (17.6), with \(\mathcal{P}_i = B_i Y_i\). Thus the two-direction tensors use this curve's grade-four and grade-five maps, and the beam and weighted trace limits use these same tensors and weights. For the corrected tensor \(T_i\), require both separated shell-flux limits
\(\displaystyle \phi_i (R) \longrightarrow y \ne 0, \qquad \phi_i (T_i) \longrightarrow y.\)

The physical and reference Ward identities and separated-jet comparisons used in Subsection 17.3 must concern these same data. Every use of grades four and five is restricted to a common tail on which the needed optical and coherence identities hold. A further subsequence is allowed only with all receipts, radii and charts restricted together. Tails may depend on each fixed finite collection of grades, labels and tests; the tables, derivative maps, \(R\), \(R_0\), \(O\), \(\kappa\) and cutoff extraction remain fixed.

Only one curve carrying these additional receipts is required for the normalization application; the prescribed-sequence one-stage property is not a premise of the chart-validity theorem. The separate existence result in Corollary 17.6 remains conditional for every sequence for which its selection input is available; no normalization receipt on every prescribed sequence is asserted. An all-sequence trace-asymptotic application must retain its stronger joint-receipt requirement on the requested sequences; the one-curve input here does not supply that requirement. Proposition 17.7 supplies this joint input on the funded family. Curves for the individual receipts separately, or a ratio of scalar normalizers tending to a constant, do not identify the charts and derivative maps. The chart theorem below does not assume or produce these additional analytic receipts.

Apply Lemma 17.3 with \(\mathcal{P}_i = B_i Y_i\), so that \(\mathcal{Y}_i = Y_i\). The corrected tensor \(T_i\) is conserved by its exact correction cancellation and the comparison identity (17.1). The lemma gives

\(\displaystyle Y_i (T_i) \longrightarrow R_0, \qquad B_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} Y_i (T_i) \longrightarrow - \kappa O, \) (17.7)

where \(R_0\) is the reference stress tensor and \(O\) the reference trace field. For the physical centered tensor \(R\), a separated shell-flux test on tensors, which we denote \(\phi_i\) to distinguish it from the functional \(\ell_i\) on \(V_0\), has limits \(\phi_i (R) \to y \neq 0\) and \(\phi_i (T_i) \to y\). Since the conserved space has dimension one, eventually \(T_i = t_i R\). Linearity gives \(t_i = \phi_i (T_i) / \phi_i (R) \to 1\), so dividing (17.7) by \(t_i\) proves

\(\displaystyle Y_i (R) \longrightarrow R_0, \qquad B_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} Y_i (R) \longrightarrow - \kappa O.\)

This is precisely the normalization argument used in Lemma 18.41. The two shell-flux limits are the physical-normalization estimates verified in Proposition 17.7: the physical and reference translation Ward identities and the separated-jet comparison must give these limits on this same comparison curve. The argument above makes explicit why the common nonzero Ward value forces the multiplicative constant to be one.

17.1.4Strict subsequences under a prescribed-scale selection input

Fix any sequence \(s_n > 0\) with \(s_n \to 0\). For a selection constrained to this prescribed sequence, the scalar recursion uses the following one-stage selection property, supplied on the funded family by Proposition 17.16, at each integer level \(d\): for every lower index \(N\) and every \(\theta > 0\) there are \(n > N\), a level \(j \geqslant d\), and a finite-level chart \(V_{d, j}\) such that

\(\displaystyle s_n < \theta, \qquad V_{d, j} \text{ has cutoff-} d \text{ matching receipts at location } j, \qquad r (V_{d, j}) = s_n . \) (17.8)

The matching receipts cover grades through \(d\), and their accuracy is the one requested at cutoff \(d\). The quantifiers require a chart at an exact member of the prescribed sequence after every lower index \(N\), not merely at some radius below \(\theta\). This is an explicit selection input.

Lemma 17.4. (Recursive strict scale selection) Suppose the one-stage selection property (17.8) holds at every level. Then there is a strictly increasing sequence of indices \(n_d\) and matching charts \(V_{d, j_d}\) at levels \(j_d \geqslant d\) such that \(s_{n_d}\) is strictly decreasing to zero and \(r (V_{d, j_d}) = s_{n_d}\).

Proof. Choose an admissible triple \((n_0, j_0, V_{0, j_0})\) at level zero, using the one-stage property with lower index zero and any positive threshold. Having chosen \((n_d, j_d, V_{d, j_d})\), apply the property at level \(d + 1\) with lower index \(n_d\) and threshold \(s_{n_d} > 0\). It gives \((n_{d + 1}, j_{d + 1}, V_{d + 1, j_{d + 1}})\) with \(n_{d + 1} > n_d\) and \(s_{n_{d + 1}} < s_{n_d}\). Induction gives strict monotonicity of the indices and strict decrease of the selected scales. Since \(n_d \to \infty\) and \(s_n \to 0\), the subsequence \(s_{n_d}\) tends to zero. The selected chart receipts hold at each level by construction.\(\Box\)

An arbitrarily-small-radius chart estimate alone does not establish (17.8): its good radii need not meet a prescribed sequence. The arbitrarily-small-radius construction chooses its own radii successively, below the preceding radius and a vanishing upper bound. The one-stage input is instead the stronger one-step output assumed in Lemma 17.15, which supplies a chart at an exact member \(s_n\) of the prescribed sequence. It is to be obtained from a funded finite-volume selection estimate whose funded chart and comparison hypotheses must hold for the present data. The lemma above proves the prescribed-sequence conclusion conditional on that input; it does not assume that a preselected scaling already follows the requested sequence. If the input is available for every positive null sequence, the conclusion holds for every such sequence. Proposition 17.16 proves that additional availability on the funded family.

17.1.5Corrected lifts and compatibility across grades

The gradewise maps obtained by finite-volume extraction and projection need not preserve the filtration inclusions. Here extraction and projection are performed first; the correction is then made directly in the selected table fields. Write \(E_d\) for the reference field space in grade \(d\) and \(S_d\) for that selected table field space. Let

\(\displaystyle \iota^E_d : E_d \longrightarrow E_{d + 1}, \qquad \iota^S_d : S_d \longrightarrow S_{d + 1}\)

be their inclusion maps. For \(d < e\), write \(\iota^E_{d, e}\) and \(\iota^S_{d, e}\) for their composites, and use the identity maps when \(d = e\). They obey the transitivity identities. The reference inclusion is injective. Choose a linear left inverse \(R_d : E_{d + 1} \to E_d\), so \(R_d \iota^E_d = 1\). When fields carry the finite signed symmetry and real structure, this retraction can be chosen to preserve them: begin with any left inverse and average its conjugates over the finite signed group. Then average that equivariant map with its conjugate-linear reflection. The signed actions commute with the real involution, so this second averaging preserves signed equivariance. Each conjugate remains a left inverse because the inclusion intertwines both actions. This is the finite-dimensional splitting used below.

For each cutoff \(k\), let \(U^{\text{raw}}_{k, d} : E_d \to S_d\) (\(d \leqslant k\)) be the raw projected chart maps. Define the corrected maps through grade \(k\) by

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} C_{k, 0} & = U^{\text{raw}}_{k, 0}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.9)}\\ C_{k, d + 1} & = \iota^S_d C_{k, d} R_d + U^{\text{raw}}_{k, d + 1} (1 - \iota^E_d R_d) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.10)} \end{array}\)

The first summand corrects the part of a field already represented at the previous grade; the second retains the new complement. Directly using \(R_d \iota^E_d = 1\) gives

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} C_{k, d + 1} \iota^E_d & = \iota^S_d C_{k, d} R_d \iota^E_d + U^{\text{raw}}_{k, d + 1} (\iota^E_d - \iota^E_d R_d \iota^E_d)\\ & = \iota^S_d C_{k, d} . \end{array}\)

Induction and transitivity therefore give, for every pair \(d \leqslant e \leqslant k\),

\(\displaystyle C_{k, e} \iota^E_{d, e} = \iota^S_{d, e} C_{k, d} . \) (17.11)

This is an exact identity whenever the displayed grades are at most \(k\), before the \(k \to \infty\) limit. It is the needed balanced inclusion relation; unrelated isomorphisms at early indices are not used to obtain it.

The same correction preserves the weak symmetry conditions by induction, since \(R_d\), both inclusions, and \(U^{\text{raw}}_{k, d}\) intertwine the signed and real structures. The unit in each grade is the iterated inclusion of the constant unit in grade zero. Thus the exact grade-zero unit identity, together with the balanced relation, gives the unit identity at every grade. It also preserves the tested approximation. To see the exact finite-slot identity, let \(I\) be a subset of the tuple's slots already using \(C\), and let \(\mathcal{W}_k^I (v)\) be the tested chart correlation with \(C\) in those slots and \(U^{\text{raw}}\) in the others. For \(i \not\in I\) with \(v_i \in E_{d + 1}\), put \(p_i = R_d v_i\) and \(q_i = (1 - \iota^E_d R_d) v_i\). Linearity in that slot, formula (17.10), and the table inclusion identity give the exact equality

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \mathcal{W}_k^{I \cup \{i\}} (v) -\mathcal{W}_0 (v) = & (\mathcal{W}_k^{I \cup \{i\}} (v [i \mapsto p_i]) -\mathcal{W}_0 (v [i \mapsto p_i]))\\ & + (\mathcal{W}_k^I (v [i \mapsto q_i]) -\mathcal{W}_0 (v [i \mapsto q_i])) . \end{array}\)

Here \(v [i \mapsto p_i]\) has grade \(d\) in slot \(i\), and \(v [i \mapsto q_i]\) has grade \(d + 1\). Induction on the number of corrected slots, using this grade reduction for a corrected slot, expresses the fully corrected discrepancy for any fixed tuple as a finite sum of raw tested discrepancies at fixed labels. Every summand tends to zero by raw approach, so corrected correlations approach the reference. The finite sum's size and label norms may depend on the tuple; this qualitative argument does not retain the raw numerical rate without tracking those factors.

The raw maps \(U^{\text{raw}}_{k, d}\) below are formed by the finite-dimensional ultrafilter limits and grade projections. Apply the correction after this projection. The balanced identity (17.11) is then exact for each fixed pair of grades. Once tested-correlation convergence makes the corrected maps \(C_{k, d}\) isomorphisms eventually at each fixed grade, the selected isomorphisms obey the inclusion identity for each fixed pair on a tail of indices. The exceptional tail may depend on the pair of grades; no single tail uniform in all grades is asserted. This is the compatible gradewise scaling conclusion needed below.

Here are the finite-dimensional gluing details. At cutoff \(k\), let

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{L}_k = \prod_{d = 0}^k \text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}} (E_d, A_d),\)

where \(E_d\) is the free reference grade-\(d\) field space and \(A_d\) is the finite family coefficient space at grade \(d\). Write \(S_d\) for the selected table field space. This is finite-dimensional. The finite-volume lifts into \(\mathscr{L}_k\) are assumed bounded on a set belonging to the one fixed ultrafilter. This is the finite-chart lift input: for each fixed \(k\) there is a finite bound, independent of volume and period, on that set. A bound of this form is assumed on an \(F\)-late set in (17.25). This stronger hypothesis supplies the ultrafilter-set bound when the ultrafilter refines the fused filter of the extraction order \(F\). Proposition 17.16 proves this bound for the chosen funded charts. The closure of a bounded set in a finite-dimensional space is compact, so every ultrafilter on that late set has a limit \(Y_k \in \mathscr{L}_k\). This is done for each integer \(k\) using the same ultrafilter. Let \(P_d : A_d \to S_d\) be the selected table's projection. Put

\(\displaystyle U^{\text{raw}}_{k, d} = P_d \circ Y_k (d) : E_d \longrightarrow S_d \qquad (d \leqslant k) .\)

The exact finite-volume reality, signed-reflection, and unit identities hold on a set belonging to the ultrafilter. Their maps are continuous, so they pass to \(Y_k\) and then to \(U^{\text{raw}}_{k, d}\).

For fixed \(k\), the calibrated-smearing argument also passes each finite-volume short-test bound to the limit. Let \(A_{k, v} (t)\) denote the tested correlation of a fixed tuple \(v = (v_1, \ldots, v_n)\) after applying \(U^{\text{raw}}_{k, d_a}\) and scaling by the supplied radius \(r_k\) (with \(r_k = s_{n_k}\) in the prescribed-sequence case); let \(A_{0, v} (t)\) denote its reference correlation. For a tuple of arity \(n \leqslant k\), labels of grades at most \(k\), and any smooth compactly supported test \(t\) inside the cutoff window, with \(\sup |t| \leqslant M\), the bound is

\(\displaystyle |A_{k, v} (t) - A_{0, v} (t) | \leqslant \frac{1}{k + 1} \hspace{0.17em} M \prod_{a = 1}^n \|v_a \| . \) (17.12)

The change-of-variables identity for the dilated test converts the finite-scale integral to the displayed tested correlation. For any \(\eta > 0\), the upper-bound receipt makes the finite-scale discrepancy at most the right side plus \(\eta\) on a late set. Since the fixed ultrafilter lies below that late filter, the limit obeys the same bound after letting \(\eta \downarrow 0\). The cutoff window radius tends to infinity with \(k\), so it eventually contains the support of each fixed test. Since \((k + 1)^{- 1} \to 0\), the tested correlations converge to their reference values. This is the approach property needed for the scaling chart.

Fix a grade \(d\). The reference smoothness and test-function uniqueness imply that the linear functionals obtained by inserting a grade-\(d\) field into smooth tested correlations separate points of \(E_d\). Since \(E_d\) is finite-dimensional, choose finitely many such tests \(\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_m\) spanning its dual. The raw approach estimate gives convergence of their values for \(U^{\text{raw}}_{k, d}\), and the finite-sum correction argument gives the same convergence for \(C_{k, d}\). On a finite basis the combined maps \(\Lambda_k = (\lambda_{j, k})\) therefore converge in operator norm to the injective reference map \(\Lambda = (\lambda_j)\). The minimum of \(\| \Lambda v\|\) on the unit sphere is positive. Hence \(\Lambda_k\) is injective for all sufficiently large \(k\), and since each functional factors through \(C_{k, d}\), so is \(C_{k, d}\). The dimension receipt gives \(\dim S_d \leqslant \dim E_d\); injectivity gives the reverse inequality. Thus \(C_{k, d}\) is an isomorphism eventually for each fixed \(d\). The exceptional tail may depend on \(d\).

Theorem 17.5. (Validity of supplied gradewise ultralimit charts) Fix the physical and reference smooth multilinear tables and their finite-dimensional field spaces, inclusions, derivative maps, units, real structures and signed actions as above. Retain the table inclusion identities and their compatibility with these structures. Fix the cutoff extraction and one ultrafilter refining its late filter. Suppose that positive radii \(r_k\) strictly decreasing to zero and matching charts at cutoff \(k\) are supplied, with the following receipts on these same data: the finite-volume lift bound, exact unit, reality and signed-covariance identities, the dimension bound \(\dim S_d \leqslant \dim E_d\) at each grade, the reference separating-test property, and the finite-volume short-test bound described above at each cutoff. The latter has arity and grade caps \(k\), tolerance \((k + 1)^{- 1}\) and cutoff windows eventually containing every compact subset of each fixed configuration space. The lifts and their calibrated tested limits use this fixed extraction and ultrafilter. For every such supplied family and its finite-volume limits, form the raw projected maps and apply the corrected-lift recursion. No prescribed-sequence one-stage property is assumed for this validity statement. The resulting maps \(C_{k, d} : E_d \to S_d\) (\(d \leqslant k\)) have the following properties: for each fixed \(d\), \(C_{k, d}\) is an isomorphism for all sufficiently large \(k\); the exact unit, reality, and signed-covariance identities hold at those indices; and for each fixed tuple and compactly supported test, the tested correlation converges to its reference value as \(k \to \infty\). More precisely, the uniform test estimate holds: for every fixed tuple \(v\) of reference labels, every compact set \(Q\) in configuration space and every \(M > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \sup_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} t \in C_c^{\infty}, \text{supp} \hspace{0.17em} t \subset Q\\ \|t\|_{\infty} \leqslant M \end{array}$}}} | A^C_{k, v} (t) - A_{0, v} (t) | \longrightarrow 0,\) (17.13)

where \(A^C_{k, v}\) is the tested correlation formed with the corrected maps \(C_{k, d}\). Moreover, for each fixed pair \(d \leqslant e\), the corrected maps obey \(C_{k, e} \iota^E_{d, e} = \iota^S_{d, e} C_{k, d}\) for all sufficiently large \(k\). The tail may depend on \((d, e)\).

Proof. Fix any supplied family and corresponding finite-volume limits with the stated receipts. At each cutoff \(k\), the common-ultrafilter finite-dimensional limit gives the raw projected maps \(U^{\text{raw}}_{k, d}\) and their tested correlation estimates. The corrected-lift calculation above produces \(C_{k, d}\); the finite slot argument transfers tested convergence, reality, and signed covariance to these maps. The finite separating-test argument then proves eventual injectivity grade by grade, and the dimension receipt makes each fixed-grade map eventually bijective. The exact balanced identity holds for every corrected map by (17.11); for each fixed pair of grades, intersecting the two eventual bijectivity tails shows the selected maps satisfy the claimed inclusion relation.

For (17.13), the finite-slot expansion writes \(A^C_{k, v} (t) - A_{0, v} (t)\) as a finite sum of raw discrepancies \(A_{k, w} (t) - A_{0, w} (t)\) at tuples \(w\) whose labels are obtained from those of \(v\) by the fixed maps \(R_d\) and \(1 - \iota^E_d R_d\); these tuples and their number do not depend on \(k\) or on \(t\). Once the cutoff window contains \(Q\) and \(k\) exceeds the arity and grades involved, (17.12) bounds each summand by \((k + 1)^{- 1} M \prod_a \|w_a \|\). Summing, the supremum in (17.13) is at most \((k + 1)^{- 1} MK_v\) with a constant \(K_v\) depending only on \(v\), and it tends to zero. The argument uses only the stated receipts of the chosen charts and limits, so it applies to every admissible choice, in particular the one retained in the joint-curve input for stress normalization.\(\Box\)

Corollary 17.6. (Existence along a prescribed null sequence) Fix a positive sequence \(s_n \to 0\) satisfying the one-stage selection property (17.8) at every cutoff. Retain the fixed table, extraction and chart receipts of Theorem 17.5 for the matching charts supplied by that property. Then there are strictly increasing indices \(n_k\) and matching charts with \(r_k = s_{n_k}\) strictly decreasing to zero. Their finite-volume ultralimits and corrected maps have all the conclusions of that theorem. The same conclusions hold for every admissible selection on this prescribed sequence with these receipts.

Proof. Lemma 17.4 supplies the indices and matching charts. The bounded lifts have ultralimits along the fixed ultrafilter, as above. All other receipts are retained by hypothesis, so Theorem 17.5 applies. Its validity conclusion applies equally to any other supplied admissible selection. This implication does not assert the one-stage property for an arbitrary sequence without its additional input.\(\Box\)

Completion and uniform tests.
To obtain isomorphisms at every index, fix for each grade \(d\) an index \(k_d \geqslant d\) for which \(C_{k_d, d}\) is invertible, and set
\(\displaystyle U_{k, d} = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} C_{k, d}, & d \leqslant k \text{ and } C_{k, d} \text{ is invertible},\\ C_{k_d, d}, & \text{otherwise} . \end{array} \right.\)

For each fixed \(d\) this changes only finitely many terms. Hence the tested limits for every fixed finite tuple are unchanged. More strongly, for a fixed tuple the equality of its finitely many grade maps holds on one tail independent of the test. Thus \(A^U_{k, v} (t) = A^C_{k, v} (t)\) there for every test \(t\), and the uniform supremum estimate (17.13) transfers to \(U\) unchanged. For each fixed pair \(d \leqslant e\), intersecting the two agreement tails transfers the balanced inclusion identity to \(U\); the same argument transfers the unit and symmetry identities. The exact identity for \(C\) before completion is therefore distinct from the eventual identity for \(U\). No tail uniform in all grades is needed or asserted. In the calibrated stress sequence above, \(i\) is this same index \(k\), with any further subsequence applied to all the data together as required by the joint-curve input.

The corrected-lift construction proves the pairwise eventual inclusion identities for the corrected maps; it does not claim one tail uniform over all grades. Production of the full calibrated curve also requires several substantive analytic estimates: the finite-volume chart selection (with the stronger prescribed-sequence property if that version is requested); the scale-dependent two-direction limits; the base and correction beam limits; the weighted false-sector flux washout and true-sector flux convergence; the separated-jet comparison; and the finite chart lift, unit, covariance, short-test, and dimension receipts used in the gluing. The next proposition installs these inputs on the same selected data. The finite-dimensional arguments above are used only after their particular hypotheses have been matched to those producers.

17.1.6Installing the analytic inputs on one selected curve

Proposition 17.7. (The joint calibrated curve on the funded family) Fix the common funded family with the joint chart purchases of Proposition 17.10, its selected hierarchy, cutoff extraction and refining ultrafilter. Use its actual response, Ward comparison and branch constructions of Subsection 18.17. For every positive null sequence there is a strict subsequence and a comparison curve on those radii satisfying the joint requirements of the calibrated-stress construction. The normalized physical stress and the reference stress are the same for all these choices. In particular, with \(q_i\) the actual arch scale,

\(\displaystyle r_i^4 U_{i, 4}^{- 1} R \longrightarrow R_0, \qquad q_i r_i^4 U_{i, 4}^{- 1} \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} R \longrightarrow - \kappa O.\)

Only the comparison charts and their radii are selected after the null sequence; the field table and its normalization are fixed beforehand.

Proof. Proposition 17.16 supplies the charts and all the validity receipts of Theorem 17.5. Complete their corrected maps at early indices as above. For every fixed finite group of grades these maps agree with the corrected maps on one tail, so all the receipts below can be restricted to that tail. Their uniform short-test bound gives derivative transport and compact convergence of all fixed jets by Subsection 17.3. The centered chart and its centered inverse identify the stress-array spaces. The right-inverse argument in that section then gives the physical conserved line before any correction tensor or trace limit is used. The reference input is the free rank calculation with its constructed derivative plan. Thus this step does not assume the joint normalization conclusion we are proving.

We identify the analytic estimates with their actual suppliers. Use the notation of Subsection 18.17: \(k_i\) is the summit of the selected chart, \(z_i = \hat{\beta}_{k_i}\), \(q_i = \zeta (k_i) > 0\) is its arch scale, \(\rho_i\) its response-size bound, and \(t_i\) its Ward comparison weight. In the present chapter's notation set

\(\displaystyle B_i = q_i, \quad A_i = \rho_i, \quad a_i = t_i, \quad h_i = z_i, \quad \mathcal{P}_i = q_i Y_i, \qquad Y_i (Q) = r_i^4 U_{i, 4}^{- 1} Q.\)

In particular \(\mathcal{P}_i\) includes the arch factor, while \(Y_i\) does not. The tensor to be compared with the physical stress is \(T_i = q_i L_i + c_i^0 \Omega_i + c_i^1 \Delta_i\), without an additional division by \(q_i\).

The optical operator is the exact operator (18.151), hence has the physical divergence kernel on this same coherent tail. The two correction columns converge in operator norm to the free two-direction map, as proved in the two-direction construction of that section. Its inverse is uniformly bounded. The response-size bound gives \(\|X_i \| \leqslant C \rho_i\), so solving the two-dimensional system gives \(\|c_i \| \leqslant C' \rho_i\) and exact cancellation. These establish the optical and correction requirements here, including conservation of \(T_i\); they do not require a limit of a chosen basis of kernels.

For the receiver, the selected medium is \(q_i^{- 1} \textsf{q}_{k_i} - \textsf{w}_{k_i}\), with precisely the chosen and reference responses of Subsection 18.23. Its three weighted limits are proved there using the priced mission and the small-means estimate. The selected Ward comparison uses the finite-volume inverse column of this chart and the same fixed-level ultrafilter limit. The application in Subsection 18.17 checks its tested and adjacent-column bounds, including their common lift identity. Therefore its receiver is uniformly bounded and its error is \(o (t_i^{- 1})\) in operator norm. Applied to the principal forecast of size \(O (\rho_i)\) and the bounded correction forecasts, it gives exactly (18.154). Since \(\rho_i \leqslant t_i\), these are (17.2)– (17.3) with the dictionary above. The scalar cancellation consequently proves \(c_i^1 \to - \kappa / 4\). The principal limit is unweighted; only the two correction errors are multiplied by \(t_i\).

For the tensor limits, the actual branch application in that same section identifies its ordered fourth-order labels with \(L_i, \Omega_i, \Delta_i\). The exact arch identity (18.157) identifies their scaled map with \(\mathcal{P}_i = q_i Y_i\) before using any limits. Thus (18.158) supplies the three beam limits. The elementary clock estimates give \(q_i / z_i \to 1\) and \(\rho_i / q_i \to 0\), supplying the last requirement in (17.5). The exponent-one branch washout gives

\(\displaystyle z_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (L_i) \to 0, \qquad z_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (\Omega_i) \to 0.\)

Since \(q_i \leqslant 2 z_i\) eventually, the first also holds with \(q_i\) in place of \(z_i\). Since \(|c_i^0 | \leqslant C \rho_i \leqslant Cz_i\), the second has exactly the weight required here. Finally the true branch has trace limit \(4 O\). These facts prove every clause of (17.6). The correction-limits lemma now yields the two asserted limits first for \(T_i\).

It remains to bind them to the fixed physical normalization. The annular field construction supplies the raw physical Ward responses for the conserved line. The finite-row argument normalizes these by one nonzero scalar; Corollary 16.4 identifies the reference tensor with coefficient one as \(- J^0\). These operations precede the trace correction and use only the chart comparison and shell law. They are the physical and reference Ward identities on the fixed table used in the common-trajectory physical-transfer argument. Choose its nonzero derivative observer. Compact convergence on the support of the shell gradient gives operator-norm convergence of its shell functionals: the error is bounded by the sum of the \(L^1\) norms of the four cutoff derivatives times the maximum correlation error on that compact separated shell. The exact change of variables is (18.168). Derivative transport gives the physical flux limit; the field limit of \(T_i\) gives its flux limit. Both limits are the same nonzero observer value, as proved in (18.169). Hence \(T_i = \lambda_i R\) with \(\lambda_i \to 1\), and division by this scalar proves the displayed limits for \(R\). This is also (18.170) under the stated dictionary.

All estimates used here hold on the selected family for every chart past a fixed finite-request threshold. The joint purchase was made before the null sequence, and the Ward comparison and branch constructions are applied to the selected charts themselves. At each fixed chart index only finitely many intermediate ultrafilter sets are intersected. Subsequent tails or subsequences restrict all maps, responses and weights together. No equality of independently selected charts is inferred from a scalar ratio, and no cutoff uniform over all grades or all tests is claimed. This proves the prescribed-sequence assertion as well as its one-curve specialization.\(\Box\)

17.2A coherent family of trace charts

This section records the actual selected-run route from one-scale chart data to the scale-dependent charts used by the trace calibration. The analytic chart receipts are provided by the funded construction and are identified below. We prove the subsequent scale selection and finite-dimensional limit steps explicitly. The correction and Ward-flux calculations are in Subsection 17.1.

17.2.1Finite-volume charts and their one-step estimates

Fix a grade \(d\), a binding, a gauge-invariant chart budget, and a scale parameter \(z \geqslant 0\). The funded chart receipt consists of three panels \(P (t, i)\), \(i = 0, 1, 2\), for \(0 \leqslant t \leqslant 1\), with arc coordinates \(b_i + t\). Their weight and angular parameters are constant in \(t\), and the three base arcs obey the finite separation inequalities for the probe count at grade \(d\). At every sufficiently large level \(k\) and every \(t \in [0, 1]\), the receipt supplies a finite-volume chart witness at that level and offset. The other fields of this witness are the chart, quantitative bound, legacy-cost, actuation, and stamp data used by the quantitative chart estimate. The single offset changes only the three arc coordinates.

The simultaneous choice needed here is stronger than choosing a funded run separately at every grade. We give its construction next. The local analytic inputs are stated separately, so that the choice of a common schedule does not hide an estimate.

Lemma 17.8. (A quantitative, signed-equivariant reading) Keep the funded hypotheses and notation of Proposition 16.25. At each fixed grade \(d\), its reading can be chosen so that, for all sufficiently large levels, uniformly in \(N, p, u\),

\(\displaystyle |Au - Tu|_1 \leqslant H^{- 1 / 2} |Au|_1, \qquad H =\mathcal{M}_k .\) (17.14)

Assume that the calibrated map \(A\) intertwines the signed coordinate symmetries of its label space. The reading can then also be chosen to intertwine those symmetries, preserving its exact unit and real structure.

Proof. The proof of Lemma 16.22 gives, before discarding its rate,

\(\displaystyle |Au - Tu|_1 \leqslant c_d \{H^{- 1} + D 2^D H^{- 3 / 4} + w_D H^{- 1 + \alpha (D + 1)} \} |Au|_1, \qquad \alpha = \frac{1}{4 (D + 2)} .\)

Since \(- 1 + \alpha (D + 1) < - 3 / 4\), this is at most \(C_d H^{- 3 / 4} |Au|_1\), with \(C_d = c_d (1 + D 2^D + w_D)\), for \(H \geqslant 1\). Thus it is at most \(H^{- 1 / 2} |Au|_1\) once \(H^{1 / 4} \geqslant C_d\).

For the symmetry assertion, let \(\Sigma\) be the finite signed coordinate group, with representation \(\rho\) on the grade-\(d\) label space, and put \(a_d = \max (1, \max_{\sigma \in \Sigma} \| \rho (\sigma)\|_{1 \to 1})\). Set

\(\displaystyle \bar{T} = \frac{1}{| \Sigma |} \sum_{\sigma \in \Sigma} \rho (\sigma) T \rho (\sigma)^{- 1} .\)

Reindexing this finite sum proves equivariance. Since \(A \rho (\sigma) = \rho (\sigma) A\), each summand's error on \(u\) is bounded by \(a_d^2 C_d H^{- 3 / 4} |Au|_1\). The triangle inequality gives the same bound for the average. Enlarging the common threshold until \(H^{1 / 4} \geqslant a_d^2 C_d\) proves (17.14) for \(\bar{T}\). Every summand fixes the unit, and signed coordinate actions commute with complex conjugation, so these identities survive the average. The finite group and constants depend only on the grade, not on \(k, N, p\). Hence taking the maximum for \(0 \leqslant d' \leqslant d\) gives one finite-cutoff threshold.\(\Box\)

Paying and averaging the complete stamp.
The symmetry of the covariance used here is a property of the actual finite-volume covariance, not a consequence of a bound on its entries. We verify it before averaging. On the finite torus let \(Q\) be the quadratic form of the selected one-form comparison, let \(d\) be the discrete curl and let \(P\) be its chosen solution operator, so that
\(\displaystyle QP = d^{\ast}, \qquad \ker Q \subseteq \ker d, \qquad \kappa = dP.\)

These are the finite comparison identities underlying the actual curvature multiplier in Subsection 14.12. For a signed coordinate change \(s\), denote its orthogonal actions on bonds and oriented faces by \(B_s, C_s\). The face action includes the shift of the base point when an axis is reversed. The defining quadratic form satisfies \(B_s^{\ast} QB_s = Q\) and \(dB_s = C_s d\). Indeed the bond and face actions are signed permutations; change the summation variables in the quadratic form. The derivative terms retain their orientation signs, while the centred block averages and their adjoints are carried to the same averages. The scalar penalty is unchanged. These statements persist through the finite ancestry by induction, applying the same identity to each composed block map.

Consequently \(Q (B_s PC_s^{\ast}) = d^{\ast}\). Subtracting \(QP = d^{\ast}\) shows that each column of \(B_s PC_s^{\ast} - P\) lies in \(\ker Q\), so application of \(d\) annihilates it. Hence

\(\displaystyle C_s \kappa C_s^{\ast} = \kappa .\) (17.15)

This argument does not require an inverse on the harmonic sector. The actual atlas uses precisely this finite curvature covariance, with the same ancestry and period. Restrict its two arguments to all oriented faces contained in the centred patch box. This face set is stable under the signed face permutations, including their base-point shifts; an arbitrary cube of positive-face base points would not suffice. In patch coordinates \(C_s\) is a signed permutation \(P_s\), and the restricted matrix \(K\) therefore satisfies \(P_s KP_s^{\ast} = K\).

Let \(\mathcal{E}_s\) denote the corresponding substitution on curvature polynomials. Each variable goes to one variable with sign \(1\) or \(- 1\). Thus it preserves degree and does not increase the absolute coefficient sum. The chain rule and (17.15) give

\(\displaystyle \Delta_K \mathcal{E}_s =\mathcal{E}_s \Delta_K, \qquad \mathcal{W}_K \mathcal{E}_s =\mathcal{E}_s \mathcal{W}_K .\)

The second equality follows by summing the finite exponential series on polynomials of bounded degree. Embedding the patch into the larger reader chart only renames variables; restricting \(K\) before this embedding gives the same identity. The radial reading also intertwines these substitutions with the signed action on reference labels: evaluate it on a formal connection jet, change the coordinates of the jet by \(s\), and change variables along each radial segment. Curvature components acquire exactly the signs in \(P_s\). Extracting a fixed weighted degree commutes with this change of variables. Equality on all formal jets is equality of the resulting label polynomials.

The corresponding physical identities use the same calibrated family. Write \(A\) for its calibrated coordinate and \(F_u^{\text{exp}}\) for its physical exponential entry. Its terminal label identification commutes with the signed action. This property passes to its transported coordinates: apply the signed pullback to the defining transfer integral and change the integration variables; the kernel and the selected representation are carried to themselves, and the terminal label is changed by \(\rho (s)\). Induction through the finitely many transfers gives the coordinate and density identities. On a completely healthy configuration the flagged terms vanish and the background density is strictly positive. Dividing the density identity by that same invariant background gives

\(\displaystyle A \rho (s) = \rho (s) A, \qquad F_{\rho (s) u}^{\text{exp}} (U) = F_u^{\text{exp}} (s^{\ast} U) .\) (17.16)

Thus the equivariance required in Lemma 17.8 holds for this family; no equivariance of an arbitrary choice of atlas or calibrated map is being asserted.

We now retain the strict margins in the selected-family construction of Subsection 16.8. For fixed polynomial order \(D\), set \(\gamma = 1 / (16 (D + 2))\). Its Taylor proof gives \(|L_u |_1 \leqslant 4 H^{2 \gamma} |Au|_1\), and the axial substitution defining the raw polynomial has row cost at most one eventually. Hence \(|W_u |_1 \leqslant 4 H^{2 \gamma} |Au|_1\). For the Wick polynomial one can use the stronger coefficient estimate already proved in Lemma 16.22,

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{H}u|_1 \leqslant (1 + H^{- 1} w_D) H^{\alpha} |Au|_1, \qquad \alpha = 1 / (4 (D + 2)) < 1.\)

The raw healthy error, before its margin is discarded in (16.67), is at most \(C_D H^{- D - 15 / 16} |Au|_1\), after increasing the common threshold; the remaining terms and the wide tail are smaller than every fixed negative power of \(H\). These are uniform bounds in the volume and period at a fixed grade and order.

For \(s \in \Sigma\) construct

\(\displaystyle W^s_u =\mathcal{E}_s W_{\rho (s)^{- 1} u}, \qquad \mathcal{H}^s_u =\mathcal{W}_K W^s_u, \qquad T^s = \rho (s) T \rho (s)^{- 1} .\)

The covariance and radial identities above prove that \(T^s\) is the reading of this very \(\mathcal{H}^s\), with the same coupling dilation as before. Let \(a_d\) be the finite orbit bound used in the quantitative bound lemma. By (17.16), \(|A \rho (s)^{- 1} u|_1 \leqslant a_d |Au|_1\). Therefore the raw and Wick coefficient bounds for every conjugate are at most respectively \(4 a_d H^{2 \gamma} |Au|_1\) and \(a_d (1 + H^{- 1} w_D) H^{\alpha} |Au|_1\). Since \(2 \gamma, \alpha < 1\), both are at most \(H |Au|_1\) on one later tail.

For the healthy ticket, signed pullback preserves the healthy set and the centred halo. The second identity in (17.16) was proved only for globally healthy configurations, so a local extension is needed. Suppose \(U\) is healthy at the smaller threshold \(\tau\) on the patch. Put it in the axial gauge of (16.65); each link used by the patch then has chord at most \(4 b \tau\). Retain those links and set every other link equal to the identity, obtaining \(Z\). Every plaquette of \(Z\), including those crossing the boundary of the retained links, is a product of at most four such links and inverses. The triangle inequality for the bi-invariant chord therefore bounds its chord by \(16 b \tau\). This is at most the background threshold \(\theta\) eventually, since \(b = H^{o (1)}\) and \(\tau / \theta = H^{- 1 + o (1)}\). Hence \(Z\) and \(s^{\ast} Z\) are globally \(\theta\)-healthy. The near insertion and the raw polynomial only read links retained in the patch, and are gauge invariant, so replacing \(U\) by \(Z\) changes neither of their values.

Apply the raw healthy approximation at \(s^{\ast} Z\), then the full-entry identity at \(Z\). The discrepancy is the sum of four terms: the raw approximation error at \(s^{\ast} Z\), the change of based paths between that raw evaluation and the signed polynomial at \(Z\), and the two wide tails at \(Z\) and \(s^{\ast} Z\). This decomposition does not assert that the near/wide split itself is equivariant. The wide tails on globally healthy fields have the superpolynomially small bounds established in Subsection 16.8.

Here is the additional path error. In the axial gauge each retained link is \(O (b \tau)\) from the identity. The two based paths for a signed image have length \(O (b)\), so their connecting holonomy is \(O (b^2 \tau)\) from the identity. Conjugating a plaquette curvature of size \(O (\tau)\) changes it by \(O (b^2 \tau^2)\), by the differential of the adjoint action near the identity. Reversing a plaquette gives its negative, with at most another quadratic error for chord coordinates. Thus the coordinate discrepancy after division by \(g\) is at most \(Cb^2 \tau^2 / g\), while both coordinate vectors have norm at most \(C \tau / g\). For \(D = 0\) the polynomial error is zero. For \(D \geqslant 1\), telescoping each monomial bounds the polynomial error by

\(\displaystyle C_D |W_{\rho (s)^{- 1} u} |_1 (1 + \tau / g)^{D - 1} b^2 \tau^2 / g.\)

Now \(\tau / g = H^{o (1)}\) and \(\tau^2 / g = x^{- 1 / 2} H^{o (1)}\), uniformly by the comparison of the actual coupling with \(x = \hat{\beta}_k\). Since \(\log H = (\log \log x)^2 = o (\log x)\), this error is smaller than every fixed negative power of \(H\). Combining the four terms gives, for one constant \(C_{d, D}\),

\(\displaystyle | F_u^{\text{near}} (U) - W^s_u (g^{- 1} \mathcal{C}(U)) | \leqslant C_{d, D} H^{- D - 15 / 16} |Au|_1 \leqslant H^{- D} |Au|_1\)

eventually, for every \(s\) and every healthy \(U\) in the prescribed halo. The last inequality follows by imposing \(H^{15 / 16} \geqslant C_{d, D}\). This pays the ticket separately from the quantitative bound's \(H^{1 / 4}\) margin. Support, locality, gauge invariance and measurability are preserved by these finite coordinate substitutions; the halo radius is unchanged.

Finally set

\(\displaystyle \bar{W} = \frac{1}{| \Sigma |} \sum_s W^s, \qquad \bar{\mathcal{H}} = \frac{1}{| \Sigma |} \sum_s \mathcal{H}^s, \qquad \bar{T} = \frac{1}{| \Sigma |} \sum_s T^s .\)

Linearity of Wick transformation and reading identifies the latter two maps with the Wick polynomial and reading of \(\bar{W}\). Degree does not increase. The triangle inequality and normalized averaging preserve each of the raw, Wick and healthy-ticket bounds just proved; there is no factor \(| \Sigma |\). Unit and reality hold termwise, and reindexing the group proves signed covariance of \(\bar{T}\). Together with Lemma 17.8, this is one complete signed stamp with the quantitative bound, at the original bounds. All the finite constants are paid by one later threshold at fixed grade and order, before the volume, period or panel offset is chosen.

Paying the finite moment and cumulant operations.
The numerical portion of the local chart purchase can be made before the coupling and the finite volume are chosen. We give the argument, including its order of choices. Put
\(\displaystyle H (s) = \exp ((\log \log s)^2), \qquad T (s) = 3 \exp ((\log \log s)^{3 / 2}) .\)

For every fixed \(q \geqslant 0\) and \(\epsilon > 0\),

\(\displaystyle (1 + \lceil (\log s)^2 \rceil)^q + T (s)^q = o (H (s)^{\epsilon}) .\) (17.17)

Indeed the logarithms of the two terms are respectively \(O (\log \log s)\) and \(O ((\log \log s)^{3 / 2})\), whereas \(\log H (s) = (\log \log s)^2\). This also holds uniformly with \(s\) replaced by \(x \in [s / 2, s]\), by monotonicity on a sufficiently late tail.

Fix an arity cap \(u \geqslant 2\), grade cap \(d\), and finitely many base arcs \(b_i \geqslant d + 6\). Set \(c = \max_i (b_i + 1)\) and \(z = cd + d + 2\). Assign to a moment word of length \(n \leqslant u\) the numerical coefficient allowance \(H^{zn + 1}\). This is a bound used in the choice of parameters, not an assertion about the moment of an arbitrary field. The passage between moments and cumulants is a finite polynomial operation in these moment coefficients: the coefficients are the fixed integer coefficients in the partition formulas, and their absolute values give nonnegative polynomial majorants. Thus every moment/cumulant coefficient bound at this fixed arity has a bound \(C_n H^{a_n}\), for some explicitly finite constants \(C_n, a_n\) depending on \(z, n\). For example, the cumulant polynomial is

\(\displaystyle \kappa_I = \sum_{\pi \in \mathcal{P} (I)} (- 1)^{| \pi | - 1} (| \pi | - 1) ! \prod_{B \in \pi} m_B ;\)

the corresponding absolute majorant is obtained by replacing each coefficient by its absolute value. Substitution into another such formula again gives a finite polynomial. No estimate here depends on the number of lattice sites.

Choose a negative weight \(f\) such that

\(\displaystyle f \leqslant - 8 - z, \qquad a_n + f + 1 < - 4 \quad (1 \leqslant n \leqslant u) .\)

This is a finite list of strict inequalities. Put \(U = 1 / 16 - f > 0\), choose an integer \(g \geqslant uU + 10\), and take Taylor order \(D = g + d + 5\). The degree and arity are now fixed before selecting the accuracy exponent below. The additional coefficient norms introduced by evaluation on a bounded cube, multiplication, truncation and Wick ordering are finite polynomial expressions in \(H\), the above moment bounds and \(T (s)\). To see this directly, the bound of a product is bounded by the product of the bounds; evaluating a polynomial of degree \(r\) on a cube of radius \(R\) costs at most \(\max (1, R)^r\) times its absolute coefficient sum. In a fixed degree, Wick ordering is a finite sum of contraction maps, so its coefficient bound is likewise a finite polynomial in the covariance bound. The finite moment and cumulant formulas introduce only finitely many such operations. Equation (17.17) therefore supplies bounds \(CH^A\) for all their bounds, with \(C, A\) independent of \(s\).

The corresponding differences are linear in a common error bound \(\varepsilon\) after enlarging those polynomial bounds. In fact, for a product of \(r\) factors with absolute values at most \(M\) and factorwise differences at most \(\varepsilon\), the telescoping identity gives a difference at most \(rM^{r - 1} \varepsilon\). Apply this to each monomial in the fixed formulas and sum its absolute coefficient. For any three fixed finite families of these polynomial difference bounds there are bounds

\(\displaystyle C_1 H^{A_1} \varepsilon, \qquad C_2 H^{A_2} \varepsilon, \qquad C_3 H^{A_3} \varepsilon .\)

Choose an accuracy exponent \(F > 0\) with \(F > A_1\), \(F > A_2 + g\) and \(F > A_3 + 3 / 2\), and use \(\varepsilon = H^{- F}\). After increasing the coupling threshold, these three bounds are at most \(1\), \(H^{- g}\) and \(H^{- 3 / 2}\), respectively. Any fixed factors, including \(2^n\) and the finite support-bound conversion constants, are absorbed by the strict inequalities. Similarly the earlier weight pays the uncorrected polynomial bound: the evaluation factor has growth at most \(H\), so \(C_n H^{a_n + f + 1} \leqslant H^{- 4}\) on a tail. These are choices of the numerical tolerances for the actual comparison estimates; they do not assert that an arbitrary field attains the chosen error.

The arity and room inequalities can be paid simultaneously. Set

\(\displaystyle M = (6 u + 3) (3 + 2 u) + 30, \qquad Q = M + 10.\)

Then \(6 n + 3 \leqslant M / (3 + 2 n)\) for \(n \leqslant u\), \(M \geqslant 10\), \(M + 10 \leqslant Q\), and \(1 - g \leqslant - uU - 2\). Taking the block length at least \(128 Q\) pays the remaining fixed geometric floor. If \(A \geqslant 1\) is any of the finitely many polynomial transport multipliers, also require \(2 \log A \hspace{0.17em} (b_i + 2) \leqslant \log L\). With

\(\displaystyle h = \left\lceil \frac{(b_i + 2) \log H (s)}{\log L} \right\rceil\)

we have \(h \log A \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2} \log H (s) + \log A \leqslant \log H (s)\) eventually, hence \(A^h \leqslant H (s)\). Taking a maximum over the finite arities and panels gives one block floor and one coupling threshold. The covariance/error estimates themselves must be applied with these very numerical choices.

Lemma 17.9. (Fixed-stage exponential budgets) Fix a block side \(L\), an arity cap \(u\), a goal \(g \geqslant 6\), and two nonnegative support exponents \(e_0, e_1\). Write \(N_L\) for the number of sites in one block (not the number of sites in the ambient torus), and set

\(\displaystyle C (x) = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil, \quad B (x) = C (x)^2, \quad Z (B) = (2 B + 1)^4, \quad W (B) = c_{\ast} B^4,\)

where \(c_{\ast} \geqslant 0\) is fixed. Put

\(\displaystyle a_i = \exp (15^4 (e_i + 7)), \quad \alpha \geqslant 8 \max (a_0, a_1), \quad \kappa = H (s)^{- 1 / 8}, \quad p = H (s)^{- 3}, \quad \tau = H (s)^{- 2 g - 10} .\)

For fixed \(a > 0\), put \(v (x) = \exp (- a (T (x) / 3)^2)\) and

\(\displaystyle \ell (k) = \max \left( 1, \frac{p}{\alpha k + H (s)^{- g}} \right), \qquad m (k) = a_1 \ell (k) k + 2 a_1 H (s)^{- 4} .\)

All the following inequalities hold uniformly for \(x, y \in [s / 2, s]\), \(n \leqslant u\), on a sufficiently late tail. Here \(B = B (x)\).

  1. The five logarithmic expansion tests hold: with \(h = e^{\kappa} - 1\) and \(f = e^{2 h} - 1\),

    \(\displaystyle Z (B) \kappa, Z (B) h, Z (B) (2 h), Z (B) (f + \kappa) \leqslant 1, \qquad e^{f + \kappa} - 1 \leqslant 1.\)

  2. For \(0 \leqslant k \leqslant H^{- 4}\),

    \(\displaystyle \frac{\kappa}{16} + W (B) N_L a_1 v (y) + W (B) nm (k) \leqslant \frac{\kappa}{4}, \qquad a_1 v (y) + m (k) \leqslant p.\)

    For \(0 \leqslant k \leqslant H^{- 1 / 2}\) and any \(0 \leqslant w \leqslant 1\),

    \(\displaystyle \frac{\kappa}{16} + W (B) N_L a_0 kw + W (B) na_0 k \leqslant \frac{\kappa}{4}, \qquad a_0 kw + a_0 k \leqslant \frac{\alpha k + H^{1 / 2} k^2}{2} .\)

    Moreover \(Z (B) \kappa / 4 \leqslant 1\), \(e^{\kappa / 4} - 1 + \kappa / 4 \leqslant \kappa\), and \(\ell (0) / x \leqslant 1\).

  3. The normalization-hole budgets hold:

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} W (B) N_L \exp (15^4 (e_1 + 8)) v (y) \leqslant \tau, \qquad 2 Z (B) \tau \leqslant 1, \qquad 16 Z (B) \tau \leqslant 1, \qquad 16 \tau \leqslant 1,\\ e^{2 \tau} - 1 \leqslant 1 - e^{- 16 \tau}, \qquad 32 \ell (0) \tau \leqslant \kappa / 4, \qquad e^{2 \tau} - 1 + \kappa / 4 \leqslant \kappa, \qquad \tau \leqslant \kappa / 16. \end{array}\)

The block floors may additionally be chosen so that \(10 \cdot 15^4 (e_i + 7) \leqslant Le_i\) whenever \(e_i > 0\); these are fixed inequalities, independent of \(s\) and the torus. For the normalization-hole test also reserve a positive input support exponent \(a_{\text{in}}\) with \(10 \cdot 15^4 (e_1 + 8) \leqslant La_{\text{in}}\), and an output exponent \(a_{\text{out}} \leqslant e_1 + 1\). These are the additional fixed support requirements of that test.

Proof. Every fixed power of \(B (x)\) is \(o (H (s)^{\epsilon})\) by (17.17). Also \(v (y) = o (H (s)^{- A})\) for every fixed \(A\): the negative logarithm of \(v (y)\) is \(a \exp (2 (\log \log y)^{3 / 2})\), whose ratio to \((\log \log s)^2\) tends uniformly to infinity on the half-band. Thus each fixed multiple of \(W (B) H^{- 1 / 2}\) is eventually at most \(\kappa / 16\). These statements allow all constants here, including \(N_L\), to be fixed before the coupling threshold.

For item 1 choose the threshold so that \(\kappa \leqslant 1 / 50\) and \(Z (B) \kappa \leqslant 1 / 100\). The inequality \(e^t - 1 \leqslant 2 t\) for \(0 \leqslant t \leqslant 1\) gives \(h \leqslant 2 \kappa\), \(f \leqslant 4 h \leqslant 8 \kappa\) and \(e^{f + \kappa} - 1 \leqslant 18 \kappa \leqslant 1\). Each of the four products is at most \(9 Z (B) \kappa \leqslant 1\).

For item 2, \(8 a_1 \ell (k) k \leqslant p\). Indeed, if \(\ell (k) > 1\), use \(8 a_1 k \leqslant \alpha k \leqslant \alpha k + H^{- g}\); if \(\ell (k) = 1\), use \(8 a_1 H^{- 4} \leqslant H^{- 3}\). Increasing the threshold also gives \(8 a_1 H^{- 4} \leqslant p\), so \(m (k) \leqslant 3 p / 8 \leqslant p / 2\). Likewise \(a_1 v (y) \leqslant p / 2\). Both \(v (y)\) and \(m (k)\) are at most \(H^{- 1 / 2}\) eventually. Hence each of the two \(W (B)\) terms in the first inequality is at most \(\kappa / 16\); their sum with \(\kappa / 16\) is at most \(3 \kappa / 16 \leqslant \kappa / 4\). The same reasoning applies to \(kw \leqslant k \leqslant H^{- 1 / 2}\) and \(a_0 k\). Its second inequality follows from \(2 a_0 k \leqslant \alpha k / 2\) and \(H^{1 / 2} k^2 \geqslant 0\). The remaining exponential bound follows from \(e^{\kappa / 4} - 1 \leqslant \kappa / 2\). Finally \(\ell (0) = H^{g - 3}\), and any fixed power of \(H (s)\) is \(o (s)\), which proves \(\ell (0) / x \leqslant 1\) uniformly.

For item 3 all the polynomial-radius inequalities follow from the first paragraph. In particular take \(\tau \leqslant 1 / 16\) and \(16 \tau \leqslant \kappa\). Then \(e^{2 \tau} - 1 \leqslant 4 \tau\), while \(e^{- 16 \tau} \leqslant (1 + 16 \tau)^{- 1} \leqslant 1 - 4 \tau\); the last inequality is equivalent to \(64 \tau^2 \leqslant 12 \tau\). This proves the first exponential comparison, and also \(e^{2 \tau} - 1 + \kappa / 4 \leqslant \kappa / 2 \leqslant \kappa\). Since \(\ell (0) \tau = H^{- g - 13}\), the patch budget follows by strict power comparison with \(H^{- 1 / 8}\). The remaining inequality \(\tau \leqslant \kappa / 16\) follows the same way. Finally the stated block floors are finite because the positive exponents have already been fixed.\(\Box\)

The first item pays the smallness tests needed when taking the local marked logarithm. The second pays its two support budgets, respectively for the marked and the unmarked energy exponents. The third pays the normalization-hole budget on one actual block step. In particular none of these tests requires a smallness hypothesis on the total volume: \(N_L\) counts a single fixed block. The accompanying geometric tests are equally uniform: \(B (y) \leqslant 2 B (x)\) on the half-band, since \(B (s) / B (s / 2) \to 1\), and a microscopic radius bounded by \(4 C (x)\) satisfies \(25 (4 C (x)) + 30 \leqslant C (x)^2\) eventually. Together with the fixed support-exponent inequalities these are precisely the radius, support and exponential tests used by the local transfer compiler. They do not supply that compiler's estimates on the actual amplitudes; those estimates must have the coefficients, covariance and normalization of the selected history.

Room and the actual restarted steps.
The period-independent room requirement is also a consequence of the paid clock. Let \(t_i\) be its logarithmic scale, so the denominator of the lattice spacing is \(e^{t_i}\) and the clock coupling is \(s_i = \beta_{\ast} + s_G t_i\). For sufficiently large \(i\) the retimed microscopic radius is at most \(4 s_i\). Therefore
\(\displaystyle \frac{100 (1 + 4 s_i)}{e^{t_i}} \longrightarrow 0.\)

Every physical period is a positive integer multiple of this denominator, so \(100 (1 + \text{radius} (r (s_i)))\) fits in every such torus. This is uniform over all period indices. It also holds simultaneously at every level in a late comparison window: if \(s_i \geqslant s_k / 2\) there, monotonicity and divergence of \(s_k\) force all such \(i\) beyond any fixed threshold. No threshold depending on the period is required.

The all-level representation needed for actuation is the actual one constructed in Proposition 13.40; an arbitrary family with the same density identity would not suffice. To apply that proposition here, retain on a step the original fine and coarse paths, their clips, the original funded tube and the local chart. The stage pair is one of \((j, j)\), \((j, j - 1)\) and \((j - 1, j - 1)\). Its block length and support exponents are fixed before the threshold. The radius and exponential budgets proved in Lemma 17.9, together with the preceding torus room, pay the corresponding local tests at that pair. Intersect these three eventual assertions, and the existing path, metric and chart requests, before choosing the stage threshold.

Here is the identification with the local compiler, which is essential when using its estimates. At the upper endpoint use the re-expression of Proposition 13.38, retaining its exact density, scalar and coupling. At the next step, the incoming representation is this very re-expression. Erase only its additive remainder to form the neutral representation used for pricing. Its path, clip, Gaussian law, forest, tag and warning sockets are unchanged; the corrected-warning argument of Subsection 14.11 therefore applies to the same selected data. The corrected-body and mixed compilers choose one alphabet and its scalar loading before the output representation. The neutral return estimate and the genuine density identity are estimates for that one alphabet. Its positive loading fixes the new scalar; its connected masked logarithm fixes the new additive remainder. Thus there is no choice of an unrelated coarse representation at this point.

Proposition 13.40 proves that the new representation satisfies the same inductive bounds: its neutral version has the inherited law, its forced allowance is \(\exp (- a_{\text{coast}} \text{hot} (x)^2)\), and its additive allowance is \(q_{n + 1}\). Its proof uses the actual path correction (13.47), the inflated component estimates and reassembly on this corrected alphabet. The step's normalization-hole allowance is \(\delta_n = q_n A_s^3\), not the preliminary allowance \(\tau\). The replacement is justified as follows. Equation (13.42) gives \(s^{- 1 / 200} \leqslant \delta_n \leqslant s^{- 1 / 400} \leqslant \tau\) uniformly in the permitted step count. Since \(\exp (- a \text{hot} (y)^2) = o (s^{- A})\) for every fixed \(A\), the first term in item 3 of Lemma 17.9 is at most \(s^{- 1 / 200} \leqslant \delta_n\). All its other upper bounds hold with \(\delta_n\) in place of \(\tau\), by monotonicity, except the comparison between the two exponentials. That comparison follows directly from the same calculation for every \(0 \leqslant \delta_n \leqslant 1 / 16\). Thus the normalization-hole estimates apply at the actual step cost; the two support-bound weakenings at the end of Subsection 14.11 preserve its values, full supports, loading and hole normalization. They therefore give the upper and lower marked transfer estimates on this particular step. Install this new representation only at its coarse level and continue backwards. Induction preserves all representations already installed, the endpoint stamps, the fixed coupling, and the original partition denominator. This supplies the actual short comparison family and its marked transfers, with the uniformity in fields, cutoff and period stated in Proposition 13.40.

This application uses the original funded path and chart data; it does not assume the desired estimates on the restarted representations. The finite-reader covariance comparison and its polynomial error bounds are separate from this actuation argument. In particular, the parameter choices above are not a substitute for verifying those comparison estimates for the selected covariance.

Reader comparison across a profile change.
The plateau formulation in Subsection 14.9 extends to each of the three adjacent profile pairs required here. This does not identify the two profiles. For a fine profile \(f\) and coarse profile \(c\), use the common strip rate \(\min (\alpha_f, \alpha_c) > 0\) and upper bound \(\max (\Omega_f, \Omega_c)\). Decreasing the exponential strip rate and increasing the majorant preserve each weighted row estimate. Keep the fine metric floor \(\eta_f\) in the common-cover construction, and keep the coarse metric floor \(\eta_c\) in the actual tower transfer. Both remain strictly positive; neither metric is replaced by the other. The retained tower, its crossing identity and the two actual clips identify the transferred covariance with the original coarse covariance, exactly as in Subsection 14.13.

For completeness, the short-row comparison has the following two cases. At an address retained by the short-row rule the prescribed row is the actual conditional row, so its error is zero. At a discarded address the truncated common-cover row is zero. The cover-tail bound on the fine metric, followed by the actual tower transfer to the coarse metric, bounds the original row at that address by \(Ce^{- ch_x}\). The same argument on a transport row gives the mean error. The constants \(C, c > 0\) now depend on the ordered pair of profiles. All periodic images are included in the discarded-address test, so there is no omitted wraparound term. The local Gaussian and guarded curvature estimates use these same paths, clips, tube, forest and tag; their coupling drift constant is the fixed one for this pair. Consequently the proof of (14.64) applies without change and yields any prescribed error \(H (s)^{- F}\) on \([s / 2, s]\) for each pair. Take the maximum of the three thresholds, rather than restricting the comparison levels to plateau interiors.

These are errors on one joint Gaussian model for the finite reader family, as in Subsection 14.15. One can see directly that composition preserves this identification. For consecutive independent centered conditional noises \(Z_i\) and actual linear transport maps \(A_i\), substitution of \(X_i = A_i X_{i - 1} + Z_i\) gives recursively

\(\displaystyle \mu_i = A_i \mu_{i - 1}, \qquad Q_i = A_i Q_{i - 1} A_i^{\ast} + \text{Cov} (Z_i) .\)

The tower transfer identity just used is the identity for these actual covariances. It holds step by step at a change of profile as well as inside a plateau. Each finite composition is therefore the joint law used in the polynomial integration, with all cross-covariances retained. No independent samples are chosen for the individual readers.

The numerical polynomial errors can now be stated without an unnamed moment bound. Use the coefficient norm \(| \cdot |_1\) of Subsection 14.16. For slot polynomials of degree at most \(D\), coefficient bounds \(A\), and calibrated tails of norm at most \(\xi\), its dimension-independent estimate is

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathcal{W}_{Q, \mu} \prod_{i \in I} H_i -\mathcal{W}_{\text{free}} \prod_{i \in I} F_i \right| \leqslant \{n \xi (A + \xi)^n + A^n \varepsilon\} [2 B (nD + 1)]^{nD},\)
