There is also a lower bound. Take an even nonnegative profile \(\chi\) equal to one on \([- 1, 1]\) and supported in \([- 2, 2]\). For \(s \geqslant 1\) containing the support of \(\chi_w\),

\(\displaystyle M_{m, s} \chi_w (E) \geqslant 2^{- m} (E + w)^{- 1} (1 + E)^{- 2 m} \quad (E \geqslant 0) .\) (17.58)

Indeed each kernel factor is at least \(\tfrac{1}{2} (1 + E)^{- 2}\) on the indicated supports. In the remaining integral restrict \(x\) to \([0, wE / (E + w)]\). There \(\chi_w = w^{- 1}\) and \(x^2 + E^2 \leqslant 2 E^2\), which gives \(2 E \int \chi_w (x) / (x^2 + E^2) \hspace{0.17em} dx \geqslant (E + w)^{- 1}\). Continuity gives the endpoint \(E = 0\).

17.6.2Changing the spectral axis by its reflection sign

For \(Q \subset \{1, 2, 3, 4\}\), \(i \in Q\), and profiles \(f_j\), write

\(\displaystyle P_i (h ; f, Q) = \int \mathcal{L}h (p_i) \prod_{j \in Q \setminus \{i\}} f_j (p_j) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_i^A (p) .\)

We use this expression only when its absolute integrability has been established by (17.57) or by a positive-time gap. If \(A\) has opposite reflection signs at \(i\) and \(j\), testing the same cluster autocorrelation in those two axes gives

\(\displaystyle P_i (f_i ; f, Q) = - P_j (f_j ; f, Q) .\)

For the full set of axes this is the spectral representation of the same ordinary integral on the two sides; their signs differ by reflection. For a smaller \(Q\), insert in each omitted coordinate the bounded profile \(\chi (p / (N + 1))\). It tends pointwise to one, and dominated convergence with the already integrable temporal response proves the stated identity. No relation between the two spectral measures has been assumed.

Suppose \(f_k \geqslant 0\) and the residual responses of \(f_i - b_i\) and \(f_j - b_j\) are nonnegative. Apply the identity to those two residuals and expand the two differences. Dropping the nonnegative full \(j\)-response gives

\(\displaystyle P_i (f_i - b_i ; f, Q) \leqslant P_i (f_i - b_i ; f [j \leftarrow b_j], Q) + P_j (f_j - b_j ; f [i \leftarrow b_i], Q) .\) (17.59)

A signed correction on the right can be replaced by any pointwise nonnegative majorant, since all its other factors are nonnegative.

Here is why this inequality buys arbitrarily small losses even if the original profile widths shrink exponentially. Suppose both reflection signs occur in \(Q\), \(A\) is on every hyperplane indexed by \(Q\), and \(w_j \leqslant 0\). For every \(\delta > 0\), sufficiently high subtraction gives exponent \(\delta\) for the residual response in axis \(i\), with transverse profiles \((f_j)_{e^{w_j n}}\).

We spell out the scale order. More generally allow \(- K \leqslant w_j \leqslant a\), and designate a set \(U \subset Q\) containing every axis whose width may shrink. Outside \(U\) require \(w_j \geqslant 0\). There is a positive admissible correction exponent \(a\). For \(0 \leqslant d \leqslant a\), \(s \geqslant 1\), require also \(w_i \leqslant d\) and eventual containment of the scaled \(i\)-profile in \([- se^{dn}, se^{dn}]\). Then this correction scale works after sufficiently many moments have been subtracted. If \(U\) is empty, use (17.57) with \(k = 0\). The transverse factors are uniformly bounded and the response has exponent \(\max (- w_i, dD)\). Choose \(aD < \delta\); the claim follows.

For the induction, change from \(i\) to an axis \(j\) with the opposite sign and apply the already established estimate with shrinking set \(U \setminus \{i\}\). Let \(a' > 0\) be its admissible correction exponent. Choose first a transverse moment \(2 k\) so large that

\(\displaystyle a' k > K \hspace{0.17em} | (Q \setminus \{i\}) \setminus \{j\} | + \max (K, \delta) .\)

Choose the corresponding moment exponent \(D\) in (17.57), and only then choose \(0 < a < a'\) with \(aD < \delta\). The finite formula (17.54) shows that the correction of \((f_i)_{e^{w_i n}}\) at \(se^{dn}\) is dominated in absolute value by \(g_{e^{dn}}\), for one fixed even nonnegative \(g\) vanishing near zero: its coefficients are bounded since \(w_i \leqslant d\), and their supports form a finite union of fixed annuli. Apply the induction hypothesis at axis \(j\) to this corrected \(i\)-profile. This bounds the second term of (17.59) with exponent \(\delta\).

The correction at axis \(j\) is bounded by \(h_{e^{a' n}}\) with fixed \(h\) vanishing near zero. Consequently

\(\displaystyle |h_{e^{a' n}} (p_j) | \leqslant Ce^{- 2 ka' n} |p_j |^{2 k} .\)

The omitted normalization \(e^{- a' n}\) only improves this bound because \(a' > 0\). Each other transverse factor costs at most \(Ce^{Kn}\). Equation (17.57) now bounds the first term of (17.59) with exponent at most

\(\displaystyle - 2 ka' + K \hspace{0.17em} | (Q \setminus \{i\}) \setminus \{j\} | + \max (- w_i, dD) < \delta .\)

This proves the induction step. To arrange the finite induction, visit an axis of \(U\) and then erase it; if its sign is the same as the present axis, first pass through an opposite-sign axis. Every such intermediate pass is allowed because both signs occur in \(Q\). Each erasure reduces \(|U|\), so the process ends. Moment orders and growing widths have been chosen in reverse order along this finite route, before \(n\) tends to infinity.

Apply this estimate after replacing \(f_i\) by \(\chi\) and its width by \(e^{qn}\), \(q < 0\), and take a fixed correction scale (\(d = 0\)). Equation (17.58), restricted to \(0 \leqslant E \leqslant 1\), proves the useful point estimate

\(\displaystyle \int_{E \leqslant 1} \frac{\prod_{j \in Q \setminus \{i\}} (f_j)_{e^{w_j n}} (p_j)}{E + e^{qn}} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_i^A (p) = O (e^{\delta n}) .\) (17.60)

This holds for every \(\delta > 0\) and all nonnegative even compactly supported transverse profiles. The constants may depend on their shapes.

17.6.3Keeping a genuine temporal gap

A collinear cluster is on each hyperplane perpendicular to its line, but need not be on the hyperplane perpendicular to the line itself. We cannot use arbitrarily small temporal displacements on that axis. Fix \(u \geqslant 0\) so that translating the cluster by \(u\) places all its points strictly in the positive half-space. Set \(c = \varphi \ast \varphi\) and \(\Phi = \hat{c} = (\hat{\varphi})^2\). Since \(\varphi\) is real and even, \(\Phi\) is nonnegative, even, rapidly decreasing, and \(\Phi (0) = 1\). Choose a smooth even cutoff \(\zeta\) equal to one on \([- 2 u - 1, 2 u + 1]\) and zero outside \([- 2 u - 2, 2 u + 2]\). Let \(b_w\) be the real profile with Fourier transform \(\zeta (t) \Phi (wt)\), and put \(r_w = c_w - b_w\). Then

\(\displaystyle \hat{r}_w (t) = (1 - \zeta (t)) \Phi (wt), \qquad \mathcal{L}r_w (E) = \int e^{- |t| E} (1 - \zeta (t)) \Phi (wt) \hspace{0.17em} dt \geqslant 0.\)

The transform vanishes on the required gap. Its response is bounded by \(Cw^{- 1} e^{- 2 uE}\). Also \(\|b_w \|_{\infty} \leqslant C_u\) uniformly in \(w\): Fourier inversion bounds it by a fixed multiple of the \(L^1\) norm of \(\zeta (t) \Phi (wt)\), and \(\Phi\) is bounded.

For all sufficiently small \(w\), uniformly on \(0 \leqslant E \leqslant 1\),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}r_w (E) \geqslant c_u (E + w)^{- 1} .\) (17.61)

To see this, put \(R = 2 u + 2\) and choose \(\gamma > 0\) such that \(\Phi (z) \geqslant 1 / 2\) for \(|z| \leqslant 3 \gamma\). Take \(wR \leqslant \gamma\) and integrate over \(R < t < R + \gamma / (E + w)\). On this interval \(wt \leqslant 2 \gamma\), \(Et \leqslant R + \gamma\), and \(\zeta (t) = 0\). Thus the integrand is at least \(e^{- (R + \gamma)} / 2\), and the interval has length \(\gamma / (E + w)\).

Suppose the remaining three axes have both reflection signs and contain an axis \(j\) of sign opposite to the gap axis \(i\). The preceding finite sign-change argument, applied only to those three axes, provides a growing subtraction scale \(e^{a' n}\) at \(j\), with any prescribed loss \(\delta > 0\). Use (17.59) with \(f_i = c_{e^{qn}}\), \(b_i = b_{e^{qn}}\), and \(q < 0\). Its second term is bounded by the uniform constant \(\|b_{e^{qn}} \|_{\infty}\) times that three-axis response; hence it has exponent \(\delta\). Its first term contains the annular correction \(h_{e^{a' n}} (p_j)\). For every fixed \(k\),

\(\displaystyle \int \mathcal{L}r_{e^{qn}} (E) |p_j |^{2 k} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_i^A \leqslant Ce^{- qn} \int e^{- 2 uE} |p_j |^{2 k} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_i^A .\)

The last integral is finite. If the other transverse widths have \(w_l \geqslant - K\), the first term therefore has exponent at most \(- 2 ka' + K \hspace{0.17em} | (\{1, 2, 3, 4\} \setminus \{i\}) \setminus \{j\} | - q\). Choose \(k\) large enough to make this at most \(\delta\). Equation (17.61) gives the point estimate with a genuine gap in axis \(i\). The measures have stayed fixed throughout.

More explicitly, let the cluster lie on a line in direction \(l\), have both reflection signs among the axes other than \(l\), and have an opposite-sign axis to \(l\) among those axes. For arbitrary \(w_j \leqslant 0\), \(q < 0\) and nonnegative even compactly supported \(f_j\), the full low-energy response in axis \(i\) has exponent

\(\displaystyle \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \delta, & i = l,\\ - w_l + \delta, & i \ne l. \end{array} \right.\) (17.62)

For \(i = l\) this is the gap argument. Otherwise apply (17.60) with \(Q\) the three axes other than \(l\), then bound the remaining factor by \(\|f_l \|_{\infty} e^{- w_l n}\). In the exterior application the profiles have shifted exponents \(\nu_j = 1 + w_j\), where \(w_l \geqslant - 150\). Thus with \(\delta = 1\) the missing-axis exponent is \(- \nu_l + 1 = - w_l \leqslant 150\); the line-axis exponent is \(1\). These bounds hold even when the resolvent regularizer is \(e^{- 3000 n}\).

17.6.4The exterior of the low spectral box

This part needs no subtraction or mixed-sign assumption. Fix complex Schwartz profiles \(f_j\), exponents \(w_j \geqslant - K\), and an axis \(d\) with \(w_d < 0\). Assume \(\hat{f}_d\) vanishes on \((- 1, 1)\). Use the temporal profile \(f_d (\hspace{0.17em} \cdot \hspace{0.17em} / e^{w_d n})\) and the transverse profiles \(e^{- w_j n} f_j (\hspace{0.17em} \cdot \hspace{0.17em} / e^{w_j n})\). If \(|b| = 1\), their temporal response \(L_n (E)\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle |L_n (E) | \leqslant \| \hat{f}_d \|_1 \exp (- E / e^{w_d n}), \qquad E \geqslant 0.\)

Indeed the Fourier transform of the temporal profile is \(e^{w_d n} \hat{f}_d (e^{w_d n} t)\), supported where \(|t| \geqslant e^{- w_d n}\), with unchanged \(L^1\) norm; the sign factor \(b\) has modulus one.

Put

\(\displaystyle C_n = \{p : 0 \leqslant p_d \leqslant 1, \quad |p_j | \leqslant e^{(1 + w_j) n} (j \ne d)\} .\)

The product of the three transverse factors is at most \(Ce^{3 Kn}\). Outside the indicated transverse box, at least one factor has normalized argument of size greater than \(e^n\). Its Schwartz seminorm of order \(r\) therefore improves this to \(C_r e^{(3 K - r) n}\). Given any real \(y\) and \(R \geqslant 0\), choose \(r\) large enough. On \(E \leqslant 1\) this proves the bound \(Ce^{yn} e^{- RE}\) off \(C_n\), with a harmless factor \(e^R\). For \(E \geqslant 1\), exponential growth of \(e^{- w_d n}\) gives, eventually,

\(\displaystyle e^{- E / e^{w_d n}} \leqslant e^{(y - 3 K) n} e^{- RE} .\)

For example choose \(n\) so that \(e^{- w_d n} \geqslant R + |y - 3 K| n + 1\) and use \(E \geqslant 1\). Thus the full packet satisfies

\(\displaystyle |\boldsymbol{1}_{C_n^c} (p) L_n (p_d) \prod_{j \ne d} e^{- w_j n} f_j (p_j / e^{w_j n}) | \leqslant Ce^{yn} e^{- Rp_d} .\) (17.63)

Choose \(R\) large enough to place the two fixed clusters strictly inside the positive-time chamber. The exponentially weighted moment statement then makes the majorant integrable for each of the four positive measures in their polarization identity. Integrating and adding that finite identity proves exponent \(y\) for the exterior cross response. In particular \(y = - 101\) is available, without taking a boundary value at zero energy.

The elementary zeroth response used inside \(C_n\) is also explicit. If \(1 + w_j \geqslant - K\), then

\(\displaystyle \int_{E \leqslant 1} \frac{\prod_{j \ne d} \chi_{e^{(1 + w_j) n}} (p_j)}{E + 1} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_d^A (p) = O (e^{3 Kn}) .\)

Each of three profiles is bounded by \(\| \chi \|_{\infty} e^{Kn}\) and the remaining integral is finite. With \(K = 300\) the exponent is \(900\).

17.6.5The diagonal-stress estimate and its algebraic reduction

For every \(\delta > 0\), even nonnegative smooth compactly supported transverse profiles \(f_j\), and \(w_j \leqslant a \leqslant 0\), the packet application also needs the improved diagonal-stress bound

\(\displaystyle \int_{E \leqslant 1} \frac{Ee^{4 an}}{(E + e^{an})^4} \prod_{j \ne d} (f_j)_{e^{w_j n}} (p_j) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_d^{J_{dd}} (p) = O (e^{(\delta + 4 a) n}), \qquad w_j \leqslant a \leqslant 0.\) (17.64)

It is a separate consequence of stress conservation, not of (17.60). The following finite algebra explains the normalization in its proof. Put \(w = e^{an}\) and keep one sufficiently large subtraction order \(m\) fixed. The subtracted differences of profiles at widths \(w\) and \(2 w\) give three real responses \(F, G, H\) with \(F \geqslant 0\), \(G \leqslant 0\), \(H \geqslant 0\). Conservation and the fixed-band estimates are required to supply real sequences \(A, B, V, W, R, T\) such that, with \(x = \delta + 2 a\), each of

\(\displaystyle V - A, \quad W - 4 B, \quad R - V, \quad T - 4 W, \quad F - (A - B), \quad G - (V - W), \quad H - (R - T)\)

has exponent \(x\). These are estimates on the same subtracted profiles. They imply \(F = O (e^{xn})\): if \(E_n\) is the sum of their absolute errors, then \(G \leqslant 0\) gives \(A - 4 B \leqslant 3 E_n\) and \(H \geqslant 0\) gives \(A - 16 B \geqslant - 7 E_n\). Hence \(B \leqslant (10 / 12) E_n\) and \(0 \leqslant F \leqslant A - B + E_n \leqslant (13 / 2) E_n\). The constants can be enlarged freely; the exponent is unchanged.

The positive response kernel of this difference has a lower bound \(c_m Ew^2 / (E + w)^4\) on \(0 \leqslant E \leqslant 1\). This follows directly from (17.55), at the fixed subtraction scale \(s = 8\). Write \(\varphi_k (x) = x^{- 2 k} \varphi (x)\) and compare its widths \(w\) and \(2 w\), with \(0 < w \leqslant 1\). After scaling back to \(x \in \text{supp} \hspace{0.17em} \varphi\), the factor \((x^2 w^2 + E^2)^{- 1}\) and every numerator of the product decrease when \(w\) is replaced by \(2 w\). The denominators of the product do not change. Thus every such dyadic response is nonnegative. For \(k = 0\) and \(E \leqslant 1\), the product at \(2 wx\) is at least \(2^{- m} 2^{- 2 m}\). Furthermore, since \(1 \leqslant |x| \leqslant 2\),

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{w^2 x^2 + E^2} - \frac{1}{4 w^2 x^2 + E^2} \geqslant \frac{w^2}{400 (E + w)^4} .\)

Indeed the numerator of the difference is \(3 w^2 x^2 \geqslant w^2\), and its positive denominator is at most \(400 (E + w)^4\). Integrate against \(\varphi (x) \varphi (v)\), both of integral one, and multiply by \(2 E\). The bound follows with \(c_m = 2 \cdot 2^{- 3 m} / 400\); \(E = 0\) follows by continuity. Multiplying its integral bound by \(c_m^{- 1} w^2\) would give exactly (17.64), with exponent \(\delta + 4 a\). For the intended choice \(a = - 99\), \(\delta = 1\), this is \(- 395\). Subsection 17.7 proves the conservation and fixed-band estimates that produce these seven errors for the same stress profiles. Together with the reduction above, it supplies the stated cone bound used in the exterior packet argument.

17.7Conservation and the diagonal-stress spectral bound

We now supply the conservation estimates required in Subsection 17.6. The stress tensor \(J_{ab}\) is real, symmetric, conserved, and covariant under signed coordinate permutations. Its derivatives are the actual insertion derivatives of the same hierarchy. The argument requires neither full rotation covariance nor a value of a correlation at a collision.

Write \(K_{abcd} (x) = S (J_{ab} (x), J_{cd} (0))\) for \(x \ne 0\). Choose one positive integer \(D\) large enough that these finitely many kernels and their first derivative kernels are bounded by \(C \min (1, \|x\|_{\infty})^{- D}\). Such an integer exists by the separated polynomial derivative bounds. In this section a test functional is required only on products \(\prod_i h_i (x_i)\) of one-dimensional Schwartz functions. We do not assert a collision extension with additional positivity properties.

17.7.1A symmetric extension and its contact terms

Choose compactly supported smooth functions \(g_r\) with \(g_r^{(k)} (0) = \delta_{rk}\) for \(k < D + 1\), and \(g_r (- x) = (- 1)^r g_r (x)\). For example use \(x^r / r\)! times a fixed even cutoff equal to one near zero. Put \(\Pi_D h = \sum_{r < D} h^{(r)} (0) g_r\). Define

\(\displaystyle R_{abcd} (h) = \int K_{abcd} (x) \left( \prod_i h_i (x_i) - \prod_i \Pi_D h_i (x_i) \right) dx.\)

The difference of products is a finite telescoping sum, each of whose terms contains one factor vanishing to order \(D\) at zero. Taylor's theorem bounds that factor by \(C |x_i |^D \leqslant C \|x\|_{\infty}^D\) near zero. Thus the integrand is bounded there. Away from zero the kernel is bounded and each product is integrable. This proves absolute integrability and multilinearity. If one \(h_i\) vanishes to order \(D\), its Taylor projection is zero and \(R (h)\) equals the ordinary integral of the kernel.

Use the same cutoff in all four coordinates. The product subtraction commutes with signed coordinate permutations. The original kernel's tensor law, symmetry in each index pair and exchange of the two pairs therefore pass to \(R\). Pair exchange uses translation, permutation of insertions and inversion \(x \mapsto - x\); the rank-four tensor has positive inversion sign.

Let \(\Delta_{bcd} (h) = \sum_a R_{abcd} (h [a \leftarrow h_a'])\). If one factor \(h_i\) vanishes to order \(D + 1\), integration by parts is legitimate and conservation gives \(\Delta_{bcd} (h) = 0\). Indeed the original test and each differentiated test cancel the stated collision powers; integrate first along a coordinate line whose transverse coordinates are nonzero, where the kernel is smooth, and then use Fubini. Those exceptional transverse coordinates have Lebesgue measure zero. The Schwartz decay and the same integrable bounds justify the integration by parts. This avoids imposing an unproved boundary value at the origin.

Replacing the four factors successively by their Taylor projections of order \(D + 1\) now shows that \(\Delta\) depends on finitely many jets at zero. We normalize its linear jets. Let \(h^{(a)}\) have factor \(g_1\) in coordinate \(a\) and \(g_0\) elsewhere, and set \(c_{abcd} = \Delta_{bcd} (h^{(a)})\). Reflection of a coordinate appearing an odd number of times among \(a, b, c, d\) changes this number's sign, so \(c_{abcd} = 0\) unless the indices occur in pairs. In the three possible pair patterns, coordinate permutation and symmetry in \(c, d\) show \(c_{abcd} = c_{bacd} = c_{cdab}\). For instance, the patterns \(aabb\), \(abab\) and \(abba\) reduce respectively to swapping the two coordinate names, swapping them followed by the last-pair symmetry, and those same two operations. Thus the correction

\(\displaystyle \tilde{R}_{abcd} (h) = R_{abcd} (h) - c_{abcd} \prod_i h_i (0)\)

retains all the stated tensor symmetries and all collision-free evaluations. Its divergence has zero linear jets. Inversion makes that divergence odd, so its jets of even total order are also zero. Its finite jet expansion therefore contains only total orders at least three.

For real frequency profiles \(f_i\) let \(T_{abcd} (f) = \tilde{R}_{abcd} ((\hat{f}_i)_i)\), and write \(P_i T\) for insertion of the multiplier \(p_i\) in that frequency profile. Our Fourier convention is \((\hat{f})' = - i \widehat{pf}\). Consequently

\(\displaystyle Q_{bcd} = \sum_a P_a T_{abcd}\)

is \(i\) times the divergence functional. If all profile widths are \(e^{w_i n}\) with \(w_i \leqslant a \leqslant 0\), its finite jet expansion has exponent \(3 a\). Indeed a jet of multiorder \(\alpha\) is a fixed coefficient times \(\exp (n \sum_i w_i \alpha_i)\), and its total order is at least three. Multiplication by one extra frequency coordinate adds at most \(a\) to this exponent. These are actual estimates for the normalized contact terms.

17.7.2Fixing one frequency band

We need a second estimate: if the profile in axis \(i\) is fixed, compactly supported and vanishes near zero, and the other widths are \(e^{w_j n}\), \(w_j \leqslant 0\), then \(T_{abcd}\) has every positive exponent \(\delta\), provided the other profiles have the reflection parities of these four tensor indices. We give the details that make this estimate independent of a rotation law.

First extend the positive subtraction argument to an odd temporal profile \(f\). Define its correction by

\(\displaystyle B^{\text{odd}}_{m, s} f (p) = p^{- 1} B_{m, s} (pf) (p) .\)

The numerator vanishes near zero, so this is a smooth compactly supported function. The residual has all moments below \(2 m\) equal to zero: its even moments vanish by oddness, and each odd moment is an even moment of the ordinary residual of \(pf\). For \(E > 0\) its signed Laplace response is

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}_{- 1} (f - B^{\text{odd}}_{m, s} f) (E) = - 2 i \int \frac{R_{m, s} (pf) (p)}{p^2 + E^2} \hspace{0.17em} dp.\)

The positive-kernel formula still applies to the integral on the right. For fixed odd \(f\), \(|f (p) | \leqslant L |p|\) by the fundamental theorem of calculus. If its support is in \([- R, R]\), \(R \geqslant 1\), then \(|pf_w (p) | \leqslant Lp^2 / w^2\) on \(|p| \leqslant Rw\). Every kernel factor is bounded by \(C_{m, s} (1 + E)^{- 2 m}\) in product. When \(E \leqslant w\), use \(p^2 / (p^2 + E^2) \leqslant 1\); when \(E \geqslant w\), use \(p^2 \leqslant R^2 w^2\) and \((p^2 + E^2)^{- 1} \leqslant E^{- 2}\). Integration over an interval of length \(2 Rw\) gives in both cases

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{L}_{- 1} (f_w - B^{\text{odd}}_{m, s} f_w) (E) | \leqslant C (E + w)^{- 1} (1 + E)^{- 2 m} .\)

The positive lower bound in (17.58) therefore majorizes this by \(C' M_{m, s} \chi_w (E)\), uniformly for \(0 < w \leqslant 1\) when \(s\) contains the supports. The assertion at \(E = 0\) follows by continuity. For an even profile, choose an even nonnegative compactly supported majorant \(g \geqslant |f|\) and use positivity on \(g + f\) and \(g - f\) instead. Thus either parity has a positive response majorant.

For two clusters \(A, B\), the common projection-valued measure gives a cross-measure estimate. If \(|q|^2 \leqslant uv\) with \(u, v \geqslant 0\), then

\(\displaystyle | \mu_i^{B, A} (q) | \leqslant t \int u \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_i^B + t^{- 1} \int v \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_i^A, \qquad t > 0.\) (17.65)

To prove it, first use a common admissible positive-time placement. Relative to the sum of its two finite diagonal spectral measures, the \(2 \times 2\) matrix of densities is positive almost everywhere: test its quadratic form on the countable dense set of vectors with rational real and imaginary parts. Its off-diagonal entry is therefore bounded by the geometric mean of the diagonal entries. Integrate, apply Cauchy–Schwarz and then \(\sqrt{UV} \leqslant tU + t^{- 1} V\). Exponential undamping multiplies all entries by the same positive function, so preserves the inequality. Truncation and absolute integrability justify its use for the responses here, including energy-zero atoms.

Suppose each cluster has both reflection signs among the four axes and is on every coordinate hyperplane. In a chosen coordinate subtract enough moments from its frequency profile, using the even or odd correction according to the product of the two reflection signs. The previous majorant, the mixed-sign estimate of Subsection 17.6 for each diagonal measure, and (17.65) give exponent \(\delta\) for the tested cross correlation. All transverse profiles can first be majorized by fixed even nonnegative profiles. There are only two diagonal measures and finitely many polarization integrability conditions, so choose the subtraction order after all of them. This proves the estimate with one subtracted coordinate.

Now keep a profile in coordinate \(i\) fixed and away from zero. At another coordinate \(j\) split the shrinking profile into its moment-zero residual and its finite correction. The residual is controlled by the preceding estimate. Each correction is a finite linear combination of fixed annular profiles, with coefficients of exponent at most zero, since \(w_j \leqslant 0\). For odd parity the additional division by \(p_j\) is harmless on those fixed annuli. Apply induction to the smaller set of shrinking coordinates in each correction term. At the base every profile is fixed, so the functional is constant. The correction order may depend on all profiles already fixed at that stage; only finitely many such choices occur. No uniform choice over an infinite family of profiles is required.

To apply this to stress components, an off-diagonal component \(J_{ab}\), \(a \ne b\), already has both signs: it is odd in axes \(a, b\) and even in the other two. A diagonal component is replaced by \(D_i J_{aa}\), which is odd in \(i\) and even in the other axes. Move that derivative to the fixed \(i\)-profile by integration by parts, dividing that frequency profile by \(p_i\). This is smooth because the profile vanishes near zero. The first and second insertions give respectively the factors \(- i\) and \(i\); doing both replacements composes these identities. Sufficiently many moments in the subtracted \(j\)-profile justify the ordinary integrals and integration by parts, as in the extension construction above.

Choose representative insertion tokens for these field labels. Average over the four commuting reflection involutions with the desired signs. This finite projection leaves the represented field unchanged and produces actual clusters with those exact signs; their positions are still zero. The two modified stress labels thus satisfy the mixed-sign hypotheses. The preceding finite induction proves the asserted fixed-band estimate for the original extended stress functional, with the required transverse parities. It also applies after any fixed finite number of frequency multiplications, with their corresponding extra scale factors.

17.7.3Three responses linked by conservation

Fix a spectral axis \(i\). All functionals in this subsection refer to the same normalized extension. Define

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} T_0 & = T_{iiii},\\ T_1 & = \sum_{a, j \ne i} P_j P_a T_{aiji},\\ T_2 & = \sum_{a, b, c, j \ne i} P_j P_a P_b P_c T_{cjab} . \end{array}\)

The indices in the last expression are grouped as the pairs \((c, j)\) and \((a, b)\). Symmetry in and between the pairs, and \(Q_{bcd} = \sum_a P_a T_{abcd}\), give

\(\displaystyle T_1 = P_i^2 T_0 + C_1, \qquad T_2 = P_i^2 T_1 + C_2 .\) (17.66)

More explicitly,

\(\displaystyle C_1 = \sum_{j \ne i} P_j Q_{iji} - P_i Q_{iii}, \qquad C_2 = \sum_{j, a, b \ne i} P_j P_a P_b Q_{jab} - P_i \sum_{j, a \ne i} P_j P_a Q_{aji} .\)

Expand the two divergence sums into their \(i\) term and their remaining terms to verify these identities; commuting the frequency multipliers and exchanging the tensor pairs identifies the displayed \(T_1, T_2\). When all four widths are at most \(e^{an}\), the contact bounds above give exponents \(4 a\) and \(6 a\) for \(C_1, C_2\), respectively. They also show that \(C_1\) vanishes if the \(i\)-profile has zero moments through order \(D + 1\), and \(C_2\) if it has zero moments through order \(D + 3\): multiplication shifts the required moment order by at most one or three.

Fix \(\delta > 0\), \(a \leqslant 0\), and three even, nonnegative, smooth compactly supported transverse profiles \(f_j\), \(j \ne i\). Keep their shapes fixed, with width exponents \(w_j \leqslant a\). The fixed-band result, with the \(i\)-profile fixed away from zero, gives exponents

\(\displaystyle \delta, \qquad \delta + 2 a, \qquad \delta + 4 a\)

for \(T_0, T_1, T_2\): their respective sums contain zero, two and four transverse frequency multipliers. The parity hypotheses hold because each multiplier flips the sign in its coordinate, cancelling the corresponding stress index sign.

Let \(w = e^{an}\) and \(\varphi_k (p) = p^{- 2 k} \varphi (p)\) as in the previous section. Write \(h_{p, w, k} = (\varphi_k)_{pw}\), for \(p = 1, 2\), and define

\(\displaystyle A_{p, k} (n) = w^{- 2 k} \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} T_k (f_1^{(n)}, \ldots, h_{p, w, k}, \ldots, f_4^{(n)}), \qquad k = 0, 1, 2.\)

The temporal slot is \(i\). Since \(p_i^2 h_{p, w, k + 1} = (pw)^2 h_{p, w, k}\), (17.66) implies that both

\(\displaystyle A_{p, 1} - p^2 A_{p, 0}, \qquad A_{p, 2} - p^2 A_{p, 1}\)

have exponent \(2 a\), hence exponent \(x = \delta + 2 a\). Here the normalized first defect costs \(w^{- 2} e^{4 an}\), and the second costs \(w^{- 4} e^{6 an}\), making the exponent explicit.

Choose one subtraction order \(m\) greater than all the finitely many moment orders just used. Replace \(h_{1, w, k} - h_{2, w, k}\) by its moment-subtracted difference at scale \(8\), and call its normalized real response \(F_k\). The correction coefficients are differences of the moments at widths \(w\) and \(2 w\). Their constant moments cancel. Each remaining even moment has order at least two, so its coefficient has exponent at most \(2 a\). There are finitely many fixed correction profiles, all away from zero. Their fixed-band costs are \(\delta + 2 ka\) for \(T_k\), and the normalization \(w^{- 2 k}\) removes the latter \(2 ka\). It follows that

\(\displaystyle F_k - (A_{1, k} - A_{2, k}) = O (e^{xn}), \qquad k = 0, 1, 2.\)

No subtraction order is sent to infinity.

The three signs are essential. Take the actual reflection-even cluster representing \(J_{ii}\) and its positive spectral measure. For a sufficiently moment-zero temporal profile, the extension agrees with the ordinary spectral integral. The contact terms in (17.66) vanish there; multiplication by \(p_i^2\) inside the profile becomes multiplication by \(- E^2\) in its Laplace response, by twice integrating by parts (the zero moments remove the boundary terms). Thus, writing \(\Gamma_{m, w, k} (E)\) for the nonnegative dyadic response from the previous section,

\(\displaystyle F_k = \tau w^{- 2 k} \int (- E^2)^k \Gamma_{m, w, k} (E) \prod_{j \ne i} (f_j)_{e^{w_j n}} (p_j) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_i^{J_{ii}} (p),\)

where \(\tau > 0\) is the fixed Fourier normalization. Hence \(F_0 \geqslant 0\), \(F_1 \leqslant 0\) and \(F_2 \geqslant 0\). The four conservation defects and these three correction defects are exactly the seven errors in the algebraic estimate of Subsection 17.6. It gives \(F_0 = O (e^{(\delta + 2 a) n})\). The positive dyadic kernel lower bound and multiplication by \(w^2\) now prove (17.64). In particular the choice \(a = - 99\), \(\delta = 1\) gives exponent \(- 395\) for the actual diagonal-stress measure, as required by the exterior packet.

17.8Analyticity along extreme-point motions

To continue a Ward identity away from a line, we need analyticity along certain one-coordinate motions. Smoothness of the hierarchy does not suffice. We derive the required analyticity from reflection positivity, positive-time heat evolution, and the collinear angular identities. The argument uses only finite-dimensional exponentials of individual spin operators; an action of the full rotation group is not an input.

Let \(S\) be the smooth, translation-invariant, reflection-positive hierarchy already constructed, with finite-dimensional filtered field spaces \(E_d\). Write \(D_b : E_d \to E_{d + 1}\) for its derivative operators and \(T_d (K) : E_d \to E_d\) for the angular operators, real-linear in a skew matrix \(K\) and complex-linear in the field. Assume the derivative insertion identities, the collinear angular Ward identities at every grade and arity, and the commutation rule

\(\displaystyle T_{d + 1} (K) D_b v = D_b T_d (K) v + \sum_c K_{cb} D_c v.\) (17.67)

Signed coordinate symmetries, including reflection of the field labels, are the ones already present in the hierarchy.

Fix a coordinate axis \(a\). Its reflection-positive Hilbert space is \(\mathcal{H}_a\). For a field \(v\) and a point \(x\) with \(x_a > 0\), let \(\Psi_v (x)\) be the vector represented by the singleton insertion there. Denote the reflected label by \(v^{\dagger_a}\) and the coordinate reflection by \(\vartheta_a\). If

\(\displaystyle F_{v, w} (x) = S (v \text{ at } x, w \text{ at } 0),\)

the exact Gram identity is

\(\displaystyle \langle \Psi_v (x), \Psi_w (y) \rangle = F_{w, v^{\dagger_a}} (y - \vartheta_a x) .\) (17.68)

The inner product is linear in its second variable. Translation invariance and the permutation symmetry of the hierarchy prove this identity directly from the reflected-word definition of the Hilbert space.

17.8.1Smooth singleton vectors and the positive radial line

For \(b \ne a\), the curve \(t \mapsto \Psi_v (x + te_b)\) is continuous by the strongly continuous spatial translations on \(\mathcal{H}_a\). Its derivative is \(\Psi_{D_b v} (x + te_b)\). To justify this as a Hilbert-space derivative, first pair with any reflected-word vector. The scalar pairing is a smooth separated-point moment, whose derivative is the stated derivative insertion. The proposed derivative curve is itself continuous by spatial translation. Its Bochner integral from \(0\) to \(t\) therefore has, against every word vector, the same pairing as \(\Psi_v (x + te_b) - \Psi_v (x)\). Density of word vectors identifies these vectors. The fundamental theorem of calculus now gives the strong derivative. Iterating this argument with \(D_b v, D_b^2 v, \ldots\) proves smoothness of the entire curve.

In the normal direction there is more: a singleton vector is analytic at every positive normal coordinate. Choose an earlier strictly positive normal coordinate. Nearby vectors are obtained from that earlier vector by the heat semigroup at a strictly positive time. The holomorphic positive-time extension of Subsection 6.1 proves the claim. Pairing with a fixed singleton and using (17.68) consequently shows that

\(\displaystyle t \longmapsto F_{v, w} (te_a) \quad \text{is real analytic for } t > 0.\) (17.69)

For example, near a specified \(t_0 > 0\), place the fixed reflected singleton at \(t_0 e_a / 2\) and the variable singleton at \((t - t_0 / 2) e_a\). Their separation is \(te_a\) and both lie in the positive half-space.

17.8.2An analytic comparison with the same transverse jets

Fix \(b \ne a\) and the plane generator \(K\) with \(Ke_a = e_b\), \(Ke_b = - e_a\). For \(u > 0\) set

\(\displaystyle \rho (u, z) = \sqrt{u^2 + z^2}, \qquad \theta (u, z) = \arctan (z / u),\)
\(\displaystyle Q_{v, w} (u, z) = F_{e^{- \theta (u, z) T (K)} v, \hspace{0.17em} e^{- \theta (u, z) T (K)} w} (\rho (u, z) e_a) .\)

The exponentials act in the respective finite-dimensional field spaces. Expand both labels in finite bases. Each summand is a product of analytic matrix coefficients and a fixed radial moment from (17.69), composed with the positive analytic function \(\rho\). Thus \(Q_{v, w}\) is real analytic on \(u > 0\).

Define \(\mathcal{R}= u \partial_z - z \partial_u\). Direct differentiation gives \(\mathcal{R} \theta = 1\) and \(\mathcal{R} \rho = 0\). Consequently

\(\displaystyle Q_{T (K) v, w} + Q_{v, T (K) w} +\mathcal{R}Q_{v, w} = 0.\) (17.70)

Also \(Q_{v, w} (u, 0) = F_{v, w} (ue_a)\). Let \(G_{v, w} (u, z) = F_{v, w} (ue_a + ze_b)\) be the actual smooth two-point function. Its corresponding expression \(G_{T (K) v, w} + G_{v, T (K) w} +\mathcal{R}G_{v, w}\) is the angular Ward expression for these two insertions.

Every transverse derivative of that Ward expression vanishes at \((u, 0)\). Here is the reason this follows from the collinear identity for all labels. Differentiate the angular expression in one insertion coordinate. The new derivative of its linear spatial coefficient contributes \(\sum_c K_{cb}\) times the corresponding derivative insertion. Combining this term with the differentiated spin insertion, using (17.67), gives exactly the angular expression with that label replaced by \(D_b v\). All other terms are just differentiated moment rows. Thus repeated coordinate differentiation only raises labels. The assumed collinear identity applies to every resulting word. In particular all \(\partial_z^n\) of the two-point angular expression vanish at every \((u, 0)\) with \(u > 0\).

We show that \(G\) and \(Q\) have the same transverse jets, without assuming that \(G\) is analytic. Put \(H = G - Q\), allowing every pair of labels in this notation. Then \(H_{v, w} (u, 0) = 0\) and all transverse jets of its angular expression vanish. The elementary commutation formula is

\(\displaystyle \partial_z^n (\mathcal{R}H) =\mathcal{R} \partial_z^n H - n \partial_u \partial_z^{n - 1} H \qquad (n \geqslant 1) .\)

Assume inductively that all transverse jets through order \(n\) of every \(H_{v, w}\) vanish along the positive \(u\)-axis. Apply \(\partial_z^n\) to its angular expression and set \(z = 0\). Both spin terms vanish by the induction hypothesis. The last term in the displayed formula also vanishes, because the preceding transverse jet is identically zero as a function of \(u\). We obtain \(u \partial_z^{n + 1} H_{v, w} (u, 0) = 0\). For \(n = 0\) the same conclusion follows directly without the last term. Since \(u > 0\), induction proves

\(\displaystyle \partial_z^n G_{v, w} (u, 0) = \partial_z^n Q_{v, w} (u, 0) \qquad (n \geqslant 0) .\) (17.71)

This equality of jets alone is not an analytic continuation statement. The next Hilbert-space estimate supplies the required convergence.

17.8.3From scalar jets to a Hilbert analytic curve

We use a useful elementary fact. Suppose \(f : \mathbb{R} \to \mathcal{H}\) is smooth and

\(\displaystyle \langle f (s), f (t) \rangle = A (t - s) .\)

Suppose the derivatives of \(A\) at zero agree with those of a scalar function analytic at zero. The power series of that function gives constants \(C, R > 0\) with \(|A^{(n)} (0) | \leqslant CR^{- n} n\)! for every \(n\). Differentiating the Gram identity in both variables yields

\(\displaystyle \langle f^{(m)} (s), f^{(n)} (t) \rangle = (- 1)^m A^{(m + n)} (t - s) .\)

In particular, uniformly for all real \(t\),

\(\displaystyle \|f^{(n)} (t)\|^2 = |A^{(2 n)} (0) | \leqslant CR^{- 2 n} (2 n) ! \leqslant C (2 / R)^{2 n} (n!)^2 .\)

The last inequality uses \(\binom{2 n}{n} \leqslant 2^{2 n}\). Taylor's integral remainder in the Hilbert space is therefore at most \(\sqrt{C} (2| h| / R)^{N + 1}\) after order \(N\). It tends to zero for \(|h| < R / 2\). The Taylor series converges in norm and equals \(f\) there, proving real analyticity at every point of the line.

Apply this fact to \(f (t) = \Psi_v (x + te_b)\) for \(x_a > 0\) and \(b \ne a\). By (17.68),

\(\displaystyle A (t - s) = F_{v, v^{\dagger_a}} (2 x_a e_a + (t - s) e_b) .\)

The smoothness was proved above. The analytic comparator is \(z \mapsto Q_{v, v^{\dagger_a}} (2 x_a, z)\), and (17.71) gives precisely the required derivatives at zero. Hence the singleton trajectory is analytic along every transverse coordinate, as well as along the positive normal coordinate. Stationarity of the Gram kernel is what makes the factorial bound uniform along the whole transverse line.

17.8.4Moving an extreme insertion in an arbitrary word

Consider a configuration of distinct points and one insertion whose \(a\)-coordinate is strictly larger than those of all other insertions. Choose a separating hyperplane \(x_a = c\) strictly between them. Translate it to zero and reflect the fixed spectator word into the positive half-space. Reflection positivity then represents the moment, locally in any coordinate motion of the extreme insertion, as

\(\displaystyle \langle \Phi, \Psi_v (x + te_b) \rangle\)

with a fixed word vector \(\Phi\). Strict separation persists in a neighborhood of the chosen parameter. If \(b = a\), positive-time analyticity applies; if \(b \ne a\), use the transverse result just proved. Applying the continuous linear functional \(\langle \Phi, \cdot \rangle\) proves analyticity of the moment row. Permutation symmetry moves any selected slot to the first slot, so no restriction on its position in the word is needed. Spectators may have equal \(a\)-coordinates, provided their points remain distinct.

For an insertion strictly smaller in coordinate \(a\), use the hierarchy's actual signed reflection in that axis, with its reflected labels. It turns the insertion into a strict maximum. A motion in coordinate \(a\) changes parameter \(t\) to \(- t\); a transverse motion leaves \(t\) unchanged. Both substitutions preserve analyticity. This reflection uses the already constructed signed symmetry, not a rotation action yet to be proved.

Every angular Ward expression is a finite sum of such moment rows, with labels either turned or raised once and coefficients affine in the moving coordinate. It is therefore analytic wherever the moving point is a strict extremum in some fixed coordinate. Along a closed segment with this property at every point, these neighborhoods cover the segment. This is the precise segment analyticity needed for the one-dimensional identity theorem in the global Ward propagation argument.

17.9From the collinear stress identity to global Ward identities

This section records the finite-configuration argument which turns a collinear rotation identity into a Ward identity at every separated configuration. It also records the two finite-level bridges needed later: the outer stress estimate supplies the collinear identity, and erasure of the internal Ward kernel turns the differentiated Ward identity into the Lie relations. All statements are at fixed finite insertion number and fixed field grades; no coincidence limit is taken.

Let \(\mathscr{S}\) be a Euclidean hierarchy. Write \(\mathcal{F}_d\) for its complex field space at grade \(d\), \(\mathcal{P}_d : \mathcal{F}_d \to \mathcal{F}_{d + 1}\) for the positional derivative, and \(\mathcal{Y}_d (K) : \mathcal{F}_d \to \mathcal{F}_d\) for a spin-row family indexed real-linearly by a skew matrix \(K \in \mathfrak{so} (4)\). A label at a slot is a pair \((d, v)\) with \(v \in \mathcal{F}_d\). For a word \(q = (q_1, \ldots, q_n)\) and a separated tuple \(x = (x_1, \ldots, x_n)\), let \(S (q ; x)\) denote its Euclidean moment. The hierarchy's derivative rule says that differentiation in the coordinate \(b\) of \(x_i\) replaces \(q_i = (d_i, v_i)\) by \((d_i + 1, \mathcal{P}_{d_i, b} v_i)\).

Define the angular Ward expression by

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \mathcal{A}_K (q ; x) = \sum_{i = 1}^n ( & S (q [i \mapsto (d_i, \mathcal{Y}_{d_i} (K) v_i)] ; x) \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.72)}\\ & \left. + \sum_{b = 1}^4 (Kx_i)_b \hspace{0.17em} S (q [i \mapsto (d_i + 1, \mathcal{P}_{d_i, b} v_i)] ; x) \right) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.73)} \end{array}\)

Here \(q [i \mapsto \cdot]\) changes only the indicated slot. The condition Still at \(x\) means \(\mathcal{A}_K (q ; x) = 0\) for every word \(q\) and every \(K\). The condition collinear means this identity holds whenever the points lie on one coordinate axis \(\mathbb{R}e_a\) through the origin and remain pairwise distinct. This axis formulation is sufficient below. In fact the angular expression is invariant under simultaneous translation. For a common shift \(h\), translation invariance of every moment, including those with a changed label, and the positional derivative rule give

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{A}_K (q ; x + h) -\mathcal{A}_K (q ; x) = \sum_{b = 1}^4 (Kh)_b \sum_{i = 1}^n \partial_{x_{i, b}} S (q ; x) = 0.\)

The last equality follows by differentiating \(S (q ; x + se_b) = S (q ; x)\) at \(s = 0\), with the same shift applied to every point. Hence the axis formulation also gives the identity on every line parallel to that axis. The compatibility relation between position and spin derivatives is

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{P}_{d, b} \mathcal{Y}_d (K) v + \sum_{c = 1}^4 K_{cb} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_{d, c} v =\mathcal{Y}_{d + 1} (K) \mathcal{P}_{d, b} v. \) (17.74)

The signs in this identity use the convention in (17.73).

Theorem 17.25. (Propagation from a line) Assume the hierarchy is smooth on the configuration space

\(\displaystyle \text{Conf}_n = \{(x_1, \ldots, x_n) : x_i \ne x_j \text{ for } i \ne j\},\)

the spin rows obey (17.74), and the angular expression (17.73) is zero at every separated collinear configuration. Assume also the extreme-segment analyticity established in Subsection 17.8: on every coordinate segment used below, the angular expression for each fixed word and generator is real analytic on a neighborhood of the segment. Then it is zero at every \(x \in \text{Conf}_n\).

Proof. We first establish a geometric reduction. Fix a coordinate axis \(a\) and another axis \(b \ne a\). Call a tuple \(a\)-ordered if the real numbers \(x_i \cdot e_a\) are pairwise distinct. Such tuples are dense in \(\text{Conf}_n\): perturb the \(a\)-coordinate of point \(i\) by \(ti\). For each pair \(i \ne j\), equality after perturbation can occur for at most one value of \(t\). Since there are finitely many pairs, there are arbitrarily small \(t\) avoiding all these exceptional values. The perturbed tuple converges to the original one and remains in the open set \(\text{Conf}_n\) for small enough \(t\).

Every \(a\)-ordered tuple can be moved to a collinear tuple by a finite chain of single-point coordinate segments which stay in \(\text{Conf}_n\). Here are the construction and the separation check. Let \(I\) be the set of points not yet on the \(a\)-axis, maintaining the invariant that points already aligned lie beyond every remaining point in the \(a\)-coordinate. Initially \(I\) is the whole set, so the invariant is vacuous. Choose \(i \in I\) with smallest \(a\)-coordinate. First move point \(i\) in coordinate \(b\) far enough in the positive direction that it is the unique largest \(b\)-coordinate. During this first segment it stays the unique smallest \(a\)-coordinate, since its \(a\)-coordinate is fixed. Then move it in coordinate \(a\) far enough to be the unique largest \(a\)-coordinate. During this second segment it stays the unique largest \(b\)-coordinate, so its \(b\)-coordinate does not meet another point's. Now, one at a time, set each of its other coordinates to zero. These moves leave its \(a\)-coordinate fixed and it remains the unique largest in \(a\). Every intermediate configuration is therefore separated. The point has now reached the \(a\)-axis without moving any other point, and it lies beyond every point still in \(I\). Repeat with the remaining finite set \(I\). This terminates after at most \(n\) stages and gives the required finite chain. The motion consists of finitely many segments; the segment parameter interval can be oriented in either direction.

It remains to show the Ward identity is constant along each such segment. Fix a segment moving point \(i\) in coordinate \(b\), with parameter \(s\) in a closed interval \([u, v]\). By construction, throughout this interval point \(i\) is either the strict minimum or the strict maximum in some coordinate \(a\); the choice may switch between segments, but not within this one. For each fixed word \(q\) and skew matrix \(K\), the scalar function

\(\displaystyle s \longmapsto \mathcal{A}_K (q ; x (s))\)

is real analytic on a neighborhood of the interval by the stated extreme-segment analyticity input. This input applies to moment rows with the moving point at either end and to every raised row appearing in the angular expression. It is precisely the result of Subsection 17.8; smoothness on \(\text{Conf}_n\) alone is not enough.

At an endpoint where Still holds, all derivatives of this analytic scalar function vanish. For order zero this is the assumed Ward identity. For higher orders, differentiate the identity along the segment and use (17.74): differentiating the spin substitution at the moving slot gives the spin substitution of the raised positional derivative; the derivative of the orbital coefficient supplies the matrix term \(\sum_c K_{cb} \mathcal{P}_{d, c}\); these terms combine exactly into the raised-slot term in the angular identity. Induction therefore identifies the \(r\)th derivative with the Ward expression for the word with that slot raised \(r\) times. It is zero at the endpoint. A real-analytic function on a connected interval whose derivatives of every order vanish at one point vanishes on the interval: its convergent Taylor series is zero near that point, and the identity theorem propagates the equality across the interval. Thus Still transfers from the final endpoint of a segment to its initial endpoint. Applying this successively along the finite chain proves Still at every \(a\)-ordered tuple. Finally, each angular expression is continuous on \(\text{Conf}_n\) by smoothness of the hierarchy there. Density of the ordered tuples proves it vanishes at the original arbitrary separated tuple. This also covers \(n = 0, 1\), where there are no pairwise collision constraints and the sums are empty or single-term.\(\Box\)

The collinear identity from the stress tensor.
The preceding theorem needs no stress tensor once its collinear hypothesis is available. In the application, let \(J_{ab}\) be a symmetric conserved stress insertion, let \(p_{d, b}\) be the local positional response, and let \(Y_d (K)\) be the spin response. The stress-law package consists of the conservation law, the symmetric raw stress identity, and the flux identity that identifies the infinitesimal rotation of a moment with the integral of the stress flux; its precise form is (S1)–(S3) before Lemma 17.27. The flux argument uses the compactly supported test described below; it does not assume that a noncompact rotation field is admissible.

Until the scalar comparison \(p = c\mathcal{P}\) is proved, write \(\mathcal{A}_K (p, Y ; q ; x)\) for the expression (17.73) with \(p, Y\) substituted for \(\mathcal{P}, \mathcal{Y}\). The compact stress argument below proves its vanishing. It does not yet identify \(p\) with a positional derivative.

The following paragraph is motivation only; the test actually used is the growing anisotropic petal described after the estimates. For fixed separated collinear \(x\), one might choose smooth compactly supported windows \(\chi_i\) around each point, equal to one near that point and with supports avoiding all other insertions. For a plane rotation in axes \(a, b\), the packet

\(\displaystyle F_c (y) = \sum_i \chi_i (y) (Ky)_c\)

is smooth, compactly supported, and agrees with the rotational vector field near each insertion. The flux identity therefore evaluates the angular expression as minus the integral of its stress-flux density. In that density the term \(\chi_i \hspace{0.17em} \partial_a (Ky)_b\) cancels after contraction against the symmetric stress, because \(K\) is skew. The terms \((\partial_a \chi_i) (Ky)_b\) do not cancel: they are the shell integrand. The estimate proved below is not for such fixed windows. It is for a petal centred at the origin, of widths \(e^{100 n}, e^{150 n}, e^{300 n}\), whose shell lies far from all insertions.

Here is the rate calculation behind the stress-packet estimate. For a sequence of scalar or finite-dimensional vector quantities \(F_n\), write \(F = O_r\) when \(\|F_n \| \leqslant Ce^{rn}\) for all sufficiently large \(n\), with \(C\) independent of \(n\). The exact choice of the fixed axes, word, and positions may affect \(C\). A positive spectral pairing satisfies the following elementary interpolation rule: if \(X_n, Y_n \geqslant 0\) and

\(\displaystyle |F_n (p) |^2 \leqslant (Me^{tn})^2 X_n (p) Y_n (p), \qquad \int X_n \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_B = O_x, \qquad \int Y_n \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_A = O_y,\)

then Cauchy–Schwarz gives

\(\displaystyle | \langle F_n \rangle_{B, A} | \leqslant M (C_X C_Y)^{1 / 2} e^{(t + (x + y) / 2) n}, \qquad \text{so } \quad \langle F_n \rangle_{B, A} = O_{t + (x + y) / 2} . \) (17.75)

Here \(C_X, C_Y\) are the constants in the respective integral bounds. This rule applies to the cross-spectral pairing after its positive supports and integrability have been verified.

For the packet under consideration the anisotropic scale exponents are

\(\displaystyle w_j = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} - 100, & j = a \text{ or } j = b,\\ - 150, & j = l \text{ and } j \not\in \{a, b\},\\ - 300, & \text{otherwise}, \end{array} \right. \qquad u = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 1, & d = l,\\ - w_l, & d \ne l. \end{array} \right.\)

They obey \(- 300 \leqslant w_j \leqslant - 100\), \(w_d < 0\), and \(u \leqslant 150\). The nonnegative spectral measures are supported on \(p_d \geqslant 0\). The cove is the set where \(p_d \leqslant 1\) and every transverse coordinate satisfies \(|p_j | \leqslant e^{(1 + w_j) n}\). Let \(h\) be the fixed nonnegative, smooth, even one-dimensional cutoff which equals one on \([- 1, 1]\) and is supported in \([- 2, 2]\). The transverse panel uses the normalized dilation

\(\displaystyle h_{j, n} (s) = e^{- (1 + w_j) n} h (se^{- (1 + w_j) n}), \qquad H_n (p) = \prod_{j \ne d} h_{j, n} (p_j) .\)

Thus \(H_n\) includes the normalization factors of the three transverse one-dimensional dilations; they are part of both bound weights below. The packet estimate is for pairwise distinct axes \(a, b\) and for \(d \ne a\), as in the stress-packet construction. Denote by \(D_{r, n}\) the positive longitudinal cost

\(\displaystyle D_{r, n} (p) =\boldsymbol{1}_{p_d \leqslant 1} \hspace{0.17em} (p_d + e^{rn})^{- 1} H_n (p),\)

Denote by \(Z_{r, n}\) its cone-weighted variant

\(\displaystyle Z_{r, n} (p) =\boldsymbol{1}_{p_d \leqslant 1} \frac{p_d e^{4 rn}}{(p_d + e^{rn})^4} H_n (p) .\)

The following are the precise estimates needed from the collinear spectral estimates. The positive line and point bounds are supplied by Subsection 17.6; the cone-weighted bound is proved by the fixed-band stress-conservation argument in Subsection 17.7. In these notations the required bounds are

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} \int D_{- 3000, n} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_B & = O_u, & \int Z_{- 99, n} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_A & = O_{- 395}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.76)} \\ \int D_{- 3000, n} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_B & = O_u, & \int D_{0, n} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_A & = O_{900}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.77)} \\ \int D_{- 3000, n} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_B & = O_u, & \int D_{w_d, n} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_A & = O_1 . \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.78)} \end{array}\)

Each bound is uniform in \(n\) for a fixed packet. The exterior Fourier estimate supplies \(O_{- 101}\) on the complement of the low cove by the damped exterior estimate, not by assuming an undamped spectral moment. The distinction between the two bound measures in each display is essential: the first controls the collinear line cost, while the second controls the packet amplitude.

The two bound inputs carry sign hypotheses, which we now check. The line bound (17.62) for the collinear cluster \(B\) requires both reflection signs among the three axes other than \(l\), and an axis among them whose sign is opposite to that of \(l\). The point bound (17.60) for \(A\), with \(Q\) all four axes, requires both signs among all four axes. In the stress decomposition below, \(B\) is the matching sign pattern of \(J_{ab}\) with \(a \ne b\): it is odd exactly under the reflections of the axes \(a\) and \(b\). The cluster \(A = J_{db}\) with \(d \ne b\) is odd exactly under the reflections of \(d\) and \(b\). For \(B\): since \(a \ne b\), at least one of \(a, b\) differs from \(l\), which gives an odd axis other than \(l\); and \(\{a, b, l\}\) has at most three elements, which gives an even axis other than \(l\). If \(l \in \{a, b\}\) then \(l\) is odd and the even axis just found has the opposite sign; otherwise \(l\) is even and the odd axis has the opposite sign. For \(A\): the axes \(d, b\) are odd and the two remaining axes are even. Hence both bound hypotheses hold.

The one-dimensional panel profile \(\varphi_d\) in the test has Fourier gap \(\hat{\varphi}_d (\xi) \ne 0 \Rightarrow | \xi | \geqslant 1\). This is a property of the panel profile, not a claim that the compactly supported spatial petal has a Fourier gap. On the cove, the frozen Fourier estimate for that panel gives the differentiated-packet bound

\(\displaystyle |F_n (p) | \leqslant Me^{3 n} H_n (p) \exp (- p_d e^{- w_d n}), \qquad p_d \geqslant 0. \) (17.79)

To derive it, use the exterior computation of Subsection 17.6. For \(p_d \geqslant 0\) the temporal factor is at most

\(\displaystyle \| \hat{\varphi}_d \|_1 \exp (- p_d / e^{w_d n}),\)

and each transverse factor has the form \(e^{- w_j n} f_j (p_j / e^{w_j n})\) with a fixed bounded profile \(f_j\), as in that section, so its modulus is at most \(\|f_j \|_{\infty} e^{- w_j n}\). On the cove \(|p_j | e^{- (1 + w_j) n} \leqslant 1\), so \(h_{j, n} (p_j) = e^{- (1 + w_j) n}\) and

\(\displaystyle \|f_j \|_{\infty} e^{- w_j n} = \|f_j \|_{\infty} \hspace{0.17em} e^n h_{j, n} (p_j) .\)

The product of the three transverse factors gives \(e^{3 n} H_n (p)\).

On the cove with \(d = b\), split at the longitudinal level \(p_d = e^{- 3000 n}\). On the region \(p_d \geqslant e^{- 3000 n}\), the elementary inequality

\(\displaystyle e^{- t / s} \leqslant 4 \left( \frac{s}{t + s} \right)^2 \quad (t \geqslant 0, 0 < s \leqslant v)\)

with \(s = e^{w_d n}\) and \(v = e^{- 99 n}\) first gives the square-tail factor \(16 (v / (p_d + v))^4\). On this region, put \(R = e^{- 3000 n}\). Since \(R \leqslant p_d \leqslant 1\), we have \(1 \leqslant 4 p_d / (p_d + R)\), and therefore

\(\displaystyle |F_n |^2 \leqslant (M' e^{3 n})^2 D_{- 3000, n} Z_{- 99, n} .\)

The factor \(4 p_d / (p_d + R)\) pays for replacing the packet's denominator at scale \(e^{- 99 n}\) by the longitudinal cost at scale \(e^{- 3000 n}\). The costs in (17.76) and (17.75) give exponent

\(\displaystyle 3 + \frac{u - 395}{2} \leqslant 3 + \frac{150 - 395}{2} = - 119.5.\)

On the other cove region, \(0 \leqslant p_d < e^{- 3000 n}\), the exponential in (17.79) is at most one. The packet square is therefore bounded with amplitude exponent \(3 - 1500 = - 1497\) against \(D_{- 3000, n} D_{0, n}\). The shallow bounds yield

\(\displaystyle - 1497 + \frac{u + 900}{2} \leqslant - 972.\)

Both estimates are stronger than \(O_{- 101}\).

If \(d \ne b\), the shear estimate bounds the squared packet by an amplitude with exponent \(3 + w_d\), against \(D_{- 3000, n} D_{w_d, n}\). It follows from (17.79): with \(\tau = p_d e^{- w_d n}\) one has \((1 + \tau)^2 e^{- 2 \tau} \leqslant 1\), so \(e^{- 2 \tau} \leqslant (1 + \tau)^{- 2} = e^{2 w_d n} (p_d + e^{w_d n})^{- 2}\); and since \(e^{- 3000 n} \leqslant e^{w_d n}\), \((p_d + e^{- 3000 n}) (p_d + e^{w_d n}) \leqslant (p_d + e^{w_d n})^2\). Together,

\(\displaystyle |F_n |^2 \leqslant M^2 e^{2 (3 + w_d) n} \hspace{0.17em} \frac{H_n^2}{(p_d + e^{w_d n})^2} \leqslant M^2 e^{2 (3 + w_d) n} \hspace{0.17em} D_{- 3000, n} D_{w_d, n}\)

on the cove. When \(d = l\), \(w_d = - 150\) and \(u = 1\), so (17.75) gives \(- 150 + 3 + (1 + 1) / 2 = - 146\). When \(d \ne l\), \(w_d = - 300\) and \(u \leqslant 150\), so the exponent is at most

\(\displaystyle 3 - 300 + \frac{150 + 1}{2} = - 221.5.\)

The cove is partitioned by these two cases, and the complement has the independent damped Fourier-tail bound \(O_{- 101}\). For clarity, outside the cove there are exactly two alternatives. If \(p_d > 1\), the gap in the panel Fourier support and the positive-time damping estimate give

\(\displaystyle e^{- p_d e^{- w_d n}} \leqslant e^{yn} e^{- Rp_d}\)

eventually, for any fixed \(R > 0\) and prescribed \(y\). Take \(y = - 1001\); the transverse readout contributes at most \(e^{900 n}\), leaving rate \(- 101\). The positive-time damped spectral moment is supplied by the exterior-estimate; no undamped zeroth moment is assumed. If instead \(p_d \leqslant 1\) and a transverse scatter bound fails, then for some \(j \ne d\), \(|p_j | > e^{(1 + w_j) n}\). The Schwartz bound of order \(k\) in that coordinate contributes the factor \(e^{- kn}\), while the order-zero bounds for the two other transverse coordinates and the ordinary bound in the failed coordinate contribute at most \(e^{900 n}\). Choose \(k > 1001\); the corresponding positive-time polynomially weighted estimate is again the exterior-bound input. These two cases give the unamplified matching cluster readout the required bound \(O_{- 101}\) under that input.

The cross-slice step needs absolute integrability on both sides. The weighted line/exterior bounds and the positive-time damped spectral moment first give the spectral-side integrability. On the position side, place the two fixed clusters at a common positive-time separation \(u\). The panel's temporal Fourier gap is at least \(e^{- w_d n}\), which is eventually greater than \(2 u\) with a strict margin. The damped moment estimate, together with the Schwartz bounds in the transverse coordinates, then gives absolute integrability and justifies Fubini. This is the gap estimate in the cross-slice input; weighted spectral Cauchy by itself would not prove position-side integrability. With these integrability hypotheses in place, the cross-slice identity gives, for a fixed sign \(\epsilon \in \{- 1, 1\}\) and fixed \(\tau > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \epsilon \int R_n (p) \hspace{0.17em} dp = \tau \hspace{0.17em} \text{cross} (F_n),\)

where \(R_n\) is the unamplified cluster readout times the position test and \(F_n\) is its slice packet. Thus the cove and exterior estimates imply \(\int R_n = O_{- 101}\). The actual insertion packet has an additional exact scalar factor \(e^{100 n}\): the profile identity is \(\text{packet}_n = e^{100 n} \hspace{0.17em} \text{readout}_n\). Consequently its integral has bound

\(\displaystyle |B_{a, b, l, d} (n) | \leqslant Ce^{100 n} e^{- 101 n} = Ce^{- n} . \) (17.80)

To pass from this packet bound to the stress insertion, first represent the chosen stress component by its finite signed cluster expansion. Decompose the other insertion labels into their finitely many sign patterns. A coordinate reflection changes the sign of each nonmatching pattern while preserving measure, so its integral is zero. For the matching pattern, the exact derivative-profile identity is \(\partial_d \text{petal}_n = e^{100 n} \text{positionTest}_n\); thus its unamplified readout has bound \(O_{- 101}\) and the actual stress packet has bound \(O_{100 - 101} = O_{- 1}\). Finally, the finite sum over patterns, axes, and insertions preserves \(O_{- 1}\), and the shell flux tends to zero.

For the plane with generator normalized by \(Ke_a = e_b\) and \(Ke_b = - e_a\), use the compact vector test with components

\(\displaystyle F_b^{(n)} (y) = \text{petal} (a, b, l, n) (y), \qquad F_a^{(n)} (y) = - \text{petal} (b, a, l, n) (y), \qquad F_c^{(n)} (y) = 0 \quad (c \not\in \{a, b\}) .\)

These tests do more than converge to the orbital field near the insertions: for every fixed point \(y_0\) there is \(n_0\) such that for \(n \geqslant n_0\) one has \(\text{petal} (a, b, l, n) (y) = y_a\) for all \(y\) in a neighborhood of \(y_0\). Indeed \(\text{petal} (a, b, l, n) (y) = y_a\) whenever every normalized coordinate \(e^{w_i n} y_i\) has modulus less than one, and these normalized coordinates tend to zero locally uniformly because every \(w_i < 0\). Hence, for all large \(n\), \(F_b^{(n)} (y) = y_a\) and \(F_a^{(n)} (y) = - y_b\) on a neighborhood of each of the finitely many insertions: they coincide there with the two components of the orbital field, which is what the compact flux identity requires. The second component carries the minus sign; it is not an additional sign in the stress contraction. By stress symmetry, its shell integral and the first component combine as

\(\displaystyle \int \text{river} (F^{(n)}) = \int \text{draught}_{a, b}^{(n)} - \int \text{draught}_{b, a}^{(n)} .\)

Each draught is a finite sum of the already estimated shell terms, hence has bound \(Ce^{- n}\). The compact-flux identity identifies the negative of this integral with the local plane-rotation response. That response is independent of \(n\), so its absolute value is at most \(Ce^{- n}\) for all sufficiently large \(n\) and therefore is zero. The generators of coordinate planes span \(\mathfrak{so} (4)\), giving the collinear identity for every skew generator. The skew matrices \(E_{ab} - E_{ba}\) form a real basis of \(\mathfrak{so} (4)\), and the angular response is real-linear in its generator, so it vanishes for every \(K\). This proves the collinear input used above. The exponential estimate is the substantive analytic input here; it is not a consequence of the geometric propagation argument.

Lemma 17.26. (Outer translation identity) Under the same stress-law assumptions, the vector positional response also satisfies the following line identity. If \(x_1, \ldots, x_n\) lie on the axis \(b\), \(j \ne b\), and \(v_i \in \mathcal{F}_d\), then

\(\displaystyle \sum_{i = 1}^n S (q [i \mapsto (d + 1, p_{d, j} v_i)] ; x) = 0, \qquad q_i = (d, v_i) .\)

Proof. Let \(K_{j, b}\) be the plane generator normalized by \(K_{j, b} e_b = e_j\). Simultaneous translation preserves the moment and all spin terms. In the orbital term, translating every point by \(e_b\) changes \((K_{j, b} x_i)_c\) to \((K_{j, b} x_i)_c + (K_{j, b} e_b)_c\). Only the \(c = j\) component changes, by one. Therefore

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{A}_{K_{j, b}} (p, Y ; q ; x + e_b) =\mathcal{A}_{K_{j, b}} (p, Y ; q ; x) + \sum_i S (q [i \mapsto (d + 1, p_{d, j} v_i)] ; x) .\)

Both tuples remain separated and lie on the same line, so both angular terms vanish. Subtracting gives the stated line identity. For the empty word it is the empty sum.\(\Box\)

Finite-level bridges used by the selected scale.
The line identity and the collinear rotational identity are only the geometric part of the finite-level argument. The selected scale uses the following additional bridges; their order matters.
The stress-law package.
At the local algebraic stage the commutation relation and the covariance, reality, unit and inclusion rules for the raw responses are derived from the stress-law package rather than postulated separately. We first state that package precisely.

A window for a separated tuple \((x_0, x_1, \ldots, x_n)\) is a smooth, compactly supported, real function \(\chi\) on \(\mathbb{R}^4\) which equals one on a neighborhood of \(x_0\) and whose support contains none of \(x_1, \ldots, x_n\). Every separated tuple has a window: take a bump function equal to one on the ball about \(x_0\) of radius \(\rho / 4\) and supported in the ball of radius \(\rho / 2\), where \(\rho\) is the least distance from \(x_0\) to the other points when \(n \geqslant 1\); for \(n = 0\), choose any \(\rho > 0\) (for example \(\rho = 1\)). If \(\chi\) is a window, then \(\nabla \chi\) vanishes on an open set \(U \ni x_0\), so the compact set \(C_{\chi} = \text{supp} \hspace{0.17em} \nabla \chi\) misses \(U\) and all \(x_i\) with \(i \geqslant 1\). For a label \(v \in \mathcal{F}_d\) at \(x_0\) and a spectator word \(\boldsymbol{q}= (q_1, \ldots, q_n)\) at \(\boldsymbol{x}= (x_1, \ldots, x_n)\) we write \(S (v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\) for the moment with \((d, v)\) in the first slot, and \(S (J_{ab}, v, \boldsymbol{q}; y, x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\) for the moment with an additional insertion \((4, J_{ab})\) at \(y\). For \(y \in C_{\chi}\) the tuple \((y, x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\) is separated.

We write \(\bar{v}\) for the conjugation of a field, \(\textsf{m}_k\) for the action of a signed permutation matrix \(k\) on fields (acting on \(\mathbb{R}^4\) by \(y \mapsto ky\)), \(\iota = \iota_{d, e}\) for the inclusion of grade \(d\) into grade \(e \geqslant d\), and \(\boldsymbol{1}_d\) for the unit at grade \(d\). Conjugation, \(\textsf{m}_k\) and \(\iota\) act on words slot by slot. The hierarchy properties used are: the derivative rule; faithfulness (a field \(v\) with \(S (v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) = 0\) for every word and every separated tuple is zero); reality, \(S (\bar{\boldsymbol{w}} ; \boldsymbol{z}) = \overline{S (\boldsymbol{w}; \boldsymbol{z})}\); signed covariance, \(S (\textsf{m}_k \boldsymbol{w}; k\boldsymbol{z}) = S (\boldsymbol{w}; \boldsymbol{z})\); the unit law, by which a unit insertion may be deleted together with its position; and invariance of moments under replacing a label \(v\) by \(\iota v\).

The stress is a family \(J = (J_{ab})_{a, b = 1}^4\) of grade-four fields with

    (S1)

    \(J_{ab} = J_{ba}\), \(\overline{J_{ab}} = J_{ab}\), and \(\sum_a \mathcal{P}_{4, a} J_{ab} = 0\) for every \(b\);

    (S2)

    \(\textsf{m}_k J_{ab} = \sum_{e, f} k_{ea} k_{fb} J_{ef}\) for every signed permutation matrix \(k\);

    (S3)

    for every separated tuple, every window \(\chi\), every label \(v \in \mathcal{F}_d\), every word \(\boldsymbol{q}\), every axis \(b\) and every \(K \in \mathfrak{so} (4)\),

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} S (p_{d, b} v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) & = - \sum_a \int_{\mathbb{R}^4} \partial_a \chi (y) \hspace{0.17em} S (J_{ab}, v, \boldsymbol{q}; y, x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) \hspace{0.17em} dy, \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.81)} \\ S (Y_d (K) v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) & = - \sum_{a, b} \int_{\mathbb{R}^4} \partial_a \chi (y) \hspace{0.17em} [K (y - x_0)]_b \hspace{0.17em} S (J_{ab}, v, \boldsymbol{q}; y, x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) \hspace{0.17em} dy. \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.82)} \end{array}\)

The integrands vanish off \(C_{\chi}\) and are continuous on it, so both integrals converge absolutely. Identities (S3) define the raw responses \(p_{d, b} : \mathcal{F}_d \to \mathcal{F}_{d + 1}\) and \(Y_d (K) : \mathcal{F}_d \to \mathcal{F}_d\) uniquely by faithfulness; they are identities of the same form as (17.91) and (17.92), where the responses appear after normalization.

Lemma 17.27. (Local response laws) Assume (S1)–(S3) and the hierarchy properties just listed. For every grade \(d\), every \(v \in \mathcal{F}_d\), all axes \(b, i\), every \(K \in \mathfrak{so} (4)\), every signed permutation matrix \(k\) and every \(e \geqslant d\):

    (i)

    (commutation)

    \(\displaystyle \mathcal{P}_{d, b} Y_d (K) v + \sum_c K_{cb} \hspace{0.17em} p_{d, c} v = Y_{d + 1} (K) \mathcal{P}_{d, b} v, \qquad \mathcal{P}_{d + 1, b} \hspace{0.17em} p_{d, i} v = p_{d + 1, i} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_{d, b} v ;\)
    (ii)

    (inclusion) \(p_{e, i} \iota v = \iota p_{d, i} v\) and \(Y_e (K) \iota v = \iota Y_d (K) v\);

    (iii)

    (unit) \(p_{d, i} \boldsymbol{1}_d = 0\) and \(Y_d (K) \boldsymbol{1}_d = 0\);

    (iv)

    (reality) \(\overline{p_{d, i} v} = p_{d, i} \bar{v}\) and \(\overline{Y_d (K) v} = Y_d (K) \bar{v}\);

    (v)

    (signed covariance) \(\textsf{m}_k p_{d, i} v = \sum_j k_{ji} \hspace{0.17em} p_{d, j} \textsf{m}_k v\) and \(\textsf{m}_k Y_d (K) v = Y_d (kKk^{\textsf{T}}) \textsf{m}_k v\).

Proof. Each assertion is an equality of two fields at one grade. By faithfulness it suffices to show that both sides have the same moment against every word at every separated tuple. Fix such a tuple \((x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\), a word \(\boldsymbol{q}\) and a window \(\chi\), with \(U\) and \(C_{\chi}\) as above.

(i) Put \(x_0 (t) = x_0 + te_b\). Choose \(t_0 > 0\) such that \(x_0 (t) \in U\) for \(|t| \leqslant t_0\). For such \(t\) the tuple \((x_0 (t), \boldsymbol{x})\) is separated and the same \(\chi\) is a window for it, and for \(y \in C_{\chi}\) the tuple \((y, x_0 (t), \boldsymbol{x})\) is separated, because \(C_{\chi} \cap U = \emptyset\). Apply (17.82) at \(x_0 (t)\) with this fixed \(\chi\):

\(\displaystyle f (t) := S (Y_d (K) v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0 (t), \boldsymbol{x}) = - \sum_{a, c} \int_{C_{\chi}} \partial_a \chi (y) \hspace{0.17em} [K (y - x_0 (t))]_c \hspace{0.17em} S (J_{ac}, v, \boldsymbol{q}; y, x_0 (t), \boldsymbol{x}) \hspace{0.17em} dy.\)

By the derivative rule, \(f' (0) = S (\mathcal{P}_{d, b} Y_d (K) v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\). On the right, the integration runs over the fixed compact set \(C_{\chi}\), and the integrand is continuously differentiable in \(t\) on \([- t_0, t_0] \times C_{\chi}\), since moments are smooth on configuration space. Hence we may differentiate under the integral. The \(t\)-derivative of the weight \([K (y - x_0 (t))]_c\) is \(- K_{cb}\). By the derivative rule, the \(t\)-derivative of the moment replaces \(v\) by \(\mathcal{P}_{d, b} v\). Thus

\(\displaystyle f' (0) = \sum_c K_{cb} \sum_a \int \partial_a \chi \hspace{0.17em} S (J_{ac}, v, \boldsymbol{q}) \hspace{0.17em} dy - \sum_{a, c} \int \partial_a \chi \hspace{0.17em} [K (y - x_0)]_c \hspace{0.17em} S (J_{ac}, \mathcal{P}_{d, b} v, \boldsymbol{q}) \hspace{0.17em} dy,\)

with all moments at \((y, x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\). By (17.81), with \(b\) replaced by \(c\), the first term is

\(\displaystyle - \sum_c K_{cb} \hspace{0.17em} S (p_{d, c} v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) .\)

By (17.82) at grade \(d + 1\) the second term is \(S (Y_{d + 1} (K)\mathcal{P}_{d, b} v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\). Comparing the two expressions for \(f' (0)\) gives the first identity in (i) against every word. The second identity follows in the same way from (17.81); there the weight does not depend on \(t\), so only the moment is differentiated.

(ii) Replacing \(v\) by \(\iota v\) does not change any moment, in particular not the integrands of (S3). Hence \(S (p_{e, i} \iota v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) = S (p_{d, i} v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) = S (\iota p_{d, i} v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\), and likewise for \(Y\).

(iii) Let \(w (y)\) be the constant vector \(e_i\), respectively the rotational field \(K (y - x_0)\). In both cases \(\partial_a w_b + \partial_b w_a = 0\). By the unit law, \(S (J_{ab}, \boldsymbol{1}_d, \boldsymbol{q}; y, x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) = g_{ab} (y) := S (J_{ab}, \boldsymbol{q}; y, \boldsymbol{x})\). The function \(g_{ab}\) is smooth on \(\mathbb{R}^4 \setminus \{x_1, \ldots, x_n \}\), which is a neighborhood of \(\text{supp} \hspace{0.17em} \chi\); the point \(x_0\) no longer carries an insertion. The products \(\chi w_b g_{ab}\) therefore extend by zero to smooth compactly supported functions, and integration by parts gives

\(\displaystyle - \sum_{a, b} \int \partial_a \chi \hspace{0.17em} w_b \hspace{0.17em} g_{ab} \hspace{0.17em} dy = \sum_{a, b} \int \chi \hspace{0.17em} (\partial_a w_b) \hspace{0.17em} g_{ab} \hspace{0.17em} dy + \sum_b \int \chi \hspace{0.17em} w_b \sum_a \partial_a g_{ab} \hspace{0.17em} dy.\)

The first sum vanishes, because \(\partial_a w_b\) is antisymmetric and \(g_{ab} = g_{ba}\) by (S1). By the derivative rule, \(\sum_a \partial_a g_{ab} (y) = S (\sum_a \mathcal{P}_{4, a} J_{ab}, \boldsymbol{q}; y, \boldsymbol{x}) = 0\) by conservation. By (S3) the left side is the moment of \(p_{d, i} \boldsymbol{1}_d\), respectively \(Y_d (K) \boldsymbol{1}_d\), against \(\boldsymbol{q}\); so both vanish. This uses conservation on the word from which the unit has been deleted; it is not a formal consequence of an identity at nonzero grade.

(iv) Conjugate both sides of (S3). The functions \(\chi\), \(w\) and the matrix \(K\) are real, and \(J_{ab}\) is real by (S1), so reality of the moments gives \(\overline{S (p_{d, i} v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x})} = S (p_{d, i} \bar{v}, \bar{\boldsymbol{q}} ; x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\). The left side is \(S (\overline{p_{d, i} v}, \bar{\boldsymbol{q}} ; x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\). Since \(\boldsymbol{q} \mapsto \bar{\boldsymbol{q}}\) is a bijection of words, faithfulness gives the first identity in (iv); the second is identical.

(v) The matrix \(k\) is orthogonal, and \(\chi' = \chi \circ k^{- 1}\) is a window for \((kx_0, k\boldsymbol{x})\). By the chain rule \(\partial_a \chi (y) = \sum_e k_{ea} (\partial_e \chi') (ky)\). By signed covariance of the moments and (S2),

\(\displaystyle S (J_{ab}, v, \boldsymbol{q}; y, x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) = \sum_{e, f} k_{ea} k_{fb} \hspace{0.17em} S (J_{ef}, \textsf{m}_k v, \textsf{m}_k \boldsymbol{q}; ky, kx_0, k\boldsymbol{x}) .\)

Insert both into (17.81) with \(b = i\) and substitute \(y' = ky\), which preserves Lebesgue measure. Orthogonality, \(\sum_a k_{e' a} k_{ea} = \delta_{e' e}\), leaves

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} S (p_{d, i} v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) & = \sum_f k_{fi} \left( - \sum_e \int \partial_e \chi' (y') \hspace{0.17em} S (J_{ef}, \textsf{m}_k v, \textsf{m}_k \boldsymbol{q}; y', kx_0, k\boldsymbol{x}) \hspace{0.17em} dy' \right)\\ & = \sum_f k_{fi} \hspace{0.17em} S (p_{d, f} \textsf{m}_k v, \textsf{m}_k \boldsymbol{q}; kx_0, k\boldsymbol{x}) . \end{array}\)

The left side equals \(S (\textsf{m}_k p_{d, i} v, \textsf{m}_k \boldsymbol{q}; kx_0, k\boldsymbol{x})\). As \((x_0, \boldsymbol{x}, \boldsymbol{q})\) ranges over all separated tuples and words, so does \((kx_0, k\boldsymbol{x}, \textsf{m}_k \boldsymbol{q})\), because \(k\) and \(\textsf{m}_k\) are invertible. Faithfulness gives the first identity in (v). For the second use in addition \([K (y - x_0)]_b = \sum_{f'} k_{f' b} \hspace{0.17em} [K' (y' - kx_0)]_{f'}\) with \(K' = kKk^{\textsf{T}}\), which holds because \(kK (y - x_0) = K' (ky - kx_0)\). The two orthogonality sums over \(a\) and \(b\) then turn (17.82) at \((x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\) into (17.82) for \(K'\) and \(\chi'\) at \((kx_0, k\boldsymbol{x})\) with labels \(\textsf{m}_k v\), \(\textsf{m}_k \boldsymbol{q}\), and faithfulness gives the claim.\(\Box\)

Readout limits on the comparison curve.
We next prove the two limits that compare the raw responses with the reference derivative and rotation action along the comparison curve. Use the notation of Subsection 17.3: \(E_d\) is the reference field space, \(F_d =\mathcal{F}_d\) the physical one, \(U_{k, d} : E_d \to F_d\) the comparison maps, \(r_k \downarrow 0\) the radii, \(\xi_k > 0\) the stress normalizers, \(J\) the physical and \(J^0\) the reference stress. We write \(S^1\) and \(S^0\) for physical and reference moments.

Proposition 17.28. (Translation and rotation readout limits) Assume the following.

    (a)

    The physical raw responses \(p, Y\) satisfy (S3) with the stress \(J\).

    (b)

    The reference table satisfies (S3) with the stress \(J^0\) and with responses \(\kappa \partial^0\) and \(\kappa C\), where \(\partial^0\) is the reference derivative, \(C\) is the reference infinitesimal rotation action, and \(\kappa \ne 0\) is the reference Ward coefficient (\(\kappa = - 1\) by Subsection 16.2).

    (c)

    The normalization (17.29) holds: \(\tilde{J}_{k, ab} := U_{k, 4}^{- 1} (\xi_k^{- 1} r_k^4 J_{ab}) \to J^0_{ab}\).

    (d)

    Order zero of the compact convergence of Subsection 17.3 holds: for every fixed tuple \(\boldsymbol{v}\) of reference labels and every compact \(Q \subset \text{Conf}\), \(\sup_{\boldsymbol{z} \in Q} |S^1 (U_k \boldsymbol{v}; r_k \boldsymbol{z}) - S^0 (\boldsymbol{v}; \boldsymbol{z}) | \to 0\).

Then for every grade \(d\), every axis \(b\) and every \(K \in \mathfrak{so} (4)\), in operator norm,

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \frac{r_k}{\xi_k} \hspace{0.17em} U_{k, d + 1}^{- 1} \hspace{0.17em} p_{d, b} \hspace{0.17em} U_{k, d} & \longrightarrow \kappa \hspace{0.17em} \partial^0_{d, b}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.83)} \\ \xi_k^{- 1} \hspace{0.17em} U_{k, d}^{- 1} \hspace{0.17em} Y_d (K) \hspace{0.17em} U_{k, d} & \longrightarrow \kappa \hspace{0.17em} C_d (K) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.84)} \end{array}\)

Proof. Step 1: finitely many separating reads. Fix a grade \(e\).
