An observer is a separated tuple \((x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\) together with a reference word \(\boldsymbol{q}\) at \(\boldsymbol{x}\); its reference read is the linear functional \(w \mapsto S^0 (w, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\) on \(E_e\). By faithfulness the reads of all observers have zero common kernel. Since \(E_e\) is finite-dimensional, finitely many observers \(o \in O\) already have zero common kernel: add observers while the common kernel drops in dimension, which happens at most \(\dim E_e\) times. Let \(M : E_e \to \mathbb{C}^O\) be the injective map formed by their reference reads, and \(M_k\) the map formed by the scaled physical reads

\(\displaystyle (M_k w)_o = S^1 (U_{k, e} w, U_k \boldsymbol{q}^o ; r_k x_0^o, r_k \boldsymbol{x}^o) .\)

By (d), applied at the single point of each observer, \(M_k w \to Mw\) for every \(w\), hence \(M_k \to M\) in operator norm. As in Subsection 17.3, compactness of the unit sphere gives \(\gamma > 0\) with \(\|Mw\| \geqslant \gamma \|w\|\), hence \(\|M_k w\| \geqslant (\gamma / 2) \|w\|\) for all large \(k\). Consequently, if \(w_k \in E_e\) and \(M_k w_k \to Mw\), then

\(\displaystyle \|w_k - w\| \leqslant \tfrac{2}{\gamma} \|M_k (w_k - w)\| \leqslant \tfrac{2}{\gamma} (\|M_k w_k - Mw\|+\|(M - M_k) w\|) \to 0.\)

It therefore suffices to prove, for each fixed \(v \in E_d\) and each observer \((x_0, \boldsymbol{x}, \boldsymbol{q})\), that the scaled physical read of

\(\displaystyle w_k = \frac{r_k}{\xi_k} U_{k, d + 1}^{- 1} p_{d, b} U_{k, d} v \quad \text{resp.} \quad w_k' = \xi_k^{- 1} U_{k, d}^{- 1} Y_d (K) U_{k, d} v\)

converges to the reference read of \(\kappa \partial^0_{d, b} v\), respectively \(\kappa C_d (K) v\). Operator-norm convergence then follows by checking a finite basis of \(E_d\).

Step 2: rescaling the flux. Fix the observer and a window \(\chi\) for \((x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\). Then \(\chi_k (z) = \chi (z / r_k)\) is a window for \((r_k x_0, r_k \boldsymbol{x})\). By (a) with \(\chi_k\),

\(\displaystyle S^1 (U_{k, d + 1} w_k, U_k \boldsymbol{q}; r_k x_0, r_k \boldsymbol{x}) = - \frac{r_k}{\xi_k} \sum_a \int \partial_a \chi_k (z) \hspace{0.17em} S^1 (J_{ab}, U_{k, d} v, U_k \boldsymbol{q}; z, r_k x_0, r_k \boldsymbol{x}) \hspace{0.17em} dz.\)

Substitute \(z = r_k y\). Then \(dz = r_k^4 \hspace{0.17em} dy\) and \(\partial_a \chi_k (r_k y) = r_k^{- 1} \partial_a \chi (y)\), so the powers of \(r_k\) combine to \(r_k \cdot r_k^{- 1} \cdot r_k^4 = r_k^4\). By linearity in the stress slot and the definition of \(\tilde{J}_{k, ab}\),

\(\displaystyle S^1 (U_{k, d + 1} w_k, U_k \boldsymbol{q}; r_k x_0, r_k \boldsymbol{x}) = - \sum_a \Lambda^{a, 1}_k (\tilde{J}_{k, ab}),\)

where, for a continuous weight \(\omega\) on \(\mathbb{R}^4\), we put

\(\displaystyle \Lambda^{a, \omega}_k (u) = \int \partial_a \chi (y) \hspace{0.17em} \omega (y) \hspace{0.17em} S^1 (U_{k, 4} u, U_{k, d} v, U_k \boldsymbol{q}; r_k y, r_k x_0, r_k \boldsymbol{x}) \hspace{0.17em} dy, \qquad u \in E_4 .\)

For the rotation, (a) with \(\chi_k\) gives the weight \([K (z - r_k x_0)]_b = r_k [K (y - x_0)]_b\) at \(z = r_k y\). The powers of \(r_k\) now combine to \(r_k^{- 1} \cdot r_k \cdot r_k^4 = r_k^4\), which is again absorbed by \(\xi_k^{- 1}\) into \(\tilde{J}_{k, ab}\):

\(\displaystyle S^1 (U_{k, d} w_k', U_k \boldsymbol{q}; r_k x_0, r_k \boldsymbol{x}) = - \sum_{a, b} \Lambda^{a, \omega_b}_k (\tilde{J}_{k, ab}), \qquad \omega_b (y) = [K (y - x_0)]_b .\)

Step 3: the limit. Let \(\Lambda^{a, \omega}\) be the same functional with \(S^0 (u, v, \boldsymbol{q}; y, x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\) in place of the scaled physical moment. The set \(Q = \{(y, x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) : y \in C_{\chi} \}\) is a compact subset of configuration space. For fixed \(u \in E_4\),

\(\displaystyle | \Lambda^{a, \omega}_k (u) - \Lambda^{a, \omega} (u) | \leqslant \int | \partial_a \chi \hspace{0.17em} \omega | \hspace{0.17em} dy \cdot \sup_Q \left| S^1 (U_k (u, v, \boldsymbol{q}) ; r_k \hspace{0.17em} \cdot \hspace{0.17em}) - S^0 ((u, v, \boldsymbol{q}) ; \hspace{0.17em} \cdot \hspace{0.17em}) | \to 0 \right.\)

by (d). Since \(E_4\) is finite-dimensional, \(\Lambda^{a, \omega}_k \to \Lambda^{a, \omega}\) in norm. Together with (c) this gives \(\Lambda^{a, \omega}_k (\tilde{J}_{k, ab}) \to \Lambda^{a, \omega} (J^0_{ab})\). Summing over the finitely many \(a\) (and \(b\)), the scaled physical reads of \(w_k\) and \(w_k'\) converge to

\(\displaystyle - \sum_a \Lambda^{a, 1} (J^0_{ab}) = S^0 (\kappa \partial^0_{d, b} v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x}), \qquad - \sum_{a, b} \Lambda^{a, \omega_b} (J^0_{ab}) = S^0 (\kappa C_d (K) v, \boldsymbol{q}; x_0, \boldsymbol{x}),\)

by (b). Step 1 with \(e = d + 1\), respectively \(e = d\), completes the proof.\(\Box\)

Only finitely many observers, labels and compact sets enter the proof for a fixed grade, so a single tail of the comparison curve serves all of them. In the translation limit the extra factor \(r_k\) compensates the one power lost by the gradient of the rescaled window. In the rotation limit the arm of the rotational weight supplies that power. These are exactly the normalizations used in Subsection 17.10.

To obtain the all-order line jets, do not differentiate an identity known only on the line. First form the mixed-cap row which sums, over the marked slot, the moment with that label raised once in a fixed transverse direction. The derivative rule and the positional/spin compatibility identity prove on the whole open configuration space that differentiating this row in any point-coordinate direction equals the corresponding row with that slot raised once more. This is a local identity on \(\text{Conf}_n\), not a line-restricted assertion. On the line, the order-zero mixed-cap row vanishes: pad all caps to their finite maximum by inclusion compatibility, then apply the line-sum identity. Now induct on derivative order. The case of order zero is this line-sum statement. For the induction step, the global derivative identity just proved expresses every coordinate directional derivative as a row with one additional raised slot; evaluate that raised row at the line and use the induction hypothesis. Since coordinate directions span the configuration-space tangent space, all multilinear positional jets vanish there. This is the transverse-jet condition used by the finite-scale argument.

First, finite test rows determine the free derivative data. At each grade, faithfulness of the free table says the family of all evaluation rows has zero common kernel. Since the field space at fixed grade is finite dimensional, finitely many such rows already have zero common kernel: add rows while the intersection of kernels drops in dimension; this can happen only finitely often. Under a scaling sequence, the prescribed local jet convergence makes each of these finitely many actual rows converge on every fixed free vector. The limiting row map is injective. Compose the actual row maps with a left inverse of the limit; in finite dimension the resulting operators converge to the identity and are invertible eventually. If the row maps are evaluated on the moving scaled derivative, the given fitted row convergence then forces that derivative to converge to the free derivative. This is the gradient component of the first scaling bridge.

Second, the same finite-row method is applied to a finite family of observers at adjacent grades. This is Proposition 17.28: the stress normalization (17.29) and the flux identities (17.81), (17.82) identify the limiting rows with \(\kappa\) times the reference derivative and reference rotation action. Its observer set is united with the one of the first bridge before one scale threshold is chosen, so these limits use one selected scale sequence. The precise rank-one argument identifying the positional scalar is given after the finite collinear rows have been transported.

Third, transport the finite collinear tests through the same scale. For each selected scene \(s\) and substitution \(q\), the exact scaling identity is

\(\displaystyle \text{test}_k \hspace{-0.17em} (s, \text{aisle}_k (q)) = r_k^{m_s} \hspace{0.17em} \text{lens}_{\text{scaled} (k, s)} (q),\)

where \(m_s\) is the number of positional derivatives in that scene and \(r_k > 0\) is the scale factor. The local jet estimates make these row maps converge on every fixed free substitution, and a finite-dimensional basis then gives operator convergence for the finite family. The derivative and the local response are both transverse: each transported test row annihilates each of them exactly at every scale. Thus the same selected family has the required common zero-row property; there is no injectivity claim for the free collinear tests. This is an exact-kernel argument, not an eventual-injectivity argument.

Theorems 16.15 and 16.16 give one more rank statement needed to identify the normalization. Work at each fixed grade \(d\) above the common detector threshold, where the free derivative \(\partial\) is nonzero. Let \(V_d\) be the finite-dimensional real space of admissible spin-sector response vectors: arrays \(u = (u_b)_b\) of linear maps from the reference grade-\(d\) space to grade \(d + 1\) which annihilate the unit, commute with conjugation, satisfy \(\textsf{m}_k u_i = \sum_j k_{ji} u_j \textsf{m}_k\) for every signed permutation matrix \(k\), and map \(\iota_{a, d} E_a\) into \(\iota_{a + 1, d + 1} E_{a + 1}\) for every \(a \leqslant d\). Let \(R : V_d \to W_d\) be the free finite collinear row map. Its kernel is exactly the span of \(\partial\). Choose a real linear functional \(\ell\) with \(\ell (\partial) \ne 0\); then the augmented map \((R, \ell)\) is injective. The selected row maps converge on a fixed basis, so adjoining this same \(\ell\) leaves them injective for all sufficiently large scales. Choose one such scale \(\nu\). Write \(p_{\nu}\) and \(\partial_{\nu}\) for the transported response vectors, and let \(A_{\nu}\) be the common aisle map from the original finite-dimensional field space into the selected coordinates. Both transported vectors lie in \(V_d\). The physical arrays \((p_{d, b})_b\) and \((\partial_{d, b})_b\) have the four defining properties: for \(p\) these are parts (ii)–(v) of Lemma 17.27, for the derivative they are the corresponding properties of the derivative plan. The aligned comparison maps of the selected scale preserve units, conjugations, signed actions and inclusions through grade \(d + 1\), so they carry such arrays into \(V_d\). The common zero-row property stated above gives \(R_{\nu} A_{\nu} p = R_{\nu} A_{\nu} \partial = 0\). Since \(A_{\nu}\) is injective and \(\partial \ne 0\), \(\partial_{\nu} \ne 0\). If \(\ell (\partial_{\nu}) = 0\), then augmented injectivity of \((R_{\nu}, \ell)\) would force \(\partial_{\nu} = 0\); thus this denominator is nonzero. Set \(c_{d, \nu} = \ell (p_{\nu}) / \ell (\partial_{\nu})\). The vector \(p_{\nu} - c_{d, \nu} \partial_{\nu}\) is killed by both \(R_{\nu}\) and \(\ell\), hence is zero. Cancel the injective common aisle map to obtain \(p = c_{d, \nu} \partial\) in the original field space. Moreover \(c_{d, \nu} \ne 0\). Indeed, if \(c_{d, \nu} = 0\), then \(p_{d, b} = 0\) for every \(b\), so the left side of (17.83) vanishes for every \(k\). Its limit \(\kappa \partial^0_{d, b}\) would then be zero for every \(b\), which is impossible: \(\kappa \ne 0\), and the reference derivative is nonzero at grades above the detector threshold. Grade inclusion intertwines \(p\) and \(\partial\). Comparing adjacent grades above the detector threshold shows that this scalar is constant there; comparing any lower grade with the fixed threshold grade and using injectivity of inclusion gives the same identity below the threshold. For any finite collection of caps and grades, take the maximum of the finitely many row-convergence thresholds. Thus the same scalar \(c\) works for every fixed finite comparison in the selected family. The independent stress-normalization result, together with the A05 observer limits, identifies the spin response with the corresponding same-scale multiple; define \(\tilde{Y} = c^{- 1} Y\). This ratio is obtained before the downstream rotation-action argument.

Finally, erasure transfers from the free table to the selected table by the same argument. At fixed grade, regard the internal Ward maps as a finite-dimensional real vector space. Free erasure says the totality of all separated row sums separates this space, so finitely many rows suffice. Their actual counterparts converge on every fixed map. Eventual injectivity follows by applying a fixed left inverse of the free row map. For a selected-table Ward map, pull it back by the aligned grade isomorphism. It lies in the free covariance subspace; if all selected row sums vanish, the finite actual rows annihilate the pullback, hence it is zero. Conjugating back proves erasure at all sufficiently large grades. The finite-dimensional injectivity argument uses pointwise convergence on a fixed basis and does not assume convergence on a moving map.

Lemma 17.29. (Differentiated Ward identity and brackets) Suppose Still holds at every separated configuration; suppose also the spin rows satisfy reality, preserve the unit, commute with grade inclusion, and obey signed-permutation covariance: for every grade \(d\), signed permutation matrix \(k\), \(u \in \mathfrak{so} (4)\) and \(v \in \mathcal{F}_d\),

\(\displaystyle \textsf{m}_k \mathcal{Y}_d (u) v =\mathcal{Y}_d (kuk^{\textsf{T}}) \textsf{m}_k v.\)

If the selected table has the erasure property just described, then for every grade \(d\) and \(u, v \in \mathfrak{so} (4)\),

\(\displaystyle [\mathcal{Y}_d (u), \mathcal{Y}_d (v)] =\mathcal{Y}_d ([u, v]) .\)

Proof. For a fixed word \(q\) define the simultaneous orbital vector field

\(\displaystyle X_u (x)_i = ux_i .\)

The Ward identity and the derivative rule give

\(\displaystyle X_u S (q ; x) = - \sum_i S (q [i \mapsto \mathcal{Y}(u) q_i] ; x) . \) (17.85)

The minus sign is fixed by the convention in the angular expression. Because Still holds throughout the open configuration space, (1) can be differentiated there and used again on each substituted word. The vector field bracket is \([X_u, X_v] = - X_{[u, v]}\); this follows directly by differentiating the linear maps \(x \mapsto ux\) and \(x \mapsto vx\). Consequently the commutator of the two orbital derivatives on a moment is \(- X_{[u, v]} S (q ; x)\).

On the other hand, apply (17.85) twice. Terms where the two spin substitutions hit distinct slots cancel in pairs: the term indexed by \((i, j)\) in one order is the same moment as the term indexed by \((j, i)\) in the reverse order. The terms with both substitutions at slot \(i\) leave the positive commutator

\(\displaystyle \sum_i S (q [i \mapsto ([\mathcal{Y}_d (u), \mathcal{Y}_d (v)] q_i)] ; x) .\)

The orbital vector-field bracket is \(- X_{[u, v]}\), and by (17.85) its action on the moment is \(+ \sum_i S (q [i \mapsto \mathcal{Y}_d ([u, v]) q_i] ; x)\). Equating the two computations gives zero row sum for the defect

\(\displaystyle D_d (u, v) = [\mathcal{Y}_d (u), \mathcal{Y}_d (v)] -\mathcal{Y}_d ([u, v])\)

has zero row sum on every separated word.

The covariance assumptions imply that \(D_d\) is an internal Ward map: it transforms under signed rotations, respects conjugation, annihilates the unit, is alternating, and is compatible with grade inclusion. Thus it belongs to the finite-level internal kernel tested by erasure. Erasure annihilates it for every grade above a fixed threshold. For any smaller grade, raise to a grade above that threshold; inclusion compatibility identifies the raised defect with the image of the original one. The grade inclusion is injective, so the original defect was zero as well. This proves the bracket identity at every grade.\(\Box\)

Corollary 17.30. (Selected finite-level Ward package) Assume the selected finite-level data supplied by Subsection 17.3 and the finite-row comparisons above. In particular, for one \(c \ne 0\) the raw responses obey \(p = cV\) and \(Y = c \tilde{Y}\), where \(V\) is the genuine positional derivative and \(\tilde{Y}\) is the normalized spin action. Assume also the two free inputs: a finite collinear classifier for the quiet derivative rows and erasure of any alternating two-generator internal map whose separated Ward rows vanish, with the hypotheses used in the finite-row comparisons above. Then the actual collinear rotational identity and line identity hold, the Ward identity holds at every separated configuration, and the spin rows satisfy the \(\mathfrak{so} (4)\) bracket relations.

Proof. The compact stress packet gives the raw collinear identity \(\mathcal{A}_K (p, Y) = 0\). Dividing the commutation relation of Lemma 17.27(i) by \(c\) and using \(p = cV\) gives (17.74) for \((V, \tilde{Y})\). Parts (ii)–(v) of that lemma, divided by \(c\), give the inclusion, unit, reality and signed covariance rules for \(\tilde{Y}\) used in Lemma 17.29. The nonvanishing of \(c\) is proved above from (17.83). By the common scalar normalization, \(\mathcal{A}_K (p, Y) = c\mathcal{A}_K (V, \tilde{Y})\), so the genuine derivative and normalized spin action satisfy the collinear hypothesis of Theorem 17.25. That theorem extends the rotational identity to all of \(\text{Conf}_n\). The finite-row bridges produce the selected scale data and transfer erasure. Apply Lemma 17.29 to that same selected family. Each use is for a fixed grade/configuration; the finite maxima are taken before the scale threshold, so the argument does not interchange a pointwise limit with an uncountable family of tests.\(\Box\)

The cove, line, mass, point and exterior bounds used by the stress packet are the exact inputs of Subsection 17.6; their exponent composition and the \(- 101 + 100 = - 1\) normalization are proved above. The common selected curve, finite observer/jet convergence and scalar normalization are supplied by Subsection 17.3. For the Maxwell reference table, Theorems 16.15 and 16.16 supply the quiet-charge classifier and its finite collinear tests at grades above their common threshold. Theorem 16.7 supplies the internal-map erasure input at the stated covariance, reality, unit and filtration hypotheses. Each fixed grade and finite observer family can use its own comparison scale; the scalar is common across grades by inclusion and nonvanishing, as proved above. No scale threshold uniform over all grades is needed. These free inputs are distinct from the packet-estimates and from the geometric line-to-configuration argument.

17.10Calibrating the physical rotation action

We construct the rotation action on the physical field spaces from the actual angular Ward maps. Equal dimensions of the physical and reference spaces would not supply this action. The essential step is to correct an approximate intertwiner while preserving the real structure and signed coordinate symmetries.

Write \(E_d\) and \(E_d^0\) for the finite-dimensional complex physical and reference field spaces at grade cap \(d\). Retain the actual derivative maps \(\partial\), the conserved stress \(J\), its translation charges \(p\), and its angular maps \(Y\). The physical Ward propagation argument supplies one real \(c \ne 0\) such that \(p = c \partial\) at every cap. Put \(D = c^{- 1} Y\). Its output also supplies the global angular Ward identity for \(D\), the Lie-bracket identity

\(\displaystyle [D_d (A), D_d (B)] = D_d ([A, B]),\)

and compatibility with conjugation, signed motions and inclusions. These are the inputs from the physical shell argument, distinct from the reference Maxwell Ward calculation.

On the reference spaces keep the fixed continuous rotation representation \(R_d^0\) and its infinitesimal maps \(C_d\). Its reference stress has Ward coefficient \(\kappa \ne 0\), with the fixed value \(- 1\) in Subsection 16.2. The reference action and its Lorentz companion are the ones attached to that same free hierarchy. We do not replace either representation when calibrating the physical one.

17.10.1The scalar normalization on a common comparison sequence

Fix \(d\). The finite-request comparison gives, on one sequence, complex-linear isomorphisms \(U_{n, e} : E_e^0 \to E_e\), radii \(r_n > 0\) and stress normalizers \(\xi_n > 0\). Choose it sufficiently rich to include the derivative test at cap \(4\), the translation-charge test there, the angular tests at cap \(d\), and the real/signed compatibility at \(d\). One can do this by taking the maximum of the finitely many requested caps, arities, derivative orders and compact radii before invoking the comparison. This is the common finite-request construction of Subsection 17.3; the shell-charge limits are (17.83) and (17.84) of Proposition 17.28.

Transport both source and target of the derivative maps through these same \(U_{n, e}\). Denote the transported cap-\(4\) derivative array, multiplied by \(r_n\), by \(A_n\). The two limits just specified say

\(\displaystyle A_n \longrightarrow A^0 \ne 0, \qquad (c / \xi_n) A_n \longrightarrow \kappa A^0 .\)

The nonzero derivative array is the fixed reference test. Choose a real linear functional \(\lambda\) on the finite-dimensional array space with \(\lambda (A^0) \ne 0\). Then \(\lambda (A_n) \ne 0\) eventually, and division gives

\(\displaystyle \frac{c}{\xi_n} = \frac{\lambda ((c / \xi_n) A_n)}{\lambda (A_n)} \longrightarrow \kappa .\) (17.86)

In particular no unproved lower bound on \(\xi_n\) is required. The angular comparison (17.84) is

\(\displaystyle \xi_n^{- 1} U_{n, d}^{- 1} Y_d (A) U_{n, d} \longrightarrow \kappa C_d (A) .\)

Multiplying by \((c / \xi_n)^{- 1}\) and using (17.86) proves

\(\displaystyle B_n (A) := U_{n, d}^{- 1} D_d (A) U_{n, d} \longrightarrow C_d (A) .\) (17.87)

Only finitely many generating planes are needed at this stage. The maps \(U_{n, d}\) commute with conjugation and the signed actions. Their convergence is neither required nor asserted.

17.10.2An exact intertwiner from nearby infinitesimal matrices

We give the finite-dimensional argument used with (17.87). Let \(E = E_d^0\) and write \(K_{ab} = e_a e_b^{\textsf{T}} - e_b e_a^{\textsf{T}}\) for the ordered plane generators, including \(K_{aa} = 0\). Index them by \(i = (a, b)\). For every pair choose an axis \(h\) distinct from \(a, b\). Direct multiplication gives

\(\displaystyle [K_{ah}, K_{hb}] = K_{ab} .\)

Thus the corresponding matrices \(B_{n, i}\) and \(C_i\) obey a fixed family of perfect-commutator identities \(B_{n, i} = [B_{n, l (i)}, B_{n, r (i)}]\), and similarly for \(C_i\). On \(\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}} (E)\) put

\(\displaystyle T_{n, i} (F) = B_{n, i} F - FC_i, \qquad T_i (F) = C_i F - FC_i, \qquad L_n = \sum_i T_{n, i}^2, \quad L = \sum_i T_i^2 .\)

Left and right multiplication commute, so the \(T_{n, i}\) satisfy the same perfect identities. In fact the full Lie-bracket relations give

\(\displaystyle [L_n, T_{n, i}] = 0.\) (17.88)

For completeness, the plane bracket formula is

\(\displaystyle [K_{ab}, K_{ij}] = \delta_{bi} K_{aj} - \delta_{bj} K_{ai} + \delta_{ai} K_{jb} - \delta_{aj} K_{ib} .\)

Insert this in \([T_{ab}^2, T_{ij}] = T_{ab} [T_{ab}, T_{ij}] + [T_{ab}, T_{ij}] T_{ab}\) and sum over the ordered pairs \((a, b)\). The four resulting sums cancel in pairs after exchanging their repeated indices and using \(T_{ba} = - T_{ab}\). This proves (17.88) for the relative representation too.

First construct a real polynomial \(q\) such that \(P = q (L)\) is a projection, \(P (I) = I\), and \(T_i P = 0\) for every \(i\). Average any positive-definite real inner product on \(\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}} (E)\) under \(F \mapsto R_d^0 (K) FR_d^0 (K)^{- 1}\), using probability Haar measure on the compact group \(O (4)\). The average is positive definite: for a nonzero vector the integrand is positive at the identity and, by continuity, on a nonempty open set of positive Haar measure. It is invariant by Haar translation. Differentiating that invariance makes each \(T_i\) skew for the averaged form \(\langle \cdot, \cdot \rangle_0\). Consequently

\(\displaystyle \langle LF, F \rangle_0 = - \sum_i \langle T_i F, T_i F \rangle_0, \qquad \ker L = \bigcap_i \ker T_i .\)

Moreover \(L^2 F = 0\) implies \(LF = 0\): the displayed kernel identity gives \(T_i LF = 0\), and skewness then gives \(\langle LF, LF \rangle_0 = \sum_i \langle T_i^2 F, LF \rangle_0 = 0\).

Cayley–Hamilton provides a nonzero polynomial annihilating \(L\). Starting with \(Lp (L) = 0\), divide out factors of the variable as long as \(p (0) = 0\). If \(p (X) = Xh (X)\), then \(L^2 h (L) = 0\) implies \(Lh (L) = 0\) by the preceding implication, applied to every vector. Eventually a polynomial with nonzero constant term remains. Normalize it to obtain \(q (0) = 1\) and \(Lq (L) = 0\). Since \(q (L)\) is the identity on \(\ker L\), it is a projection onto that kernel. The identity matrix belongs to this kernel, so \(P (I) = I\). This proof also covers \(E = \{0\}\).

Let \(\mathcal{F}\) be the closed real vector space of endomorphisms of \(E\) commuting with the reference signed actions and conjugation. The identity belongs to \(\mathcal{F}\). Every \(L_n\) preserves \(\mathcal{F}\). Indeed, a signed coordinate transformation permutes the ordered planes and multiplies each by a sign. Both \(B_{n, i}\) and \(C_i\) acquire the same sign, so it cancels in \(T_{n, i}^2\) and the sum is unchanged. Conjugation commutes with both matrix families; since the polynomial coefficients are real, it commutes with all subsequent polynomial operations too. It follows that \(P_n = q (L_n)\) preserves \(\mathcal{F}\), commutes with every \(T_{n, i}\), and converges to \(P\).

The \(P_n\) need not be projections. Correct them by the polynomial iteration

\(\displaystyle Z_{j + 1} = 3 Z_j^2 - 2 Z_j^3, \qquad Z_0 = P_n .\)

For \(e (Z) = Z^2 - Z\) the exact identities are

\(\displaystyle Z_{j + 1} - Z_j = (I - 2 Z_j) e (Z_j), \qquad e (Z_{j + 1}) = (4 e (Z_j) - 3 I) e (Z_j)^2 .\)

Here is a uniform convergence estimate. Choose \(A > \|P\|\) and \(C > \|I\| + 2 A\). For sufficiently large \(n\), put \(\varepsilon_n = \|e (P_n)\|\); then

\(\displaystyle \|P_n \| + 2 C \varepsilon_n \leqslant A, \qquad (4 \varepsilon_n + 3\|I\|) \varepsilon_n \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2} .\)

Induction using the two identities gives \(\|e (Z_j)\| \leqslant 2^{- j} \varepsilon_n\) and \(\|Z_{j + 1} - Z_j \| \leqslant C 2^{- j} \varepsilon_n\). Their sum keeps \(\|Z_j \| \leqslant A\), closing the induction. Thus \(Z_j \to Q_n\), with \(Q_n^2 = Q_n\) and \(\|Q_n - P_n \| \leqslant 2 C \varepsilon_n\). In particular \(Q_n \to P\). Closedness preserves the constraints: \(Q_n\) preserves \(\mathcal{F}\) and commutes with all \(T_{n, i}\).

The restricted operators \(T_{n, i} Q_n\) satisfy the same perfect identities, since \(Q_n\) is idempotent and commutes with them. They converge to \(T_i P = 0\). Let \(m_n = \max_i \|T_{n, i} Q_n \|\). The perfect identities give \(m_n \leqslant 2 m_n^2\). Eventually \(m_n < 1 / 2\), hence \(m_n = 0\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle T_{n, i} Q_n = 0 \quad \text{for every } i.\)

Apply \(Q_n\) to the identity matrix and set \(F_n = Q_n (I)\). Then \(F_n \in \mathcal{F}\), \(F_n \to I\), and \(B_{n, i} F_n = F_n C_i\). For one sufficiently late \(n\), \(F_n\) is invertible by the Neumann series. Thus

\(\displaystyle Q_d = U_{n, d} F_n : E_d^0 \longrightarrow E_d\) (17.89)

is an isomorphism preserving reality and signed motions, and \(D_d (A) Q_d = Q_d C_d (A)\) for every skew matrix \(A\). To pass from planes to all \(A\), use the exact spanning identity \(A = \frac{1}{2} \sum_{a, b} A_{ab} K_{ab}\). No complete reducibility or unitarity of the deformed matrices was assumed.

17.10.3The physical action and its compatibility

Choose one map (17.89) in each cap and define

\(\displaystyle R_d (K) = Q_d R_d^0 (K) Q_d^{- 1} .\)

It is a continuous representation of all of \(O (4)\), preserves the real structure, agrees with the prescribed signed action, and has derivative \(D_d (A)\) along \(\exp (tA)\) at zero. The group law moves this derivative to every \(t\). The choices of \(Q_d\) need not commute with inclusions.

For arbitrary distinct points and field labels, differentiate

\(\displaystyle t \longmapsto S (R_{d_1} (e^{tA}) v_1, \ldots, R_{d_n} (e^{tA}) v_n ; e^{tA} x_1, \ldots, e^{tA} x_n) .\)

Joint differentiability follows by expanding the labels in finite bases: the expression is a finite sum of smooth position coefficients times multilinear coordinate products. The chain rule gives the global angular Ward expression for \(D\), which is zero. Thus the function is constant. Simultaneous covariance holds for every one-parameter rotation and also for signed transformations. The set of transformations for which it holds is a subgroup, by composition and inversion. Plane rotations generate \(SO (4)\), and a signed reflection supplies its other component, as in the group-generation argument of Subsection 6.5. Hence covariance holds for all \(O (4)\).

The unit, inclusions and derivatives are now compatible with this actual action. For the unit, test \(R_d (K) 1\) against any separated spectator word, move all labels and positions back by \(K^{- 1}\), and delete the unit using the original unit law. Move the remaining word forward again. The result is the test of \(1\), so faithfulness of the physical quotient gives \(R_d (K) 1 = 1\). For an inclusion \(\iota_{de}\), the identical argument uses the original inclusion identity at the moved configuration and yields

\(\displaystyle R_e (K) \iota_{de} = \iota_{de} R_d (K) .\)

Finally differentiate the covariance identity in the coordinate of the head insertion, keeping arbitrary spectators. The ordinary linear chain rule and the original derivative identity give

\(\displaystyle R_{d + 1} (K) \partial_a v = \sum_i K_{ia} \partial_i (R_d (K) v) .\) (17.90)

Again all-spectator faithfulness converts equality of correlations into an identity of field classes. This proves the full physical Euclidean action laws rather than merely laws at each isolated cap.

17.10.4Normalizing and averaging the stress

First replace \(J\) by \(J_0 = c^{- 1} J\). Linearity of the collar flux makes its translation charge exactly \(\partial\) and its angular charge exactly \(D\). The tensor remains real, symmetric and divergence-free. To make it transform tensorially under the action just constructed, define

\(\displaystyle (\mathcal{R}_K J_0)_{ab} = \sum_{i, j} K_{ai} K_{bj} R_4 (K) (J_{0, ij}), \qquad \hat{J} = \int_{O (4)} \mathcal{R}_K J_0 \hspace{0.17em} dK.\)

This integral is in the finite-dimensional space \(E_4^{16}\), with probability Haar measure. Its integrand is continuous. The group law gives \(\mathcal{R}_K \mathcal{R}_L =\mathcal{R}_{KL}\), so Haar invariance implies \(\mathcal{R}_K \hat{J} = \hat{J}\) and hence

\(\displaystyle R_4 (K) \hat{J}_{ij} = \sum_{a, b} K_{ai} K_{bj} \hat{J}_{ab} .\)

The average is real and symmetric. Equation (17.90) and orthogonality show

\(\displaystyle \sum_a \partial_a (\mathcal{R}_K J_0)_{ab} = \sum_j K_{bj} R_5 (K) \left( \sum_i \partial_i J_{0, ij} \right) = 0.\)

The derivative maps are continuous linear maps of finite-dimensional spaces and therefore commute with the integral. Thus \(\hat{J}\) is conserved.

We verify that averaging retains both exact Ward normalizations. For vectors \(u, v\) write \(J [u, v] = \sum_{a, b} u_a v_b J_{ab}\). Then

\(\displaystyle (\mathcal{R}_K J) [u, v] = R_4 (K) J [K^{- 1} u, K^{- 1} v] .\)

In a collar flux with cutoff \(\chi\) and vector field \(W\), change variables \(y = Kz\), moving the external points and labels by \(K^{- 1}\). Orthogonal maps preserve volume, and \(\nabla (\chi \circ K) = K^{- 1} (\nabla \chi) \circ K\). The transformed vector field is \(K^{- 1} W (Kz)\). Thus the flux of \(\mathcal{R}_K J_0\) equals the flux of \(J_0\) in this transformed collar. The compact support of \(\nabla \chi\) avoids all insertion points, so the integrands are smooth there and the change of variables is legitimate.

For a translation \(W = e_a\), the transformed charge is the linear combination with coefficients \((K^{- 1} e_a)_i\). Its Ward identity, (17.90) and covariance of the external correlation return exactly the original derivative charge \(\partial_a\). For \(W (y) = A (y - x_0)\) the transformed field is \((K^{- 1} AK) (z - K^{- 1} x_0)\). Differentiating the representation identity \(R_d (K) R_d (e^{tA}) R_d (K)^{- 1} = R_d (e^{tKAK^{- 1}})\) gives

\(\displaystyle R_d (K) D_d (A) = D_d (KAK^{- 1}) R_d (K) .\)

Its use in the transformed Ward identity returns exactly \(D_d (A)\). This also holds for orientation-reversing \(K\); volume measure contributes \(| \det K| = 1\), not a determinant sign. Each fixed collar flux is a continuous linear functional of the stress labels. It therefore passes through the finite-dimensional Haar integral, and the constant target Ward expression integrates to itself. Both charges of \(\hat{J}\) are consequently normalized as required.

17.10.5Keeping the same maps at the Lorentz step

The maps \(Q_d\) in (17.89), with the reference Euclidean and Lorentz actions already fixed, give precisely the calibrated actions of Input 6.9. Their reality and signed compatibility were preserved by the polynomial correction; their Euclidean actions now commute with inclusions. Define the physical Lorentz action at each cap by \(Q_d T_d^0 Q_d^{- 1}\) with these very maps. Proposition 6.10 proves its cross-cap compatibility from the boost differential equation, and Subsection 6.5 supplies the boundary covariance argument. There is no new choice of a Lorentz intertwiner. The hierarchy, its derivative maps and its field quotients have remained the same through this construction.

17.11The same stress laws in every character phase

The character construction evaluates an operator for a whole word. This allows us to transfer a Ward identity with spectators, even though the identity includes an integral and is not a product of individual character values. We prove the required operator identity before evaluating it.

Retain the unselected hierarchy \(S\), its Euclidean Hilbert space \(\mathcal{H}_E\), ground subspace \(\mathcal{H}_0\), orthogonal projection \(P_0\), and the commutative compression algebra \(\mathcal{A}\) of Subsection 5.2. Write \(A_D (w)\) for its bounded insertion operator in a time slab of width \(D > 0\). The norm smoothness and derivative identities are those of Subsection 5.4; the whole-word character hierarchies are those of Subsection 5.6. Retain the same physical field spaces, derivative maps \(\partial_b\), rotation generators \(\mathcal{R} (M)\) and normalized stress tensor \(J_{ab}\) constructed in Subsection 17.10. In this section its unselected translation and rotation Ward identities are hypotheses already supplied by that construction. No new normalization of \(J\) is made in a phase.

Fix a distinguished insertion \(B\) at \(x_0\), spectator fields \(B_1, \ldots, B_n\) at distinct points \(x_1, \ldots, x_n\), and a smooth real cutoff \(\chi\) of compact support. Require that \(\chi = 1\) on a neighborhood of \(x_0\) and that \(x_j \not\in \text{supp} \hspace{0.17em} \chi\) for \(j > 0\). All \(n + 1\) points are distinct. Put

\(\displaystyle S_{\xi} (B ; x_0 \mid \boldsymbol{B}, \boldsymbol{x}) = S_{\xi} (B, B_1, \ldots, B_n ; x_0, x_1, \ldots, x_n) .\)

Here \(\xi \in \Xi\) is an arbitrary character. The corresponding notation with an extra field before \(B\) includes its extra position before \(x_0\).

Proposition 17.31. (Inheritance of the shell identities) For every character \(\xi\), every coordinate \(b\), and every real skew-symmetric matrix \(M\), one has

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} S_{\xi} (\partial_b B ; x_0 \mid \boldsymbol{B}, \boldsymbol{x}) & = - \sum_a \int_{\mathbb{R}^4} \partial_a \chi (y) S_{\xi} (J_{ab}, B ; y, x_0 \mid \boldsymbol{B}, \boldsymbol{x}) \hspace{0.17em} dy, \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.91)} \\ S_{\xi} (\mathcal{R}(M) B ; x_0 \mid \boldsymbol{B}, \boldsymbol{x}) & = - \sum_{a, b} \int_{\mathbb{R}^4} \partial_a \chi (y) [M (y - x_0)]_b S_{\xi} (J_{ab}, B ; y, x_0 \mid \boldsymbol{B}, \boldsymbol{x}) \hspace{0.17em} dy. \hspace{3cm} \text{(17.92)} \end{array}\)

Both integrals are absolutely convergent. The same cutoff and the same field maps work for every character, including the selected one.

17.11.1A continuous evaluator on the uncompressed operators

A character is continuous and complex linear on \(\mathcal{A}\). By the complex Hahn–Banach theorem, extend it to a bounded complex-linear functional \(\tilde{\xi}\) on \(\mathcal{B} (\mathcal{H}_0)\), and set

\(\displaystyle \Phi_{\xi} (T) = \tilde{\xi} (P_0 T|_{\mathcal{H}_0}), \qquad T \in \mathcal{B} (\mathcal{H}_E) .\)

Compression is bounded linear, so \(\Phi_{\xi}\) is bounded linear. We use no positivity or multiplicativity of its extension outside \(\mathcal{A}\). For a separated word \(w\) placed strictly inside a slab by a translation \(s\), the whole-word definition gives

\(\displaystyle S_{\xi} (w) = \Phi_{\xi} (A_D (w + s)) .\) (17.93)

The evaluator is fixed while positions, labels, and the slab placement vary. In particular it may be passed through a norm-convergent integral.

Choose \(s\) and \(D\) so that both the finite configuration and the whole compact support of \(\chi\), after translation by \(s\), lie strictly between time \(0\) and time \(D\). To see that this is possible, bound their time coordinates between \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\), translate by \((1 - \alpha) e_0\), and take \(D > \beta - \alpha + 1\). This leaves a positive margin at each slab boundary. For a field tuple \(v\) and a configuration \(z\) in this fixed slab, write

\(\displaystyle K_v (z) = A_D (v ; z + s) .\)

Representatives in the original label spaces can be fixed once for the finitely many fields involved. The slab identities and equality of the phase null spaces ensure that the resulting correlation values are the ones on the physical field spaces. Norm regularity gives continuity of \(K_v\) on separated configurations in this slab.

17.11.2Integrating the shell before taking a character

Let \(u : \mathbb{R}^4 \to \mathbb{C}\) be continuous; the cases needed are \(u = 1\) and \(u (y) = [M (y - x_0)]_b\). Put

\(\displaystyle w_a (y) = \partial_a \chi (y) u (y) .\)

Its support is compact and contained in \(\text{supp} \hspace{0.17em} \chi\). Moreover it misses a neighborhood of every \(x_j\): at \(x_0\) the cutoff is locally constant, and at the spectator points it is locally zero. Consequently the operator-valued function

\(\displaystyle F_{ab, u} (y) = w_a (y) K_{(J_{ab}, B, \boldsymbol{B})} (y, x_0, \boldsymbol{x})\)

is continuous and compactly supported when defined to be zero in the neighborhoods where \(w_a\) vanishes. On its support the inserted point is separated from all fixed points and remains strictly inside the fixed slab, so norm continuity follows from that of \(K\). At a point outside the support of \(w_a\), the entire product is locally zero, even if the unweighted correlation there would have a collision. This proves continuity on all of \(\mathbb{R}^4\).

A continuous compactly supported Banach-valued function has bounded norm, separable range on its compact support, and integrable norm. Thus the Bochner integral

\(\displaystyle U_{ab} (u) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^4} F_{ab, u} (y) \hspace{0.17em} dy \quad \text{in } \mathcal{B} (\mathcal{H}_E)\)

exists. Every bounded linear functional \(\Lambda\) commutes with this integral. One may verify this first for simple functions and then use norm-integrable approximation and the bound \(| \Lambda (T) | \leqslant \| \Lambda \| \hspace{0.17em} \|T\|\). Applied to (17.93), it gives

\(\displaystyle \Phi_{\xi} (U_{ab} (u)) = \int w_a (y) S_{\xi} (J_{ab}, B ; y, x_0 \mid \boldsymbol{B}, \boldsymbol{x}) \hspace{0.17em} dy.\) (17.94)

It also proves absolute integrability of the scalar integrand. Values at collisions have no bearing on this equality, since the weight is zero in a neighborhood of each possible collision.

17.11.3Testing the operator identity against exterior words

Let \(l, r\) be arbitrary positive-time word vectors in \(\mathcal{H}_E\). The slab matrix-element identity expresses \(\langle [l], K_v (z) [r] \rangle\) as an unselected correlation containing \(v\) at \(z\), together with a reflected copy of \(l\) below the slab and a translated copy of \(r\) above it. Translate all points back by \(- s\). Permutation symmetry puts the inserted fields first; translation invariance then gives precisely this correlation in the original coordinates. The two extra word configurations are internally separated and lie on opposite sides of the slab. They therefore introduce neither collisions with one another nor collisions with its interior insertions.

Crucially, these extra spectators lie outside the entire support of \(\chi\), since that support was placed strictly inside the slab. Thus \(\chi\) is still an admissible cutoff for the unselected Ward identity with these additional spectators. Commuting the continuous matrix-element functional with each Bochner integral, that Ward identity proves

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \left\langle [l], - \sum_a U_{ab} (1) [r] \right\rangle & = \langle [l], K_{(\partial_b B, \boldsymbol{B})} (x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) [r] \rangle,\\ \left. \left\langle [l], - \sum_{a, b} U_{ab} ([M (\hspace{0.17em} \cdot - x_0) \right]_b) [r] \right\rangle & = \langle [l], K_{(\mathcal{R}(M) B, \boldsymbol{B})} (x_0, \boldsymbol{x}) [r] \rangle . \end{array}\)

Positive-time word vectors span a dense subspace. If a bounded operator has zero matrix elements on this span, fix its right argument there and use density on the left to see that its value is zero. Continuity then extends the vanishing to every right argument. Hence the two matrix-element equalities are equalities of bounded operators.

Apply \(\Phi_{\xi}\) to these operator equalities. Its linearity, the whole-word evaluation formula, and (17.94) give (17.91) and (17.92). This proves the proposition. Notice that no limiting argument over characters and no almost-everywhere exceptional set occurred.

17.11.4The mixed and character continuations

The continuation construction applies to the mixed table and to every character, with the same field labels and calibrated rotation action. For the mixed table use its original Hilbert space, bounded slabs and slot identities. For each character use the Hilbert space and slab operators of Corollary 5.22 and Proposition 5.24. Their norm bounds, derivative identities and field inclusions have already been verified; equality of null spaces is supplied by Subsection 5.8. Reflection transport supplies the same orthogonal field covariance. The common label action of Subsection 6.4 consequently applies to the mixed table and to every component.

Apply Sections 6.1–6.2 to each of these sets of Hilbert-space data. The continued functions are built from each table's own slab matrix elements, so their imaginary-time restrictions recover that table, and their tempered boundary distributions are its own boundary values. The Fourier-support, Lorentz-covariance, full-tube and locality arguments use these same functions in each application. None of those arguments uses one-dimensionality of the time-invariant subspace. The word-domain construction and heat-vector isometry likewise apply to each table.

Theorem 5.16 first gives vacuum uniqueness almost everywhere through the isometric embedding and translation intertwining of Subsection 6.10. The uniform low-energy argument in Theorem 5.19 then proves vacuum uniqueness for every reconstructed character. It uses locality and the forward spectrum already obtained, not a prior uniqueness assumption. No such uniqueness is asserted for the mixed theory. All these continuations therefore use the original field quotient; the selected character and its stress normalization are unchanged.
